Statistical Games Playful approach to statistics
József Konczer

Abstract




1
Brief introduction

It might appeal to some.

Abraham Wald
[
169
]

The first and main part of this work has a mathematical character.
It explores and analyses a few simple two-player non-cooperative games (for the explicit definition, see Def.
2.1
, or Def.
3.2
), in which many concepts of probability theory and statistics naturally emerge.
In these games – termed Fisher and Bayesian games – an adversarial player can choose from a set of possible scenarios, while the other player can collect data, based on which, she has to make a guess or bet on the scenarios.
Besides the mathematical exploration of games, in which concepts from Frequentist and Bayesian statistics can be identified in equilibrium, in Section
5
, a generalized betting game, termed “Statistical game” is introduced (for the definition, see Def.
5.1
).
Unification of Bayesian and Fisher games is possible by interpreting these as general Statistical games, differing only in the agent’s relative risk aversion.
From a mathematical point of view, the emergent structures and nontrivial limit behaviour of such games, along with their equilibrium solutions, seem worthy of detailed examination.
This work can be viewed as the beginning of the mathematical investigation and exploration of statistical games.

Later, in Section
6
, these games are proposed to be models or analogues for statistical inference itself.
This suggestion is more philosophical – and in some sense more radical. The proposition aims to ground statistical and probabilistic concepts with non-cooperative games, instead of devices of chance or subjective degree of belief.
This can be viewed as a different framework for the
interpretation
of probability and related statistical procedures.

A secondary contribution of this work
comes from a non-exhaustive but broad review of the diverse scientific literature, spread both in time and context.
The main philosophical and some technical concepts promoted in this work have been present in the literature in a fragmented form, often referred to as minimax regret criterion
[
118
,
149
]
. (The ideas of Wald
[
197
]
, Savage
[
169
]
, Good
[
57
]
, Kelly
[
96
]
, Kashyap
[
94
,
93
]
already contained the fundamental concepts from which the framework could have been constructed.)
The scope of the topic – decision making, statistics, and probabilistic inference – is enormous and highly interdisciplinary.
An incomplete list of related fields includes: Economics
[
102
]
, Philosophy
[
78
,
18
]
, Statistics
[
33
]
, Computer science
[
187
,
96
]
, Mathematics
[
99
]
, Physics
[
150
,
185
]
, Biology
[
27
]
, Machine learning
[
124
,
14
,
162
,
182
]
et cetera
1
1

1

Finance
[
87
,
142
]
, Control theory
[
183
]
, Operations Research
[
201
,
50
]
…
.
The collection of related ideas – which do not necessarily refer to each other – and presenting these concepts in a unified, coherent framework will hopefully inspire further research and stimulate interdisciplinary collaboration.

Hopefully, the presented simple but clear toy models can serve as a solid foundation for future research and development, and – together with the listed future directions in Section
7
– build a compelling case for a more general and coherent framework for decision making in the face of uncertainty.

2
Binary decisions

Also known as forced binary choice
[
52
]
, binary classification problem
[
55
]
or two-category classification
[
160
]
. It can be viewed as a hypothesis testing problem
[
52
,
33
]
, in which one alternative must be selected.

2.1
Description of the game

Definition 2.1

(Fisher game)
.

There are two players,
Player 1
and
Player 2
.

Player 2
needs to choose between scenario A or B first and then produce a binary sequence of length
M
containing precisely
K_{A}
or
K_{B}
number of
1
-s. (Without losing generality, we will assume
K_{A}\leq K_{B}
.)
Following this,
Player 1
(not knowing the actions of
Player 2
) can sample
N
number of bits, and after observing their value, she guesses scenario A or B.

If
Player 1
guessed the scenario correctly, she wins the game (
\blacksquare
) and loses otherwise (
\blacksquare
).
The above-defined Fisher game will be denoted as

{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
.

∎

A tabular description of a general Fisher game is shown by Game Table
1
.

Game Table 1
:
General description of

{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)

Player 1

Player 2

•

Chooses scenario A or B,

–

then chooses a binary sequence available for the chosen scenario.

•

Chooses
N
indices for sampling,

–

based on the bits in the chosen sample, guesses scenario A or B.

If
Player 1
guessed the scenario correctly, she wins the game (
\blacksquare
) and loses otherwise (
\blacksquare
).

2.1.1
Trivial cases

Blind guessing:

A maximally trivial version of this guessing game occurs when
M=0
, i.e. there are no sequences to investigate. In this case,
Player 1
needs to guess blindly.
Formally, we will call this game
{\swabfamily Game}(N=0,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=0,M=0)
.
The utility matrix of this game looks the following
2
2

2

a utility matrix for
Player 1
is a matrix, in which rows represent the actions of
Player 1
(in this case guessing A, guessing B), and columns represent the actions of
Player 2
(in this case choosing A, choosing B). Therefore
Player 1
can pick a row, while
Player 2
selects a column. The matrix element in the intersection represents the utility for
Player 1
of the consequence of the selected actions.
:

u_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\end{bmatrix}

This is equivalent to the well-known game called Matching Pennies
[
97
]

3
3

3

alternatively it can be viewed as a “Guess The Hand”, “Which hand is the coin in?” or “Hand Game”
[
34
,
196
]
guessing game
. In the context of Game Theory
[
97
,
117
,
32
,
128
]
the game has one unique Nash equilibrium: the players are guessing/choosing A or B with an equal
50\%
chance, and both players have a
50\%
chance to win or lose.

Any further games, where
M>0
, while
N=0
are degenerate
[
132
]
cases of the blind guessing game.
4
4

4

Another family of games leading to blind guessing are games in which
K_{A}=K_{B}
. In this case,
Player 1
gains no information about A or B by sampling from the provided sequence.

Sure winning:

A further trivial case is
{\swabfamily Game}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=1)
. For this situation, if
Player 2
chooses A, then she needs to provide the sequence
(0)
(or
(\square)
), while if she chooses B, she must provide sequence
(1)
(or
(\blacksquare)
).

Player 1
can sample one bit, which can be chosen only one way and which completely reveals the choice of
Player 2
.
Player 1
has an absolute winning strategy denoted by the set of rules:
\{(\square)\to A,(\blacksquare)\to B\}
(if she sees
\square
, chooses A, if she sees
\blacksquare
, chooses B). This strategy guarantees a
100\%
success rate for
Player 1
regardless of
Player 2
’s strategy.

In all games in which
N>0
,
K_{A}=0
,
K_{B}=M
,
M>0
,
Player 1
has essentially the same trivial absolute winning strategy.

Another trivial sure winning strategy is available when
N=M
, i.e.
Player 1
can sample all the bits.
5
5

5

In general, we will call a game “Sure winning” if there is no possible observation after sampling, which could be realized both by scenario A and B. Using the notation in Section
2.3
this formally means that
\mathbb{K}_{AB}=\mathbb{K}_{A}\cap\mathbb{K}_{B}=\emptyset
.

2.1.2
Smallest nontrivial case

The smallest and simplest nontrivial game is
{\swabfamily Game}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)
, which can be seen from Table
3
.
6
6

6

The other smallest nontrivial game is
{\swabfamily Game}(N=1,K_{A}=1,K_{B}=2,M=2)
. The two games are related by the transformation
\square\leftrightarrow\blacksquare
, and
A\leftrightarrow B
; therefore, results from one generalizes for both.

Player 2
can either choose A, in which case only one sequence is possible:

\{(\square,\square)\}

Alternatively, she can choose scenario B, by which two sequences can be generated:

\{(\square,\blacksquare),(\blacksquare,\square)\}

These are
1+2=3
distinct sequences in total.

Player 1
can sample from
2
indices. The observed bit can take
2
values (
\square
or
\blacksquare
). Based on this information, she can guess one of the
2
scenarios, A or B. This results in a total of
2\times 2^{2}=8
possible choices.

2.1.3
Exact solution of the smallest nontrivial case

Action sets:

In this small finite case, it is feasible to enumerate all possible actions of the players, construct the utility matrix, and find all equilibrium strategies of the players.

The action set of
Player 2
is denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
For clarity, the notation will be kept redundant. As an example,
(B,(\square,\blacksquare))
stands for the action of
Player 2
, in which she has chosen scenario B and picked
(\square,\blacksquare)
from the allowed sequences in scenario B. Enumeration of all possible choices of
Player 2
results:

\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{(A,(\square,\square)),(B,(\square,\blacksquare)),(B,(\blacksquare,\square))\}

(1)

The whole action set of
Player 1
is more complicated.
To clarify the notation, let us take an example:
Player 1
selects the second bit to investigate, and if it is
\square
, then guesses scenario A and if the bit is
\blacksquare
, then guesses B. This will be denoted as
(2,\{(\square)\to A,(\blacksquare)\to B\})
. Using this notation, the full action set looks the following:

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{1}=\{&(1,\{(\square)\to A,(\blacksquare)\to A\}),(1,\{(\square)\to A,(\blacksquare)\to B\}),\\
&(1,\{(\square)\to B,(\blacksquare)\to A\}),(1,\{(\square)\to B,(\blacksquare)\to B\}),\\
&(2,\{(\square)\to A,(\blacksquare)\to A\}),(2,\{(\square)\to A,(\blacksquare)\to B\}),\\
&(2,\{(\square)\to B,(\blacksquare)\to A\}),(2,\{(\square)\to B,(\blacksquare)\to B\})\}\end{split}

(2)

Utility matrix:

It is tedious but straightforward to construct the utility matrix of this zero-sum game.
The utility matrix for
Player 1
looks the following:

u_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\end{bmatrix}

(3)

Steps toward solution:

Either by eliminating dominated strategies
[
97
,
117
]
, or using common sense, one can exclude strategies in which
Player 1
would guess A after seeing a
\blacksquare
. This can reduce the action set of
Player 1
:

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}=\{&(1,\{(\square)\to A,(\blacksquare)\to B\}),(1,\{(\square)\to B,(\blacksquare)\to B\}),\\
&(2,\{(\square)\to A,(\blacksquare)\to B\}),(2,\{(\square)\to B,(\blacksquare)\to B\})\}\end{split}

(4)

resulting in a reduced utility matrix:

u_{1}^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\end{bmatrix}

(5)

This finite zero-sum game can be solved completely
7
7

7

even using a standard computational software, for example
online bimatrix solver
,
Nashpy
,
Sage
or
Gambit
etc
…
:

First, we might observe that the
2.
and
4.
rows in the reduced utility matrix are identical. Let us assume that we can reach an equilibrium by keeping only one from rows
2
and
4
. (This assumption needs to be checked in the end.)
If we delete the
4.
row and swap the
1.
row with the
2.
row, then we get the following utility matrix:

u_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}&{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}\end{bmatrix}

(6)

We get the same utility matrix if we delete the
2.
row and then permute the new rows
\{1\to 2,2\to 3,3\to 1\}
.

Matching Game:

A game with this utility matrix can be reinterpreted as a (reverse) “Matching Game”:

Imagine that both
Player 1
and
Player 2
can choose from
3
different items (I, II, III).
Player 1
loses (
{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}
) if they choose the same item (and wins (
{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}
) otherwise).
8
8

8

The game is also similar to a
Cups and Balls trick
(also known as Shell Game) from the tricksters perspective, if her moves were disregarded by the chooser.

This is a symmetric, zero-sum game, which is easier to understand intuitively.
The game has one single Nash equilibrium, in which all players are randomizing uniformly between all possible items. This behaviour is described by the following mixed strategy profile, which is essentially a pair of probability distributions for all possible actions.

\underline{\sigma}^{\prime\prime*}_{1}=(1/3,1/3,1/3)\quad\underline{\sigma}^{\prime\prime*}_{2}=(1/3,1/3,1/3)

(7)

To prove that this is indeed a Nash equilibrium, one needs to check if
Player 1
or
Player 2
can expect more utility by unilaterally deviating from her strategy. If
Player 2
chooses items I, II, III uniformly, all choices of
Player 1
lead to the same
2/3
expected chance of winning. This shows that there is no item, the choice of which could enhance
Player 1
’s winning chance. Similarly, if
Player 1
chooses items I, II, and III uniformly, then
Player 2
has a
1/3
chance of winning with all items. This proves that this strategy profile is a Nash equilibrium.

There is another, more dynamic way to approach this game:
If
Player 1
would use any mixed strategy profile
\underline{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, then
Player 2
could learn her distribution and always choose the item which is chosen by
Player 1
most frequently.
Formally, the minimal chance of winning for
Player 1
when she uses a
\underline{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}_{1}
profile is:

v_{1}^{\mathrm{worst}}(\underline{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}_{1})=1-\max(\underline{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}_{1})

(8)

Player 1
can maximize her worst-case winning rate if she minimizes the maximum of her mixed strategy’s distribution. For
3
items, the minimum of the probabilities maximum is
1/3
, when all items are chosen uniformly.
A similar line of reasoning shows that
Player 2
can maximize her minimal winning rate by uniform randomization.

This is why equilibrium strategies in zero-sum games are often called “minimax” strategies.

Switching to the original game:

We can translate this equilibrium strategy profile back to the original game and actions. To check if this is a Nash equilibrium in the complete action set, we need to show that
Player 1
can not get better results by playing the deleted row:

To see this, one needs to recall that the deleted row was always identical to a row that remained in the reduced game (namely the row: choosing I), so the expected winning rate is the same
2/3
for it. This shows that
Player 1
can not gain more by including the excluded action in the mixture.

Complete solution of the smallest nontrivial game:

It might have been surprising from the original description that this game has not only one unique Nash equilibrium but a continuous set of possible equilibrium strategies.
9
9

9

This is equivalent to the statement that this game is degenerate
[
132
]
.
The spanning Nash equilibria for the reduced action set are:

\begin{split}{\underline{\sigma}^{\prime*}_{1}}^{(1)}=(1/3,1/3,1/3,0),&\quad{\underline{\sigma}^{\prime*}_{2}}^{(1)}=(1/3,1/3,1/3),\\
{\underline{\sigma}^{\prime*}_{1}}^{(2)}=(1/3,0,1/3,1/3),&\quad{\underline{\sigma}^{\prime*}_{2}}^{(2)}=(1/3,1/3,1/3)\end{split}

(9)

All equilibria can be characterized as a one-parameter family, with a continuous parameter
\lambda\in[0,1]
:

\underline{\sigma}^{\prime*}_{1}(\lambda)=(1/3,\lambda/3,1/3,(1-\lambda)/3),\quad\underline{\sigma}^{\prime*}_{2}(\lambda)=(1/3,1/3,1/3),

(10)

Symmetric solution:

There is only one symmetrical solution when
Player 1
picks the first and second bit equally likely. This solution corresponds to
\lambda=1/2
:

\underline{\sigma}^{\prime*S}_{1}=(1/3,1/6,1/3,1/6),\quad\underline{\sigma}^{\prime*S}_{2}=(1/3,1/3,1/3)

(11)

One can reinterpret the symmetrical solution in a more familiar procedural language, described in Game Table
2
.

Game Table 2
:
Symmetric equilibrium strategy for

{\swabfamily Game}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)

Player 1

Player 2

•

Choose scenario A with probability
1/3
, and scenario B with probability
2/3

–

Choose uniformly from all different allowed sequences.

•

Sample randomly from all possible indices uniformly

–

in case the sampled bit is
\square
:

*

guess A with probability
2/3

*

and B with probability
1/3

–

in case the sampled bit is
\blacksquare
:

*

guess B

The optimal policy is visualized in figure
1
.

Figure 1
:
Visualization of the optimal guessing policy for
Player 1
in the game:
{\swabfamily Game}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)
. Where
k
is the number of
\blacksquare
-s in the sample, and the height corresponds to the chance of choosing A from the possible scenarios.

2.3
General structure of the action sets

Most general construction:

The action set of
Player 2
contains all the possible permutations of the sequences allowed in scenario A or B, denoted by
\mathcal{K}_{A}
and
\mathcal{K}_{B}
:

\mathcal{K}_{A}=\{\underline{b}\in\{\square,\blacksquare\}^{M}\ |\ \#_{\blacksquare}\ \underline{b}=K_{A}\}

(12)

\mathcal{K}_{B}=\{\underline{b}\in\{\square,\blacksquare\}^{M}\ |\ \#_{\blacksquare}\ \underline{b}=K_{B}\}

(13)

Where the notation
\#_{y}X
stands for the number of members in
X
equal to
y
.

The possible actions of
Player 2
is the union of all allowed sequences:

\mathcal{A}_{2}=\mathcal{K}_{A}\cup\mathcal{K}_{B}

(14)

(To reproduce the redundant notation used in the previous section, one can additionally label the sequences with A or B:
\mathcal{A}_{2}=\left(\{A\}\times\mathcal{K}_{A}\right)\cup\left(\{B\}\times\mathcal{K}_{B}\right)
.)

The action set of
Player 1
consists of two parts: firstly, choosing a concrete sampling and, subsequently, selecting a policy.

\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{P}

(15)

\mathcal{S}=\{S\subset\{1,\dots,M\}\ |\ |S|=N\}

(16)

\mathcal{P}=\{\phi:\{\square,\blacksquare\}^{N}\mapsto\{A,B\}\}

(17)

Action set sizes:

To navigate better in the action spaces, calculating the size of these sets might be illuminating. This can be done by simple combinatorics.
In the general case
{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)

Player 2
has the following number of possible actions:

|\mathcal{A}_{2}|=|\mathcal{K}_{A}|+|\mathcal{K}_{B}|=\binom{M}{K_{A}}+\binom{M}{K_{B}}

(18)

If we take into account all possible guesses for all possible sample sequences, we get the following double exponential expression for the action set size of
Player 1
:

|\mathcal{A}_{1}|=|\mathcal{S}|\times|\mathcal{P}|=\binom{M}{N}\ 2^{2^{N}}

(19)

2.3.1
Policy restrictions

For a general
{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
the number of
\blacksquare
-s,
k
might take values only from a restricted set
\mathbb{K}
.
If
Player 2
chooses scenario A, then in the sample, maximally
K_{A}
number of
\blacksquare
-s, and maximally
M-K_{A}
number of
\square
-s can appear. If
N
is greater then the maximal number of
\blacksquare
-s or
\square
-s, then
k
can take values only from a restricted set:

\mathbb{K}_{A}=\{\max\{0,N-(M-K_{A})\},\dots,\min\{K_{A},N\}\}

(20)

If
Player 2
chooses scenario B, then the potentially restricted set looks the following:

\mathbb{K}_{B}=\{\max\{0,N-(M-K_{B})\},\dots,\min\{K_{B},N\}\}

(21)

Figure 2
:
Illustration of
\mathbb{K}_{A}
and
\mathbb{K}_{B}
for
{\swabfamily Game}(N=6,K_{A}=4,K_{B}=5,M=8)

Player 1
does not know in advance if scenario A or B has been chosen by
Player 2
. Therefore, she needs to prepare strategies for any possible restricted outcome:

\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{K}_{A}\cup\mathbb{K}_{B}

(22)

This can slightly restrict the policy set of
Player 1

10
10

10

because
Player 1
does not need to consider policies for sampled sequences, which can not come out under any scenario
:

|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|=\left(2^{\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}}\binom{N}{k}}\right)\leq|\mathcal{P}|=2^{2^{N}}

(23)

Permutation invariant policies:

A meaningful restriction of the guessing game could be that
Player 1
needs to make her guess only by knowing the total number of
\blacksquare
-s in the sampled subsequence, regardless of the exact order of bits in the sample. In this case, the number of actions for
Player 1
is greatly reduced:

|\mathcal{P}^{R}|=2^{N+1}

(24)

|\mathcal{P^{\prime}}^{R}|=2^{|\mathbb{K}|}

(25)

However, there might be strategies played by
Player 2
for which the best response of
Player 1
is not part of the
\mathcal{P}^{R}
set.
In the following parts, we will focus on equilibrium strategies in which this restriction will be justified.

Obviously dominated policies:

We can restrict further the set of policies by removing obviously wrong choices.
A sane player will not choose scenario B, seeing a sample which could only come out under scenario A (and vice versa).
Formally: any policy, which gives B for any sample, in which the number of
\blacksquare
-s
k\in\mathbb{K}_{A}\setminus\mathbb{K}_{B}
is dominated by policies, which always gives A in such case.
To reduce the possible policies, one can keep only those which can not be dominated in this obvious way:

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}=\{\phi:\{\square,\blacksquare\}^{N}\mapsto\{A,B\}\ |&\ \forall(\underline{d}\in\{\square,\blacksquare\}^{N},\#_{\blacksquare}\underline{d}\in\mathbb{K_{A}}\setminus\mathbb{K}_{B})\ \phi(\underline{d})=A\\
&\ \forall(\underline{d}\in\{\square,\blacksquare\}^{N},\#_{\blacksquare}\underline{d}\in\mathbb{K_{B}}\setminus\mathbb{K}_{A})\ \phi(\underline{d})=B\}\end{split}

(26)

This condition keeps the possible policies unrestricted only on the set:

\mathbb{K}_{AB}=\mathbb{K}_{A}\cap\mathbb{K}_{B}

(27)

|\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}|=2^{\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}\binom{N}{k}}

(28)

This, combined with permutation invariance, gives:

|\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime R}|=2^{|\mathbb{K}_{AB}|}

(29)

2.4
Symmetric solution of the general case

Without losing generality, we will assume that
K_{A}<K_{B}
in this section.

The Ansatz:

An Ansatz
11
11

11

an educated guess, or an assumption about the form of the solution
can be formulated for the general case, described in Game Table
3
.

Game Table 3
:

Ansatz
for the general
{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
case,

having 3 free variables:
P
,
k^{\bullet}
and
\nu
:

Player 1

Player 2

•

choose scenario A with probability
\pi(\mathrm{A})=P
, or B with probability
\pi(\mathrm{B})=1-P
.

–

Choose uniformly from all different allowed sequences.

•

sample
N
bits randomly and uniformly from all available
M
bits,

–

in case the number of
\blacksquare
-s
k<k^{\bullet}
guess A,

–

in case
k=k^{\bullet}
:

*

guess A with probability
\mu(\hat{\phi})=\nu
,

*

guess B with probability
\mu(\check{\phi})=1-\nu
,

–

in case the number of
\blacksquare
-s
k>k^{\bullet}
guess B.

Fixing the parameters:

For the sake of derivation, we denote by
p_{k}(A)
the probability that in scenario A,
N
randomly selected bits contain precisely
k
number of
\blacksquare
-s. (While
p_{k}(B)
denotes the same concept for scenario B).

To be specific, these probabilities follow a hypergeometric distribution
[
15
,
52
,
156
]
:

p_{k}(A)=\frac{\binom{K_{A}}{k}\binom{M-K_{A}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}},\quad p_{k}(B)=\frac{\binom{K_{B}}{k}\binom{M-K_{B}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}}

(30)

Figure 3
:
Illustration of
p_{k}(A)
and
p_{k}(B)
for
{\swabfamily Game}(N=17,K_{A}=10,K_{B}=16,M=27)
.

In formulating the parameters, we will not use the specific form of these probabilities, only a generic observation, vaguely stating: “as
k
becomes larger, scenario B becomes more probable than scenario A”. The property we will use can be formulated in the following way:

\forall\ \ell,k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB},\ \ell>k\implies\frac{p_{\ell}(B)}{p_{\ell}(A)}>\frac{p_{k}(B)}{p_{k}(A)}

(31)

To maximize her expected utility,
Player 1
should always choose:

\begin{split}&A,\quad\mathrm{If}\quad P\ p_{k}(A)>(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)\\
&B,\quad\mathrm{If}\quad P\ p_{k}(A)<(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)\end{split}

(32)

This leaves only one possibility for the case in which
k=k^{\bullet}

12
12

12

in the general case, when
\nu\neq 0
, which is equality:

P\ p_{k^{\bullet}}(A)=(1-P)\ p_{k^{\bullet}}(B)

(33)

Equation (
33
) can determine
P
as a function of
k^{\bullet}
:

P(k^{\bullet})=\frac{p_{k^{\bullet}}(B)}{p_{k^{\bullet}}(A)+p_{k^{\bullet}}(B)}

(34)

Player 2
is mixing between A and B; therefore, she needs to be indifferent about choosing between them:

\sum_{k<k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(A)+\nu\ p_{k^{\bullet}}(A)=\sum_{k>k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(B)+(1-\nu)\ p_{k^{\bullet}}(B)

(35)

By rearranging the terms, we get an expression for
\nu
:

\begin{split}\mu(\hat{\phi})&=\nu=\frac{\sum_{k\geq k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(B)-\sum_{k<k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(A)}{p_{k^{\bullet}}(A)+p_{k^{\bullet}}(B)}\\
\mu(\check{\phi})&=1-\nu=\frac{\sum_{k\leq k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(A)-\sum_{k>k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(B)}{p_{k^{\bullet}}(A)+p_{k^{\bullet}}(B)}\end{split}

(36)

(It is important to note that
\nu
represents a probability. We will use the convention that
\nu\in[0,1)
)

Collecting all the variables, we can express the probability of guessing correctly for
Player 1

13
13

13

setting the utility of winning to
1
and losing to
0
, this is equivalent to the expected utility or “value” of the game
as a function of
k^{\bullet}
:

v(k^{\bullet})=\frac{p_{k^{\bullet}}(A)\ \left(\sum_{k\geq k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(B)\right)+p_{k^{\bullet}}(B)\ \left(\sum_{k<k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(A)\right)}{p_{k^{\bullet}}(A)+p_{k^{\bullet}}(B)}

(37)

Player 1
can maximize this quantity by appropriately choosing
k^{\bullet}
.
An alternative approach to find
k^{\bullet}
is to satisfy the inequalities:
0\leq\nu<1
,
0<1-\nu\leq 1
together with equations (
36
):

\sum_{k\geq k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(B)\geq\sum_{k<k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(A)

(38)

and

\sum_{k>k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(B)<\sum_{k\leq k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(A)

(39)

2.4.1
Main theorem on Fisher games

Theorem 2.1

(Symmetrical equilibrium)
.

{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
has a symmetrical Nash equilibrium, in which:

•

Player 2
chooses scenario A or B with probability
P^{*}
and
1-P^{*}
;

–

then picks an allowed sequence with equal probability (from
\mathcal{K}_{A}
or
\mathcal{K}_{B}
).

•

Player 1
first samples
N
bits uniformly from the provided sequence. Based on
k
– the number of
\blacksquare
-s – she performs the following action:

–

if
k<k^{*}
she guesses A

–

if
k=k^{*}
then

*

she guesses A with probability
\nu^{*}
or B with probability
1-\nu^{*}

–

if
k>k^{*}
she guesses B

The parameters
(k^{*},\nu^{*},P^{*})
can be determined from the parameters of the game
(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
:

p_{k}(A)=\frac{\binom{K_{A}}{k}\binom{M-K_{A}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}},\quad p_{k}(B)=\frac{\binom{K_{B}}{k}\binom{M-K_{B}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}}

(40)

P^{*}=\frac{p_{k^{*}}(B)}{p_{k^{*}}(A)+p_{k^{*}}(B)}

(41)

\nu^{*}=\frac{\sum_{k\geq k^{*}}p_{k}(B)-\sum_{k<k^{*}}p_{k}(A)}{p_{k^{*}}(A)+p_{k^{*}}(B)}

(42)

Finally,
k^{*}
is the smallest integer, for which the sum of probabilities becomes greater than
1
:

\sum_{k\leq k^{*}}p_{k}(A)+p_{k}(B)>1,\quad\mathrm{while}\quad\sum_{k<k^{*}}p_{k}(A)+p_{k}(B)\leq 1

(43)

∎

For the proof, see Appendix
A
.

Remark
.

The equilibrium quantity
k^{*}
can be interpreted as the median
[
170
,
15
]
of a mixture of random variables
\kappa_{A},\kappa_{B}
:

k^{*}=\mathbbm{m}\left[\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{A}+\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{B}\right]

(44)

where
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{B}
are characterized by
p_{k}(A)
and
p_{k}(B)
. And
\mathbbm{m}[.]
represents the (strong) median
[
91
]
:

\mathbbm{m}[\kappa]=c\iff\Pr(\kappa\leq c)\geq\frac{1}{2}\ \wedge\ \Pr(\kappa\geq c)\geq\frac{1}{2}

(45)

(The only difference is that such definition would imply the
\nu\in(0,1]
choice instead of
\nu\in[0,1)
.)

∎

Lemma 2.1

(Intermediacy of the median)
.

For any random variables with unique median
\mathbbm{m}[\xi]\leq\mathbbm{m}[\eta]
and any
\lambda\in[0,1]
mixing parameter (if
\lambda\cdot\xi+(1-\lambda)\cdot\eta
also has a unique median):

\mathbbm{m}[\xi]\leq\mathbbm{m}[\lambda\cdot\xi+(1-\lambda)\cdot\eta]\leq\mathbbm{m}[\eta]

(46)

∎

The proof can be found in Appendix
A
, Section
A.0.1
.

Theorem 2.2
.

The intermediacy property of the median implies the following bounds for
k^{*}
:

\mathbbm{m}[\kappa_{A}]\leq k^{*}\leq\mathbbm{m}[\kappa_{B}]

(47)

where
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{B}
represent random variables with hypergeometric distribution:

\kappa_{A}\sim\mathrm{Hypergeom}(N,K_{A},M),\quad\kappa_{B}\sim\mathrm{Hypergeom}(N,K_{B},M)

(48)

\Pr(\kappa_{A}=k)=p_{k}(A)=\frac{\binom{K_{A}}{k}\binom{M-K_{A}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}},\quad\Pr(\kappa_{B}=k)=p_{k}(B)=\frac{\binom{K_{B}}{k}\binom{M-K_{B}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}}

(49)

∎

Theorem 2.3

(Symmetric equilibrium is Optimal)
.

There is no equilibrium which yields a greater winning rate for
Player 1
than the Symmetric equilibrium.

∎

Proof.

This is a special case of a more general theorem:

Minimax theorem (von Neumann, 1928)

In any finite, two-player, zero-sum game, in any Nash equilibrium each player receives a payoff that is equal to both his maxmin value and his minmax value.

— Theorem 3.1.4 in
[
97
]
.

∎

Remark
.

In the general case,
{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
is highly degenerate. Meaning that its solution is very far from unique and, in fact, has a continuous set of Nash equilibria. (Similar to the complete solution of the simplest case in (
10
).)

These solutions are spanned by strategies which are not symmetric in their sampling or the choice of binary sequences.
Any continuous mixture of these spanning solutions is itself a Nash equilibrium, meaning that the set of solutions forms a high dimensional simplex.

∎

Definition 2.2
.

A two-player game is called
nondegenerate
if no mixed strategy
of support size
k

14
14

14

a mixed strategy can be viewed as a discrete probability distribution on the possible actions, and so its support size is the support of this distribution, i.e. the set of actions which are played with nonzero probability
has more than
k
pure best responses
15
15

15

an action belongs to the set of “pure best responses” – responding to the other player’s strategy –, if no other action could yield higher expected utility (or expected winning chance in the context of Fisher games)
.
[
132
]

∎

Actions appearing in equilibrium:

In the symmetric solution, the support of
Player 2
’s strategy includes all possible sequences:

\supp(\sigma_{2})=\mathcal{A}_{2}=\mathcal{K}_{A}\cup\mathcal{K}_{B}

(50)

|\supp(\sigma_{2})|=|\mathcal{K}_{A}|+|\mathcal{K}_{B}|=\binom{M}{K_{A}}+\binom{M}{K_{B}}

(51)

For
Player 1
, the symmetric equilibrium strategy contains all sampling choices twice if
\nu>0
. First, with a policy, in which she guesses A if
k=k^{*}
(denoted by
\hat{\phi}
), and second in which she guesses B if
k=k^{*}
(denoted by
\check{\phi}
). If
\nu=0
, then only the second policy remains part of the support:

\supp(\sigma_{1})=\begin{cases}\mathcal{S}\times\{\hat{\phi},\check{\phi}\}&\text{if }\nu>0\\
\mathcal{S}\times\{\check{\phi}\}&\text{if }\nu=0\end{cases}

(52)

|\supp(\sigma_{1})|=\binom{M}{N}\times(1+\lceil\nu\rceil)

(53)

All actions with nonzero probability in the equilibrium strategy profile are, by definition, best response regarding the other player’s strategy. In the general case

|\supp(\sigma_{1})|=(1+\lceil\nu\rceil)\ \binom{M}{N}\neq\binom{M}{K_{A}}+\binom{M}{K_{B}}=|\supp(\sigma_{2})|

(54)

This shows that in general,
{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
is degenerate, i.e. the symmetric Nash equilibrium is not a unique equilibrium strategy profile. Uniqueness could be possible only if (
54
) becomes an equality.

2.5
Examples and Visualizations

Equilibrium strategy plot:

To show the structure and equilibrium parameters of general
{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
-s, we introduce the “strategy plot”. Figure
4
shows the equilibrium solution of the smallest nontrivial example,
{\swabfamily Game}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)
.

Figure 4
:
Strategy plot for the symmetric equilibrium of
{\swabfamily Game}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)
.

Definition 2.3

(Equilibrium strategy plot)
.

Equilibrium strategy plot is a square-shaped collection of tiles which are divided into two parts, both horizontally and vertically:

•

Horizontally, it is first divided into two parts, in the ratio
P^{*}
:
1-P^{*}
, representing the equilibrium probability of
Player 2
choosing A or B. These two bigger domains are subdivided into equal parts, representing all possible sequences (in lexicographic ordering
[
171
]
), which can be realized in scenarios A or B.

•

Vertically it is first divided into two parts, in the ratio
\nu^{*}
:
1-\nu^{*}
, representing the equilibrium probability of
Player 1
choosing the policy
\hat{\phi}
or
\check{\phi}
. These bigger two domains are subdivided into equal parts, representing all possible
N
long sampling choices from
M
long sequences (in lexicographic ordering
[
171
]
).

By this construction, all pairs of actions appearing in the equilibrium solution are represented proportionally to the probability they appear in the equilibrium strategy profile.

Every tile, representing a specific pair of actions, is coloured according to the winning (
{\color[rgb]{0.9063,0.6289,0.2969}\blacksquare}
) or losing (
{\color[rgb]{0.1445,0.3281,0.543}\blacksquare}
) of
Player 1
in that case.

∎

For further, slightly larger examples, see figures
5(a)
,
5(b)
, which starts to reveal a more general structure and intriguing patterns.

(a)

{\swabfamily Game}(N=2,K_{A}=2,K_{B}=4,M=7)

(b)

{\swabfamily Game}(N=3,K_{A}=2,K_{B}=4,M=8)

Figure 5
:
Strategy plots for symmetric equilibria

Mainly for aesthetic reasons, a relatively large, highly symmetric equilibrium strategy has been selected in figure
6
, showing an intricate fractal-like pattern.

Figure 6
:
Strategy plot for the symmetric equilibrium of
{\swabfamily Game}(N=4,K_{A}=4,K_{B}=6,M=10)
.

Generalization of variables:

For aesthetic reasons, we will allow games with
K_{A}\geq K_{B}
, with the following convention
16
16

16

P^{*}_{o}
,
k^{*}_{o}
,
\nu^{*}_{o}
and
v^{*}_{o}
stands for the original quantities described in the previous sections (where we assumed that
K_{A}<K_{B}
)
:

P^{*}(K_{A},K_{B})=\begin{cases}\pi(A)=P^{*}_{o}(K_{A},K_{B})&\text{if }K_{A}<K_{B}\\
1/2&\text{if }K_{A}=K_{B}\\
\pi(B)=P^{*}_{o}(K_{B},K_{A})&\text{if }K_{A}>K_{B}\end{cases}

(55)

k^{*}(K_{A},K_{B})=\begin{cases}k^{*}_{o}(K_{A},K_{B})&\text{if }K_{A}<K_{B}\\
-&\text{if }K_{A}=K_{B}\\
k^{*}_{o}(K_{B},K_{A})&\text{if }K_{A}>K_{B}\end{cases}

(56)

\nu^{*}(K_{A},K_{B})=\begin{cases}\nu^{*}_{o}(K_{A},K_{B})&\text{if }K_{A}<K_{B}\\
-&\text{if }K_{A}=K_{B}\\
\nu^{*}_{o}(K_{B},K_{A})&\text{if }K_{A}>K_{B}\end{cases}

(57)

For the winning rate (or value):

v^{*}(K_{A},K_{B})=\begin{cases}v^{*}_{o}(K_{A},K_{B})&\text{if }K_{A}<K_{B}\\
1/2&\text{if }K_{A}=K_{B}\\
v^{*}_{o}(K_{B},K_{A})&\text{if }K_{A}>K_{B}\end{cases}

(58)

And an introduced new variable
s^{*}
combining
k^{*}
and
\nu^{*}
can be generalized automaticly:

s^{*}=\frac{k^{*}+\nu^{*}}{N+1}

(59)

2.6
Interpretation of the equilibrium strategy

Sufficient statistics:

It is worth noting that from the potentially huge amount of available policies, for the symmetric equilibrium, only the total number of
\blacksquare
-s,
k
matters for choosing a scenario.

Player 1
can ignore the actual order of bits in the sampled sequence
\underline{d}
to form an optimal strategy. This quantity (the total number of
\blacksquare
-s) can be interpreted as a
sufficient statistics

[
52
,
169
]
for this decision problem.

Type I and Type II errors:

The concepts of Type I (“false positive”, the rejection of the null hypothesis when it is true) and Type II (“false negative”, the failure to reject the null hypothesis that is false) errors were central to Neyman and Pearson
[
129
]
and are still important concepts in Hypothesis testing in “Frequentist” statistics
[
52
,
38
]
.

The equilibrium policy in a Fisher game can be viewed as a statistical test for the hypothesis, stating that A is the correct scenario.
This test’s special feature, however, is that for a particular value (
k=k^{*}
), it gives a probabilistic answer (guess/accept A with probability
\nu^{*}
and reject A with probability
1-\nu^{*}
).
In this context, we can make the following identification:

\text{Type I error:}\quad\alpha_{\mathrm{Error}}=1-v_{A}

(60)

\text{Type II error:}\quad\beta_{\mathrm{Error}}=1-v_{B}

(61)

where
v_{A}
and
v_{B}
, defined in equations (
228
) and (
229
), are the winning ratios if
Player 2
chose scenario A or B respectively.

In equilibrium, the winning ratios are equal; therefore, the winning rate, i.e. the probability of making an error, is independent of the “a priory probability” of A being the true scenario
\pi(A)=P
. By introducing the probabilities of making Type I and Type II errors:

P_{\mathrm{I}}=\pi(A)\alpha_{\mathrm{Error}}

(62)

P_{\mathrm{II}}=\pi(B)\beta_{\mathrm{Error}}

(63)

and the probability of an error of any kind:

P_{\mathrm{Error}}=P_{\mathrm{I}}+P_{\mathrm{II}}

(64)

We can conclude that the equilibrium strategy can be viewed as a “statistical test”, which is “independent of the probability law a priori”, and fulfils Definition A in
[
129
]
:

We may now discuss what meaning could be given to the words:
“a test independent of the probability law a priori.”

Definition A. The phrase might be defined as implying a choice
of critical region
w
in such a way that the probability
P_{\mathrm{Error}}
of
making an error in testing
H_{0}
had a value independent of the
probabilities
\pi(A)
,
\pi(B)
.

— Definition A in
[
129
]

18
18

18

the notation has been slightly modified to harmonize with the notation used in this work
.

Randomized sampling:

For
Player 1
, taking the sample randomly with a uniform distribution is a vital part of her equilibrium strategy.
This has also been an essential concept in “Frequentist” statistics, Fisher being its prime proponent
[
49
,
172
]
.
In the game theoretic framework, such randomized acts appear naturally as part of mixed equilibrium strategies.

Emergence of probability distributions:

The emergence of
p_{k}(A)
and
p_{k}(B)
distributions cannot be interpreted by purely combinatorial arguments (as the Classical interpretation of probability might suggest
[
78
,
4
]
).
These distributions result from the mixed equilibrium strategies of both
Player 1
and
Player 2
.
In this framework,
Player 1
is not merely a passive observer of random events but, by randomized sampling, partially contributes to the behaviour and specific distribution of the sample.

Randomized policies:

In the game theoretic framework for general parameters
Player 1
is not only sampling randomly, but there is a critical value of
\blacksquare
-s,
k=k^{*}
when her action is probabilistic: chooses A with probability
\nu^{*}
and B with probability
1-\nu^{*}
.
Such probabilistic choice or rejection is absent from both “Frequentist” and Bayesian hypothesis testing frameworks.
However, randomized policies are not uncommon in the real world. Evidence for randomization is supported from many sides:

•

From a human behavioural point of view, experts seem to realize mixed strategies by “uncorrelated” complex patterns
[
138
,
109
]
.

•

From an animal behavioural point of view, wasps seem to realize a mixed strategy
[
20
]
(for broader context, see for example
[
175
]
), and primates can learn to play Matching Pennies
[
177
]
.

•

Even in collective decision-making, ritualistic practices such as divination could play a role in randomizing the tribe’s actions
[
123
]
.

Interpretation of mixed states:

In the definition of Fisher games
2.1
, we did not include any stochastic or probabilistic parameter.
Therefore, when we talk about “probability” in the game theoretical context, then we can use a purely game theoretical interpretation:

Mixed strategy can alternatively be viewed as the belief held by all other players concerning a player’s actions. A mixed strategy equilibrium is then an
n
-tuple of common knowledge expectations, which has the property that all the actions to which a strictly positive probability is assigned are optimal, given the beliefs. A player’s behavior may be perceived by all the other players as the outcome of a random device even though this is not the case.

— Ariel Rubinstein in
[
166
]
.

This means that inside a Fisher game,
Player 1
does not need to adopt a probability concept based on frequencies
[
121
]
, degree of belief
19
19

19

or even as qua bases of action
[
152
]

[
41
]
or the principle of insufficient reason
[
4
]
.
“Probabilities” in this context appear only in the reasoning process of players, who act
as if
certain distributions would be justified, but these concepts do not have to have any grounding outside the game.
This might be called a pre-Bayesian or game theoretical interpretation of probability.

“…we do not approach the problem as Bayesians, saying that agent
i
’s
beliefs can be described by a probability distribution; instead, we use a ’pre-Bayesian’ model in which
i
does not know such a distribution and indeed has no beliefs about it.”
[
97
]
.

3
Bayesian betting

The concept is intimately related to Bayesian inference
[
41
,
88
]
, Bayesian hypothesis testing
[
41
]
and Bayesian decision theory
[
51
]
. Some authors use the analogy of a horse race
[
187
]
to introduce the concept.

3.1
General concepts

Double or nothing:

Now, we enter a different casino, where instead of betting simply on scenario A or B, we can place some portion of our capital on each alternative. The portion we put on the winning scenario (chosen by
Player 2
) will be doubled, while the portion placed on the other scenario will be lost.

3.1.1
The statistically trivial case

Remarkably, if a gambler can repeatedly bet a portion of her capital to outcome A or B (which occur with probability
P
and
1-P
respectively), then in the long run, splitting the bet can be “better” compared to placing all the money to the more likely outcome
[
96
]
.

Growth factor:

To demonstrate that a balanced splitting strategy might be “better”, we can consider the following simple example:

Let us investigate how the expectation of the ratio of capitals for two gamblers looks after
n
rounds if they use different splitting strategies for their capital. They place
p^{\prime}_{1}
and
p^{\prime}_{2}
portion of their capital to A, and
1-p^{\prime}_{1}
,
1-p^{\prime}_{2}
to B.

The ratio can be represented by a random variable
\rho
:

\rho=\frac{(2p^{\prime}_{1})^{\kappa}(2(1-p^{\prime}_{1}))^{n-\kappa}}{(2p^{\prime}_{2})^{\kappa}(2(1-p^{\prime}_{2}))^{n-\kappa}}

(65)

Where
\kappa
is a random variable representing the number of A-s in the randomly generated
n
long sequence of A-s and B-s. If the occurrence of scenarios A and B are independent in separate rounds, and their distribution is identical
\pi(A)=P
,
\pi(B)=1-P
, then
\kappa
can be characterized by the following Binomial distribution:

\kappa\sim\mathrm{Binom}(n,P)

(66)

\Pr(\kappa=k)=\binom{n}{k}P^{k}(1-P)^{n-k}

(67)

At this point, we introduce the difference of the two gamblers’ ”growth factors“:

\Delta G=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\log(\rho)]

(68)

By recalling that for a Binomial random variable
\mathbb{E}[\kappa/n]=P
, this difference can be expressed by the parameters
p^{\prime}_{1}
,
p^{\prime}_{2}
and
P
:

\begin{split}\Delta G=&\left(P\log(2\ p^{\prime}_{1})+(1-P)\log(2(1-p^{\prime}_{1}))\right)\\
&-\left(P\log(2\ p^{\prime}_{2})+(1-P)\log(2(1-p^{\prime}_{2}))\right)\end{split}

(69)

\Delta G(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})
is visualized in figure
13
, with
P=2/3
. The graph clearly shows a saddle point at
p^{\prime*}_{1}=p^{\prime*}_{2}=P=2/3
.

Theorem 3.1
.

In a repeated double or nothing gamble (where the right scenarios are identically and independently distributed with probability
P
), two gamblers’ growth rate difference has a saddle point at:

p^{\prime*}_{1}=p^{\prime*}_{2}=P

(70)

This means that for any gambler deviating from this splitting ratio, the expectation of her relative capital will be lower than her optimally playing counterpart’s capital.

∎

Proof.

This can be proved by directly solving the set of equations:

\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{1}}\Delta G(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}_{1}=p^{\prime*}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime*}_{2}}=0,\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{2}}\Delta G(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}_{1}=p^{\prime*}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime*}_{2}}=0

(71)

\frac{P}{p^{\prime*}_{1}}-\frac{1-P}{1-p^{\prime*}_{1}}=0,\quad-\frac{P}{p^{\prime*}_{2}}+\frac{1-P}{1-p^{\prime*}_{2}}=0

(72)

and directly calculating the Hessian of
\Delta G(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})
:

\underline{\underline{H}}^{*}=\frac{1}{P(1-P)}\begin{bmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

(73)

The signature of the Hessian verifies that we indeed found a saddle point.

∎

Remark

(Connection with Entropy)
.

Remarkably, the growth factor in equilibrium is closely related to the Shannon entropy (
H
)
[
174
,
187
]
of the random variable
\Pi
, which is characterized by a probability distribution
\pi
on
\Theta=\{A,B\}
:
\pi(A)=P
,
\pi(B)=1-P
.

G^{*}=P\log(2\ P)+(1-P)\log(2(1-P))

(74)

G^{*}=\log(2)+P\log(P)+(1-P)\log(1-P)

(75)

G^{*}=\log(2)-H(\Pi)

(76)

∎

Figure 13
:
Contour plot of the expected log ratio of two gamblers’ capitals (or growth factor difference)
\Delta G(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})
for
P=2/3
. (Colouring closer to blue represents negative values, while colouring closer to gold represents positive values.)

3.1.2
Adopting a utility function

In appendix
E
, I argue that adopting a utility function, which associates the logarithm of the gained capital to the utility of the outcome:

u(c)=\log(c)

(77)

is a “natural choice” in many aspects. This is because this utility function is
aligned
with a wide variety of multiplicative multiplayer contests.
Alignment in this context means that the directly calculated optimal policies in the multiplicative multiplayer games are the same
as if
agents were trying to maximise their expected logarithmic utility (For details, see Section
E.4
).

However, I want to point out that adopting a utility function is ultimately a normative choice of an agent, and different choices may result in different optimal policies.

For instance, a whole one-parameter family of utility functions fulfil a weaker consistency requirement
20
20

20

also known as isoelastic, isocurvature, power utility
[
111
]
or constant relative risk aversion (CRRA) utility
[
5
,
146
]
:

u_{\gamma}(c)=\frac{c^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(78)

Where
\gamma>0
is the so-called Arrow-Pratt measure of relative risk aversion
[
26
,
111
]
.

Assuming that the agent is maximising her expected utility
[
19
]
, different utility functions result in different optimal splitting ratios:

p^{\prime*}_{\gamma}(P)=\frac{P^{1/\gamma}}{P^{1/\gamma}+(1-P)^{1/\gamma}}

(79)

In the following sections – until stated otherwise – we will assume that
Player 1
is adopting the “most natural” logarithmic utility function.

3.2
Description of the general Bayesian game

Definition 3.1

(Bayesian game
21
21

21

not to be confused with the Bayesian Game introduced by Harsányi
[
67
,
68
,
69
]
,
[
204
]
. An alternative naming might be pre-Bayesian game
[
97
]
.
)
.

There are two players,
Player 1
and
Player 2
.

Player 2
needs to choose between scenario A or B, then produce a binary sequence of length
M
containing precisely
K_{A}
or
K_{B}
number of
1
-s. (Without losing generality, we will assume
K_{A}\leq K_{B}
.)
Following this,
Player 1
(not knowing the actions of
Player 2
) can sample
N
number of bits. After observing their values, she determines what portion of her capital
p^{\prime}
she places on scenario A (while the other
1-p^{\prime}
portion is placed on scenario B).

The portion
Player 1
places on the scenario, chosen by
Player 2
, will be doubled, while the other part of her capital will be lost.
For this specific game, we will assume that
Player 1
has a logarithmic utility function and that
Player 1
and
Player 2
are playing a zero-sum game
22
22

22

zero-sum in utilities (not in capital)
.
The above-defined Bayesian game will be denoted as

{\swabfamily BGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
.

∎

A tabular description of the general Bayesian game is shown by Game Table
4
.

Game Table 4
:
General description of

{\swabfamily BGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)

Player 1

Player 2

•

Chooses scenario A or B,

–

then chooses a binary sequence available for the chosen scenario.

•

Chooses
N
indices for sampling,

–

based on the bits in the chosen sample determines a continuous parameter
p^{\prime}\in[0,1]
,

*

and places her capital’s
p^{\prime}
portion to scenario A and
1-p^{\prime}
portion to scenario B.

The portion
Player 1
places on the scenario chosen by
Player 2
will be doubled, while the other part of her capital will be lost.

Player 1
has a logarithmic utility function,
Player 1
and
Player 2
are playing a zero-sum game.

Remark
.

Player 1
can choose from a continuous set of actions. This means that, strictly speaking, this situation cannot be solved using the framework of finite games
[
117
]
.

Unfortunately, because of the unbounded logarithmic utility function (
Player 1
can potentially realise
-\infty
losses), the problem is not even a so-called Continuous Game
[
56
]
.

However, against all these difficulties, by direct calculation, a unique equilibrium solution can be found for the above-defined Bayesian games.

∎

3.3
Simplest case

For Bayesian games, analysing the statistically trivial case might be useful, which can be considered the analogy of Blind guessing described in Section
2.1.1
.

Utility function:

In the game
{\swabfamily BGame}(N=0,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=0,M=0)

Player 1
needs to determine how she splits her capital without using any meaningful information about the scenarios.

The utility function (which is, in this logarithmic case, equivalent to the growth rate) looks the following:

U_{1}(p^{\prime})=\begin{bmatrix}\log(2\ p^{\prime})&\log(2\ (1-p^{\prime}))\end{bmatrix}

(80)

This continuous-discrete utility function is visualised in figure
14
. Since the utility function is unbounded from below (
Player 1
can lose all her capital, in which case her utility goes to
-\infty
), we used a negative cutoff. Utilities, lower than the cutoff are not present which is indicated by grey colouring.

Figure 14
:
Utility function of
{\swabfamily BGame}(N=0,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=0,M=0)
.

Equilibrium solution:

To find equilibrium, the following expected utility (or growth rate) has to be maximised by
Player 1
(controlling
p^{\prime}
) and minimised by
Player 2
(controlling P):

G(P,p^{\prime})=P\log(2\ p^{\prime})+(1-P)\log(2(1-p^{\prime}))

(81)

Maximisation respect to
p^{\prime}
:

P\frac{1}{p^{\prime*}}-(1-P)\frac{1}{1-p^{\prime*}}=0

(82)

p^{\prime*}=P

(83)

Minimising respect to
P
:

G(P)=\log(2)+P\log(P)+(1-P)\log(1-P)

(84)

G(P)=\log(2)-H(P)

(85)

which is minimal when the entropy
H(P)
is maximal, i.e.
P^{*}=1/2
.

Summarising the result:

P^{*}=1/2,\quad p^{\prime*}=1/2,\quad G^{*}=0

(86)

3.4
Simplest statistically nontrivial case

We could tabulate the smallest and simplest Bayesian games and compose a table very similar to
3
.
It is straightforward to find the analogues of Blind guessing and Sure winning games for Bayesian games.

After identifying the trivial cases, we can conclude that the simplest statistically nontrivial Bayesian game is:

{\swabfamily BGame}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)
.

Action sets:

The possible actions of
Player 2
, are the same as in (
1
):

\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{(A,(\square,\square)),(B,(\square,\blacksquare)),(B,(\blacksquare,\square))\}

(87)

Player 1
can sample one bit from indices
i\in\{1,2\}
, (formally
\mathcal{S}=\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
)
and choose 4 different splitting ratios for scenario A
23
23

23

which are the splitting ratios for scenario B subtracted from 1, because
p^{\prime}_{(S,\underline{d}\to A)}+p^{\prime}_{(S,\underline{d}\to B)}=1
:

\begin{split}p^{\prime}_{(i,\square)}&=p^{\prime}_{(\{i\},(\square)\to A)}=1-p^{\prime}_{(\{i\},(\square)\to B)}\\
p^{\prime}_{(i,\blacksquare)}&=p^{\prime}_{(\{i\},(\blacksquare)\to A)}=1-p^{\prime}_{(\{i\},(\blacksquare)\to B)}\end{split}

(88)

Utility function:

Player 1
has both discrete and continuous choices (sampling is a discrete choice, and choosing a splitting ratio is a continuous one), while
Player 2
has only discrete choices.

The utility for all possible outcomes can be captured by this hybrid utility function matrix
24
24

24

where
Player 1
is choosing a row and picks a set of
\{p^{\prime}\}
-s, while
Player 2
is choosing a column
:

U_{1}(\{p^{\prime}\})=\begin{bmatrix}\log(2p^{\prime}_{(1,\square)})&\log(2(1-p^{\prime}_{(1,\square)}))&\log(2(1-p^{\prime}_{(1,\blacksquare)}))\\
\log(2p^{\prime}_{(2,\square)})&\log(2(1-p^{\prime}_{(2,\blacksquare)}))&\log(2(1-p^{\prime}_{(2,\square)}))\end{bmatrix}

(89)

Parametrization of strategies:

We will use the following parametrization for
Player 2
’s mixed strategy in her discrete choices (
P\in[0,1],r\in[0,1]
)
25
25

25

formally the mixed strategy of choosing from allowed sequences after choosing a scenario is:
\underline{\varpi}(A)=(1)
,
\underline{\varpi}(B)=(r,1-r)
:

\underline{\sigma}_{2}=(P,(1-P)r,(1-P)(1-r))

(90)

While the mixed choice of sampling of
Player 1
will be parametrized by
q\in[0,1]
:

\underline{\eta}_{1}=(q,1-q)

(91)

Growth rate difference:

Using this notation, we can formulate the following expression for the growth rate difference relative to the sure winning case
26
26

26

measuring the difference between the absolute growth rate and the growth rate of a player who can always win (i.e. double her capital).
\Delta G=G-\log(2)
:

\begin{split}\Delta G=P&\left(q\log(p^{\prime}_{(1,\square)})+(1-q)\log(p^{\prime}_{(2,\square)})\right)+\\
(1-P)r&\left(q\log(1-p^{\prime}_{(1,\square)})+(1-q)\log(1-p^{\prime}_{(2,\blacksquare)})\right)+\\
(1-P)(1-r)&\left(q\log(1-p^{\prime}_{(1,\blacksquare)})+(1-q)\log(1-p^{\prime}_{(2,\square)}\right)\end{split}

(92)

Equilibrium solution:

First, we can observe that
Player 1
can set

p^{\prime}_{(1,\blacksquare)}=p^{\prime}_{(2,\blacksquare)}=p^{\prime*}_{1}=0,

(93)

to maximize her gain. This follows from common sense because
Player 1
can safely push all her capital to scenario B if she ever sees a
\blacksquare
in her sample (because no allowed sequence from scenario A contains
\blacksquare
).

For any
(P,r)
parameters we can find the optimal
p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)}
and
p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)}
by which
Player 1
can maximize her gain
27
27

27

this can be calculated by taking the derivative of
\Delta G
with respect to
p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)}
and
p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)}
; and then finding the values, when the derivatives are equal to zero
:

p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)}=\frac{P}{P+(1-P)r},\quad p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)}=\frac{P}{(1-r)+Pr}

(94)

If
Player 1
is using a mixed strategy, then the expected growth rate from sampling the first or second index must be equal:

P\log(p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)})+(1-P)r\log(1-p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)})=P\log(p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)})+(1-P)(1-r)\log(1-p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)})

(95)

This equation can be solved for
r
. For all
P
, this gives a simple result:

r^{*}(P)=1/2

(96)

This means that in equilibrium,
Player 2
chooses the two available sequences in scenario B uniformly.

Having a
r\in(0,1)
value for
r
means that the expression (
92
)’s derivative respect to
r
has to be 0:

q\log(1-p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)})=(1-q)\log(1-p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)})

(97)

r^{*}=1/2
, in which case
p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)}=p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)}
, so the equilibrium value for
q
has to be:

q^{*}=1/2

(98)

Combining these results, we get the following expression for
\Delta G(P)
, which has to be minimized by
Player 2
by choosing an optimal
P^{*}
:

\Delta G(P)=P\log\left(\frac{P}{1/2+P/2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}(1-P)\log\left(1-\frac{P}{1/2+P/2}\right)

(99)

After taking the derivative respect to
P
, and simplification, we get the following equation for
P^{*}
:

\log\left(\frac{2P^{*}}{1+P^{*}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{1-P^{*}}{1+P^{*}}\right)=0

(100)

\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{4{P^{*}}^{2}}{1-{P^{*}}^{2}}\right)=0

(101)

\frac{4{P^{*}}^{2}}{1-{P^{*}}^{2}}=1

(102)

P^{*}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}

(103)

Substituting back to the expressions for
p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)}
and
p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)}
we get:

p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)}=p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)}=p^{\prime*}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{5}-1\right)

(104)

Which is remarkably the reciprocal of the celebrated golden ratio
\phi
(or
\tau
)
[
40
,
47
,
63
]
.

Summary:

Figure
15
shows
\Delta G(P)/\log(2)
, which can be interpreted as a doubling factor difference relative to Sure winning. (
\Delta G(P)/\log(2)=0
means that
Player 1
can double her capital in every round, while
\Delta G(P)/\log(2)=-1
means that her capital will stagnate and not have an expected growth.)

Figure 15
:
Visualization of the doubling factor difference (relative to a Sure winning game)
\Delta G(P)/\log(2)
for
Player 1
in the game:
{\swabfamily BGame}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)
and a Blind guessing game, as a function of
Player 2
’s strategy of choosing A. The dashed grid line is placed to the equilibrium value of
P^{*}=1/\sqrt{5}
.

The equilibrium quantities are:

P^{*}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}},\quad p^{\prime*}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{5}-1\right),\quad p^{\prime*}_{1}=0

(105)

Optimal gain and doubling factor:

G^{*}\approx 0.2119,\quad\frac{G^{*}}{\log(2)}\approx 0.3058

(106)

The game table of the optimal play can be found in Game Table
5
.

Game Table 5
:
Equilibrium strategy for

{\swabfamily BGame}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)

Player 1

Player 2

•

Choose scenario A with probability
P^{*}=1/\sqrt{5}\approx 0.447
, and scenario B with probability
1-P^{*}=1-1/\sqrt{5}\approx 0.553

–

Choose uniformly from all different allowed sequences.

•

Sample randomly from all possible indices uniformly

–

in case the sampled bit is
\square
:

*

place

p^{\prime*}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{5}-1\right)\approx 0.618
portion of the capital to A

*

place

1-p^{\prime*}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\left(3-\sqrt{5}\right)\approx 0.382
portion of the capital to B

–

in case the sampled bit is
\blacksquare
:

*

place all capital to B

Remark
.

We found an equilibrium solution assuming that
Player 1
will use a pure policy, i.e. she will not mix different
\{p^{\prime}\}
splitting ratios.

A mixed policy would be suboptimal relative to an appropriately chosen pure policy because of the concavity of both expressions:

\log(p^{\prime}),\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\log(1-p^{\prime})

(107)

∎

3.5
General structure of the action sets

Continuous Policy set:

Most of the general statements about action sets remain valid from
2.3
. The most important difference is that in Bayesian games, policies are mappings from sequences to continuous splitting ratios in
[0,1]
interval.

Excluding all-in policies:

If the sample could come out both from A and B, then we can exclude “all-in” strategies, where
Player 1
would bet all her capital only to A or B. This is because, by such “reckless” behaviour, she could potentially lose all her capital, i.e. realize a
-\infty
loss.

Considering these restrictions, we can define the following general continuous policy sets:

\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{c}\cong\{\phi:\{\square,\blacksquare\}^{|\mathbb{K}_{AB}|}\mapsto(0,1)\}\cong(0,1)^{2^{|\mathbb{K}_{AB}|}}

(108)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime R}_{c}\cong\{\phi:\mathbb{K}_{AB}\mapsto(0,1)\}\cong(0,1)^{|\mathbb{K}_{AB}|}

(109)

Remark
.

These policy sets are open sets; therefore, not compact.
This prevents us from automatically using theorems for Continuous Games
[
56
]
or Separable Games
[
179
]
, for which the action sets have to be compact.

∎

Action sets:

The action sets are the following for Bayesian games:

\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{P}_{c}

(110)

Where the sampling set
\mathcal{S}
is identical to (
16
).

\mathcal{A}_{2}=\mathcal{K}_{A}\cup\mathcal{K}_{B}

(111)

Together with the logarithmic utility function, these conditions result in a Non-Compact Continuous Game.

3.6
Equilibrium solution of the general Bayesian game

The Ansatz:

An Ansatz
28
28

28

an educated guess, or an assumption about the form of the solution
can be formulated for the general case, described in Game Table
6
.

Game Table 6
:

Ansatz
for

{\swabfamily BGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)

having a set of free variables:
P
,
\{p^{\prime}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{K}}

Player 1

Player 2

•

choose scenario A with probability
\pi(\mathrm{A})=P
, or B with probability
\pi(\mathrm{B})=1-P
.

–

Choose uniformly from all different allowed sequences.

•

sample
N
bits randomly and uniformly from all available
M
bits,

–

based on the number of
\blacksquare
-s
k
, determine a continuous parameter
p^{\prime}_{k}\in[0,1]
,

*

and place the capitals
p^{\prime}_{k}
portion to scenario A and
1-p^{\prime}_{k}
portion to scenario B.

Fixing the splitting ratios:

Assuming this Ansatz, we can introduce the probabilities of seeing
k

\blacksquare
-s in the sample, given the chosen scenario by
Player 2
has been A or B:

p_{k}(A)=\frac{\binom{K_{A}}{k}\binom{M-K_{A}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}},\quad p_{k}(B)=\frac{\binom{K_{B}}{k}\binom{M-K_{B}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}}

(112)

Using these quantities, the expected logarithmic utility (or growth rate) is the following:

G=\pi(A)\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)\log(2\ p^{\prime}_{k})+\pi(B)\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)\log(2\ (1-p^{\prime}_{k}))

(113)

We can take the derivative respect to
p^{\prime}_{\ell}
, to find the maximum respect to that splitting ratio:

\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{\ell}}G\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}_{\ell}=p^{\prime*}_{\ell}}=\pi(A)p_{\ell}(A)\frac{1}{p^{\prime*}_{\ell}}-\pi(B)p_{\ell}(B)\frac{1}{1-p^{\prime*}_{\ell}}=0

(114)

p^{\prime*}_{\ell}=\frac{\pi(A)p_{\ell}(A)}{\pi(A)p_{\ell}(A)+\pi(B)p_{\ell}(B)}

(115)

Remark
.

The result for the optimal splitting ratio formally follows Bayes’ rule
[
89
]
, however, the interpretation of each individual component differs from their conventional meanings in Bayes’ rule.

∎

Finding
P^{*}
:

After substituting the optimal splitting ratios
p^{\prime*}_{k}
, we have the following equation for the growth rate difference
29
29

29

relative to the growth rate of an agent who always wins (i.e. doubles in every round).
\Delta G=G-\log(2)
:

\begin{split}\Delta G(P)=&P\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)\log\left(\frac{P\ p_{k}(A)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)p_{k}(B)}\right)+\\
&(1-P)\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)\log\left(\frac{(1-P)p_{k}(B)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)p_{k}(B)}\right)\end{split}

(116)

Which can be reorganized:

\begin{split}\Delta G(P)=&P\log(P)+(1-P)\log(1-P)+\\
&P\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)\log(p_{k}(A))+(1-P)\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)\log(p_{k}(B))-\\
&\sum_{k}(P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)p_{k}(B))\log(P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)p_{k}(B))\end{split}

(117)

Remark

(Connection with Conditional Entropy)
.

Introducing the random variables:
\Pi
, representing the randomly chosen scenarios, and
X
representing the data, i.e. the number of
\blacksquare
-s in the sample, the growth rate difference can be expressed as:

\Delta G=-H(\Pi)-H(X|\Pi)+H(X)

(118)

\Delta G=-H(\Pi|X)

(119)

i.e. the conditional Shannon entropy
[
187
]
of the scenarios, given the observed data.
(where
H(X)=H(\underline{p})=-\sum_{k}p_{k}\log(p_{k})
stands for the Shannon entropy of a discrete random variable
X
characterized by a probability distribution
\underline{p}
.)

∎

In general, the equilibrium value
P^{*}
, where
\Delta G(P)
takes its minimum, can not be expressed in closed form using elementary functions
[
163
]
.

However to show that a unique
P^{*}\in(0,1)
exists, one can observe that:
\Delta G(0)=\Delta G(1)=0
, and directly calculate the second derivative of
\Delta G(P)
respect to
P
:

\begin{split}\Delta G^{\prime}(P)=&\log(P)-\log(1-P)-\Delta H+\\
&-\sum_{k}(p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B))\log(P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)p_{k}(B))\end{split}

(120)

where:

\Delta H=H_{A}-H_{B}

(121)

H_{A}=-\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)\log(p_{k}(A)),\quad H_{B}=-\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)\log(p_{k}(B))

(122)

and

\Delta G^{\prime\prime}(P)=\frac{1}{P}+\frac{1}{1-P}-\sum_{k}\frac{(p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B))^{2}}{Pp_{k}(A)+(1-P)p_{k}(B)}

(123)

\Delta G^{\prime\prime}(P)=\sum_{k}\left(\frac{p_{k}(A)}{P}+\frac{p_{k}(B)}{1-P}-\frac{(p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B))^{2}}{Pp_{k}(A)+(1-P)p_{k}(B)}\right)

(124)

\Delta G^{\prime\prime}(P)=\sum_{k}\frac{p_{k}(A)p_{k}(B)}{P(1-P)(Pp_{k}(A)+(1-P)p_{k}(B))}

(125)

The second derivative is positive for all
P\in(0,1)
, meaning that this is a convex optimization problem
[
180
]
, which has one unique solution
P^{*}\in(0,1)
.

P^{*}
can be found numerically, for instance, using the Bisection method
[
148
]
on
\Delta G^{\prime}(P)
, resulting in a simple and robust controlled approximation.

3.6.1
Main theorem on Bayesian games

Theorem 3.2

(Bayesian equilibrium)
.

{\swabfamily BGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
has a unique Nash equilibrium, in which:

•

Player 2
chooses scenario A or B with probability
P^{*}
and
1-P^{*}
;

–

then picks an allowed sequence with equal probability (from
\mathcal{K}_{A}
or
\mathcal{K}_{B}
).

•

Player 1
first samples uniformly
N
bits from the provided sequence. Based on
k
– the number of
\blacksquare
-s – she determines
p^{\prime*}_{k}\in[0,1]
, and bets in the following way:

–

places her capitals
p^{\prime*}_{k}
portion to A

–

places her capitals
1-p^{\prime*}_{k}
portion to B

The parameters
(P^{*},\{p^{\prime*}_{k}\})
can be determined from the parameters of the game
(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
:

p_{k}(A)=\frac{\binom{K_{A}}{k}\binom{M-K_{A}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}},\quad p_{k}(B)=\frac{\binom{K_{B}}{k}\binom{M-K_{B}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}}

(126)

p^{\prime*}_{k}=\frac{P^{*}\ p_{k}(A)}{P^{*}\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P^{*})\ p_{k}(B)}

(127)

while
P^{*}
is the unique minimum of the growth rate difference:

\begin{split}\Delta G(P)=&P\ \sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{A}}p_{k}(A)\log\left(\frac{P\ p_{k}(A)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}\right)+\\
&(1-P)\ \sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{B}}p_{k}(B)\log\left(\frac{(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}\right)\end{split}

(128)

∎

For the proof, see Appendix
B
.

3.7
Numerical approximations of
P^{*}

In this section, we list a few numerical algorithms by which the value of
P^{*}
can be approximated.

3.7.1
Controlled approximations

Bisection method:

Directly using the Bisection method
[
148
]
for
\Delta G^{\prime}(P)
(
120
).

Interval iteration:

Applying
F(.)
(from equation (
266
)) iteratively starting from the whole
\mathbb{R}
number line:

\mathbb{F}_{n+1}=F(\mathbb{F}_{n}),\quad\mathbb{F}_{0}=\mathbb{R}

(129)

Works well if
\mathbb{K}_{A}=\mathbb{K}_{B}
, because in this case:

\mathbb{F}_{1}=[-D_{KL}(\underline{p}(A)||\underline{p}(B),D_{KL}(\underline{p}(B)||\underline{p}(A)]

(130)

every iteration provides a shrinking exact interval for
\vartheta^{*}
, because
F(.)
is a contraction on the log-odds space.

Remark
.

If
\mathbb{K}_{A}\neq\mathbb{K}_{B}
, then a modified variable and contraction can be introduced:

Z_{A}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}p_{k}(A),\quad Z_{B}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}p_{k}(B)

(131)

\chi=\sqrt{\frac{Z_{A}}{Z_{B}}}\log(P)-\sqrt{\frac{Z_{B}}{Z_{A}}}\log(1-P)

(132)

For which a modified iteration can be defined:

\invbreve{F}(\chi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{A}Z_{B}}}\left(\Delta\invbreve{H}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}(p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B))\log(P(\chi)p_{k}(A)+(1-P(\chi)p_{k}(B)))\right)

(133)

\Delta\invbreve{H}=\invbreve{H}_{A}-\invbreve{H}_{B},\quad\invbreve{H}_{\theta}=-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}p_{k}(\theta)\log(p_{k}(\theta))

(134)

\chi^{*}=\invbreve{F}(\chi^{*})

(135)

\invbreve{F}(\mathbb{R})=[-\invbreve{D}_{KL}(\underline{p}(A)||\underline{p}(B)),\invbreve{D}_{KL}(\underline{p}(B)||\underline{p}(A))]

(136)

\invbreve{D}_{KL}(\underline{p}(A)||\underline{p}(B))=\frac{\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}p_{k}(A)\log\left(\frac{p_{k}(A)}{p_{k}(B)}\right)}{\sqrt{\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}p_{k}(A)}\sqrt{\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}p_{k}(B)}}

(137)

∎

Conjecture 3.1
.

\invbreve{F}(\chi)
is a contraction:

|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|\leq\invbreve{q},\quad\invbreve{q}<1

(138)

∎

See the proof attempt in appendix
G
.

Fixed point iteration with error estimation:

Standard iteration of
F(.)
starting from zero:

\vartheta_{n+1}=F(\vartheta_{n}),\quad\vartheta_{0}=0

(139)

and estimating the error
[
147
]
:

|\vartheta^{*}-\vartheta_{n}|\leq\frac{|\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n-1}|}{1-q}

(140)

where equation (
269
) gives an upper bound for
q
.

Controlled Newton–Raphson method:

Newton–Raphson method
[
148
]
for
\Delta(\vartheta)=\vartheta-F(\vartheta)
, for which the error can be estimated by knowing that

|F^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\leq\frac{1}{2}q=Q

(141)

The iteration has the following explicit form:

\vartheta_{n+1}=\frac{F(\vartheta_{n})-\vartheta_{n}F^{\prime}(\vartheta_{n})}{1-F^{\prime}(\vartheta_{n})}

(142)

If we reach a point where
2Q|\vartheta_{n}-F(\vartheta_{n})|\leq\left(1-F^{\prime}(\vartheta_{n})\right)^{2}
, then the following bounds hold for the exact fixed point
\vartheta^{*}
:

\vartheta^{*}-\vartheta_{n}\geq\frac{1-F^{\prime}(\vartheta_{n})}{Q}\left(\sqrt{1-2Q\frac{|\vartheta_{n}-F(\vartheta_{n})|}{\left(1-F^{\prime}(\vartheta_{n})\right)^{2}}}-1\right)

(143)

\vartheta^{*}-\vartheta_{n}\leq\frac{1-F^{\prime}(\vartheta_{n})}{Q}\left(\sqrt{1+2Q\frac{|\vartheta_{n}-F(\vartheta_{n})|}{\left(1-F^{\prime}(\vartheta_{n})\right)^{2}}}-1\right)

(144)

3.7.2
Monte Carlo Methods

MCMC:

Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)
[
35
]
can be combined with Blahut–Arimoto algorithm
[
104
,
105
]
, which with some modifications might yield a general and scalable stochastic approximation for the equilibrium prior.

3.2
.

the controlled numerical methods listed in Section
3.7
.

(147)
