3.9
Interpretation of the results

The construction and the results are in some ways, similar to the Reference prior
[
13
,
88
]
. However, we will see that in the
N\to\infty
limit,
P^{*}_{N}
seems to converge to a different limit prior than the Reference prior.
This is because of the slight difference in the objective of the optimization problem.

•

The Reference prior is related to the mutual information
I(\Pi;X)
,

•

while the equilibrium prior is related to conditional entropy
H(\Pi|X)
.

Remark
.

The “Prior” depends on the game,
P^{*}_{N}\neq P^{*}_{N+1}
even if other parameters of the game (
K_{A},K_{B},M
) are kept the same.

∎

Remark
.

Exchangeability (central to de Finetti
[
48
]
) is emerging by the uniform randomization of sequences and sampling, both by
Player 1
and
Player 2
.

∎

4
Limiting cases

4.1
Binomial games

4.1.1
Binomial Fisher games

There is an important limit case, when
M\to\infty
, while
K_{A}/M\to x_{A}\in(0,1)
and
K_{B}/M\to x_{B}\in(0,1)

33
33

33

x_{A},x_{B}
are similar fractions as mole fractions in
chemistry

[
81
]
.

This sequence of games will be denoted by:

\lim_{\begin{smallmatrix}M\to\infty&\\
K_{A}/M\to x_{A}\\
K_{B}/M\to x_{B}\end{smallmatrix}}{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)=\overline{{\swabfamily Game}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(148)

This limit is hopefully intuitive; however, because of the importance of the construction, it might be useful to specify the meaning of this triple limit.

In technical terms,
\overline{{\swabfamily Game}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
is the equivalence class of ‘‘convergent’’ infinite sequences of the form
34
34

34

the construction is in some way parallel to the construction of real numbers as a collection of equivalent Cauchy sequences of rational numbers
\mathbb{R}=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}

[
184
]
:

{\swabfamily G}_{i}={\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A,i},K_{B,i},M_{i})

(149)

\begin{split}\{{\swabfamily G}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\in\overline{{\swabfamily Game}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})\iff&\lim_{i\to\infty}M_{i}=\infty\ \wedge\\
&\lim_{i\to\infty}K_{A,i}/M_{i}=x_{A}\ \wedge\\
&\lim_{i\to\infty}K_{B,i}/M_{i}=x_{B}\end{split}

(150)

One particular choice of this kind of sequence might be:

\{{\swabfamily Game}(N,[x_{A}\ i],[x_{B}\ i],i)\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\in\overline{{\swabfamily Game}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(151)

Or if both parameters are rational:
x_{A}=a_{A}/b_{A}
,
x_{B}=a_{B}/b_{B}
,
a_{\theta},b_{\theta}\in\mathbb{N}

\{{\swabfamily Game}(N,i\ (a_{A}\ b_{B}),i\ (a_{B}\ b_{A}),i\ (b_{A}\ b_{B}))\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\in\overline{{\swabfamily Game}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(152)

Remark
.

The concept of simultaneous approximation of two real numbers (
x_{A},x_{B}
) with rationals having the same denominator appears in the Littlewood Conjecture
[
144
]

∎

Intuitively, one can expect that in this sequence, some aspects of the symmetric solution will “diverge”, while other aspects will “converge” to a specific strategy.

Concretely, for
Player 2
, the number of sequences for a given scenario undergo a combinatorial explosion, while her mixing between scenarios might converge to a definite distribution.

For
Player 1
, the sampling part of the action ‘‘diverges’’ (undergoes a combinatorial explosion), while her policy will ‘‘converge’’ to a definite policy.
35
35

35

To define a proper concept of convergence, we need to define topology or a metric on the space of policies. Without providing a general definition, in our case we will combine
(k^{*},\nu^{*})
to one single variable
s^{*}=(k^{*}+\nu^{*})/(N+1)\in[0,1]
, and associate the standard topology and metric to this interval.

Theorem 4.1

(Symmetrical equilibrium of Binomial Fisher games)
.

\overline{{\swabfamily Game}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
, where
x_{A},x_{B}\in(0,1)
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, has converging policy for
Player 1
:

•

Player 1
samples
N
bits uniformly from the provided (infinite) sequence. Based on
k
– the number of
\blacksquare
-s – she performs the following action:

–

if
k<k^{*}
she guesses A

–

if
k=k^{*}
then

*

she guesses A with probability
\nu^{*}
or B with probability
1-\nu^{*}

–

if
k>k^{*}
she guesses B

Player 2
might have a convergent policy:

•

Player 2
’s probability of choosing scenario A or B converges to
P^{*}
and
1-P^{*}
if
(x_{A},x_{B})\notin\mathbb{S}_{N}
, and has an accumulation point in the interval
[\underline{P}^{*},\overline{P}^{*}]
if
(x_{A},x_{B})\in\mathbb{S}_{N}
;

–

after choosing a scenario, she picks a sequence uniformly from the allowed sequences for scenario A or B.

The parameters
(k^{*},P^{*},\nu^{*})
or
(k^{*},\underline{P}^{*},\overline{P}^{*},\nu^{*})
can be determined from the parameters of the game
(N,x_{A},x_{B})
:

p_{k}(A)=\binom{N}{k}x_{A}^{k}(1-x_{A})^{N-k},\quad p_{k}(B)=\binom{N}{k}x_{B}^{k}(1-x_{B})^{N-k}

(153)

\nu^{*}=\frac{\sum_{k\geq k^{*}}p_{k}(B)-\sum_{k<k^{*}}p_{k}(A)}{p_{k^{*}}(A)+p_{k^{*}}(B)}

(154)

k^{*}
is the smallest integer, for which the sum of probabilities becomes greater than
1
:

\sum_{k\leq k^{*}}p_{k}(A)+p_{k}(B)>1,\quad\mathrm{while}\quad\sum_{k<k^{*}}p_{k}(A)+p_{k}(B)\leq 1

(155)

The definition of
\mathbb{S}_{N}
is the following:

\mathbb{S}_{N}=\{(x_{A},x_{B})\ |\ \nu^{*}(x_{A},x_{B})=0\}

(156)

P^{*}=\frac{p_{k^{*}}(B)}{p_{k^{*}}(A)+p_{k^{*}}(B)},\quad\text{if }(x_{A},x_{B})\notin\mathbb{S}_{N}

(157)

And if
(x_{A},x_{B})\in\mathbb{S}_{N}

\underline{P}^{*}=\frac{p_{k^{*}-1}(B)}{p_{k^{*}-1}(A)+p_{k^{*}-1}(B)},\quad\overline{P}^{*}=\frac{p_{k^{*}}(B)}{p_{k^{*}}(A)+p_{k^{*}}(B)}

(158)

∎

See Appendix
C
for the build-up of the proof, or see the specific proof in
C.1
.

Remark
.

The function
P^{*}(x_{A},x_{B})
has discontinuities or “scars” on
\mathbb{S}_{N}
. At these points, the function can jump, but any value between the lower and upper bounds
P^{*}\in[\underline{P}^{*}(x_{A},x_{B}),\overline{P}^{*}(x_{A},x_{B})]
could be chosen by
Player 2
. This technically means that for all such
P^{*}
, the maximal expected winning rate for
Player 1
converges to the same
v^{*}
value.

Remarkably, this does not impact the convergence of
Player 1
’s strategy, expressed with
s^{*}(x_{A},x_{B})
, which is a continuous function in the limit.

(a)

N=2

(b)

N=5

Figure 22
:
Scars,
\mathbb{S}_{N}\subset[0,1]\times[0,1]
for
N=2
and
N=5
.

∎

Remark
.

The equilibrium quantity
k^{*}
can be interpreted as the median
[
170
,
15
]
of a mixture of random variables
\kappa_{A},\kappa_{B}
:

k^{*}=\mathbbm{m}\left[\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{A}+\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{B}\right]

(159)

where
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{B}
are characterized by
p_{k}(A)
and
p_{k}(B)
.
(The only difference is that such definition would imply the
\nu\in(0,1]
choice instead of
\nu\in[0,1)
.)

∎

The intermediacy property of the median implies the following bounds for
k^{*}
:

Theorem 4.2
.

\lfloor x_{A}N\rfloor\leq\mathbbm{m}[\kappa_{A}]\leq k^{*}\leq\mathbbm{m}[\kappa_{B}]\leq\lceil x_{B}N\rceil

(160)

where
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{B}
represent random variables with Binomial distribution:

\kappa_{A}\sim\mathrm{Binom}(N,x_{A}),\quad\kappa_{B}\sim\mathrm{Binom}(N,x_{B})

(161)

∎

Proof.

The statement follows from lemma
2.1
, and bounds obtained for the median of the Binomial distribution in
[
91
]
:

\lfloor xN\rfloor\leq\mathbbm{m}[\kappa]\leq\lceil xN\rceil,\quad\kappa\sim\mathrm{Binom}(N,x)

(162)

∎

4.1.2
Binomial Bayesian games

One can define Binomial Bayesian games
\overline{{\swabfamily BGame}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
with the same triple limit,
and state an analogous theorem:

Theorem 4.3

(Binomial Bayesian equilibrium)
.

\overline{{\swabfamily BGame}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
, where
x_{A},x_{B}\in(0,1)
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, has converging policies:

•

Player 2
chooses scenario A or B with probability
P^{*}
and
1-P^{*}
;

–

then picks a sequence uniformly from the allowed sequences for scenario A or B.

•

Player 1
samples
N
bits uniformly from the provided (infinite) sequence. Based on
k
– the number of
\blacksquare
-s – she determines
p^{\prime*}_{k}\in(0,1)
, and bets in the following way:

–

places her capitals
p^{\prime*}_{k}
portion to A

–

places her capitals
1-p^{\prime*}_{k}
portion to B

The parameters
(P^{*},\{p^{\prime*}_{k}\})
can be determined from the parameters of the game
(N,x_{A},x_{B})
:

p_{k}(A)=\binom{N}{k}x_{A}^{k}(1-x_{A})^{N-k},\quad p_{k}(B)=\binom{N}{k}x_{B}^{k}(1-x_{B})^{N-k}

(163)

p^{\prime*}_{k}=\frac{P^{*}\ p_{k}(A)}{P^{*}\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P^{*})\ p_{k}(B)}

(164)

while
P^{*}
is the unique minimum of the growth rate difference:

\begin{split}\Delta G(P)=&P\ \sum_{k}p_{k}(A)\log\left(\frac{P\ p_{k}(A)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}\right)+\\
&(1-P)\ \sum_{k}p_{k}(B)\log\left(\frac{(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}\right)\end{split}

(165)

∎

See Section
C.2
in Appendix
C
for the build-up of the proof, or see the specific proof at
C.2
.

Remark
.

All equilibrium quantities are continuous as a function of
x_{A},x_{B}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<x_{A}\leq x_{B}<1
.

∎

Examples show continuous limiting policy plots
s^{*}(x_{A},x_{B})
in fig.

4.2.3
Interpretation

Emergent probabilities:

Here, I want to emphasize that the distributions appearing in the construction
p_{k}(A)
and
p_{k}(B)
do not represent independently introduced “stochastic” variables.
The imagined distribution of the number of
\blacksquare
-s in the sample emerges from the joint mixing (or randomization) of
Player 1
and
Player 2
.

In the limit
M\to\infty
, sampling and sequence choosing strategies do not converge to a proper distribution, while the imagined distributions of
k
do converge.
This is why the emergent binomial distributions
p_{k}(A)
and
p_{k}(B)
remain a useful part of the description of the limit case but are fundamentally only a consequence of the mixed strategies of both players.

“The importance of randomization in applied statistics can scarcely
be exaggerated. From the personalistic viewpoint it is one of the most
important ways to bring groups of people into virtual unanimity; from
the objectivistic viewpoint it not only makes possible great reductions
in maximum loss, but it is seen as an invention by which the theory of
probability is brought to bear on situations to which probability on
first (objectivistic) sight would seem irrelevant.”

[
169
]

4.3
Limiting policies for
N\to\infty

4.3.1
Fisher policy limit

Notation:

x_{0}^{*}(x_{A},x_{B})=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1-x_{A}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}{\log\left(\frac{(1-x_{A})x_{B}}{(1-x_{B})x_{A}}\right)}

(166)

Theorem 4.4

(Binomial Fisher limiting policy)
.

For any
0<x_{A}<x_{B}<1
the equilibrium policy of
Player 1
in a Binomial Fisher game converges to:

\lim_{N\to\infty}s^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})=s^{*}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})=x_{0}^{*}(x_{A},x_{B})

(167)

meaning that for fixed
x_{A},x_{B}
:

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{k^{*}_{N}+\nu^{*}_{N}}{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{k^{*}_{N}}{N}=s^{*}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}=x_{0}^{*}

(168)

∎

The proof will be presented in Appendix
D
.

Remark
.

Because of Theorem
2.2
, we can easily get upper and lower bounds for
s^{*}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}
:

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\lfloor Nx_{A}\rfloor}{N}=x_{A}\leq s^{*}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})\leq x_{B}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\lceil Nx_{B}\rceil}{N}

(169)

∎

4.3.2
Binomial Fisher limiting prior bounds

Conjecture 4.1

(Binomial Fisher limiting prior bounds)
.

The prior of Binomial Fisher games does not converge as
N\to\infty
, but it has finite upper and lower bounds in the limit:

\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}P^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})=\overline{P}^{*}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})

(170)

\liminf\limits_{N\to\infty}P^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})=\underline{P}^{*}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})

(171)

The conjectured explicit expression for the bounds can be found in equation
401
and
402
.

∎

A derivation supporting this conjecture is presented in Appendix
D
.

4.3.3
Binomial Bayesian limiting prior

Notation:

A phase factor can be introduced to characterize the asymptotic behaviour of equilibrium quantities:

\varphi=2\pi Nx_{0}^{*}\mod 2\pi

(172)

Conjecture 4.2

(Binomial Bayesian limiting prior)
.

For a general
\overline{{\swabfamily BGame}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
the prior
P^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})
converges to a periodic function
P^{*,\varphi}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}
, depending on the phase factor
\varphi=\varphi_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})
as
N\to\infty
:

P^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})=P^{*,\varphi_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})+\mathcal{O}(1/N)

(173)

The implicit expression for the conjectured asymptotic log-odds
\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}
can be found in equations (
572
), (
573
), (
574
).

∎

A derivation to support this conjecture is presented in Appendix
D
.

Remark

(Binomial Bayesian limiting prior approximation)
.

There is a finite

P^{\approx}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}
for
\overline{{\swabfamily BGame}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
, which is a “good approximation” of
P^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})
as
N\to\infty
:

P^{\approx}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})=\frac{\log\left(\frac{(1-x_{0}^{*})x_{B}}{(1-x_{B})x_{0}^{*}}\right)}{\log\left(\frac{(1-x_{A})x_{B}}{(1-x_{B})x_{A}}\right)},\quad x_{0}^{*}(x_{A},x_{B})=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1-x_{A}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}{\log\left(\frac{(1-x_{A})x_{B}}{(1-x_{B})x_{A}}\right)}

(174)

∎

A derivation which motivates this approximation can be found in Appendix
D
.

36
36

36

[

]
, resulting an apparent wave with relatively long wave length in
N
.

The plotted values are the results of controlled approximations. Therefore, the oscillation is not caused by numerical errors.

All other examples seemingly converge slowly but steadily to values, which can be approximated well with the Binomial Bayesian limiting prior approximation
P^{\approx}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})
.
Qualitatively, the behaviour is consistent with the conjectured periodic Binomial Bayesian limiting prior asymptotics
P^{*,\varphi}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})
.

First-order asymptotics:

Conjecture 4.3
.

The Binomial Bayesian prior has the following asymptotic expansion for any
0<x_{A}<x_{B}<1
:

\vartheta^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})=\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})+\frac{1}{N}\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}^{\varphi}(x_{A},x_{B})+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)

(175)

where
\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}^{\varphi}(x_{A},x_{B})
37
37

37

, sampi “like pi” is an archaic letter of the Greek alphabet
[
84
]
. Originally, it might stand for a sibilant sound, probably [ss] or [ts]. It also represents
900
among the Milesian numerals
[
70
,
63
]
. The symbol is part of UTF-8
character encoding

[
31
]

U+03E1
, and can be accessed in mathematical software such as
Wolfram Mathematic

[
159
]
(the form used in this text is generated by
Mathematica
, using the
wasy10
Font Family)
can be defined explicitly by knowing
\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}
.
The explicit definition can be found in equation (
602
).

∎

(178)

5
Unification through relative risk aversion

5.1
Description of the general Statistical game

Definition 5.1

(Statistical game)
.

There are two players,
Player 1
and
Player 2
.

Player 2
needs to choose between scenario A or B first and then produce a binary sequence of length
M
containing precisely
K_{A}
or
K_{B}
number of
1
-s. (Without losing generality, we will assume
K_{A}\leq K_{B}
.)
Following this,
Player 1
(not knowing the actions of
Player 2
) can sample
N
number of bits. After observing their value, she determines what portion of her capital
p^{\prime}
she places on scenario A (while the other
1-p^{\prime}
portion is placed on scenario B).

The portion
Player 1
places on the scenario, chosen by
Player 2
, will be doubled, while the other part of her capital will be lost.
For this game, we will assume that
Player 1
has an isoelastic utility function:

u_{\gamma}(c)=\frac{c^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(179)

with relative risk aversion parameter
\gamma>0,\gamma\neq 1
.
Furthermore, we will assume that
Player 1
and
Player 2
are playing a zero-sum game
38
38

38

zero-sum in utilities (not in capital)
.

The above defined Statistical Game will be denoted as

{\swabfamily SGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M,\gamma)
.

∎

Remark
.

The crucial difference between the definition of Bayesian games
3.2
and Statistical games
5.1
is the utility function for
Player 1
.
It is logarithmic for Bayesian games
{\swabfamily BGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
and isoelastic with relative risk aversion parameter
\gamma
for Statistical games
{\swabfamily SGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M,\gamma)
.

∎

5.2
Simplest nontrivial example

The simplest statistically nontrivial general Statistical game is
{\swabfamily SGame}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2,\gamma)
.
To find its equilibrium solution, we can introduce very similar notation and go through analogous steps to Section
3.4
.

Expected utility:

The expected utility for
Player 1
looks the following in the notation borrowed from Section
3.4
:

\begin{split}U_{\gamma}=P&\left(q\ u_{\gamma}(p^{\prime}_{(1,\square)})+(1-q)u_{\gamma}(p^{\prime}_{(2,\square)})\right)+\\
(1-P)r&\left(q\ u_{\gamma}(1-p^{\prime}_{(1,\square)})+(1-q)u_{\gamma}(1-p^{\prime}_{(2,\blacksquare)})\right)+\\
(1-P)(1-r)&\left(q\ u_{\gamma}(1-p^{\prime}_{(1,\blacksquare)})+(1-q)u_{\gamma}(1-p^{\prime}_{(2,\square)}\right)\end{split}

(180)

Equilibrium parameters:

Following the steps analogous to the derivation in Section
3.4
, we get:

p^{\prime}_{(1,\blacksquare)}=p^{\prime}_{(2,\blacksquare)}=p^{\prime*}_{1}=0,

(181)

p^{\prime*}_{(1,\square)}=\frac{P^{1/\gamma}}{P^{1/\gamma}+((1-P)r)^{1/\gamma}},\quad p^{\prime*}_{(2,\square)}=\frac{P^{1/\gamma}}{P^{1/\gamma}+((1-P)(1-r))^{1/\gamma}}

(182)

r^{*}=1/2,\quad q^{*}=1/2

(183)

Finding
P^{*}_{\gamma}
:

Substituting back to equation (
180
), we get the following expression for the expected utility as a function of
P
:

U_{\gamma}(P)=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\left(P\left(\frac{P^{(1-\gamma)/\gamma}}{(P^{1/\gamma}+((1-P)/2)^{1/\gamma})^{1-\gamma}}-1\right)+\frac{(1-P)}{2}\left(\frac{((1-P)/2)^{1/\gamma})}{(P^{1/\gamma}+((1-P)/2)^{1/\gamma})^{1-\gamma}}-1\right)\right)

(184)

Figure 37
:
Expected utility for
{\swabfamily SGame}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)
as a function of
P
for different
\gamma
(relative risk aversion) values.
(Vertical gridlines are placed at
1/3
and
1/\sqrt{5}
values.)

P^{*}_{\gamma}
is defined as the value, where
U_{\gamma}(P)
takes its minimum.
We can take the derivative with respect to
P
, and find the root of this expression. For general
\gamma\neq 1
parameters this gives an explicit, but transcendental equation for
P^{*}_{\gamma}
:

\left(Q(2P)^{1/\gamma}-PQ^{1/\gamma}\right)\left(P^{1/\gamma}+(Q/2)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{\gamma}=\frac{PQ}{2}\left((2P)^{1/\gamma}+Q^{1/\gamma}\right),\quad Q=1-P,\quad P=P^{*}_{\gamma}

(185)

In general, equation (
185
) has no closed-form solution, but it can be effectively solved numerically.
Numerically calculated values are plotted in figures
40
,
41(a)
and
41(b)
in Section
5.6.3
.

Remark
.

There are a few special relative risk aversion values
\gamma
, for which equation (
185
) has a closed form solution:

P^{*}_{\gamma=1/2}=2/5,\quad P^{*}_{\gamma=2}=1/2,\quad P^{*}_{\gamma=3}=(3+\sqrt[4]{12}-\sqrt{3})/6

(186)

∎

Remark
.

Based on numerical evidence and explicit calculation, we can determine three important limit cases for
P^{*}_{\gamma}
:

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}P^{*}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{3},\quad\lim_{\gamma\to 1}P^{*}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}},\quad\lim_{\gamma\to\infty}P^{*}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}

(187)

∎

Equilibrium splitting ratios:

Based on the numerically calculated
P^{*}_{\gamma}
, and equation (
182
), we can numerically determine the equilibrium splitting ratios
p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}
.
The results of numerical calculations are shown in figure
38
.

Figure 38
:
Splitting strategies,
p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}
for
{\swabfamily SGame}(N=1,K_{A}=0,K_{B}=1,M=2)
as a function of
\gamma
(relative risk aversion parameter). Horizontal gridlines are placed at
\{2/3,(\sqrt{5}-1)/2,1/2\}
.

5.3
General Statistical games

5.3.1
Main theorem on Statistical games

Theorem 5.1

(Isoelastic equilibrium)
.

{\swabfamily SGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M,\gamma)
has a unique Nash equilibrium, in which:

•

Player 2
chooses scenario A or B with probability
P^{*}_{\gamma}
and
1-P^{*}_{\gamma}
;

–

then picks an allowed sequence with equal probability (from
\mathcal{K}_{A}
or
\mathcal{K}_{B}
).

•

Player 1
first samples uniformly
N
bits from the provided sequence. Based on
k
– the number of
\blacksquare
-s – she determines
p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}\in[0,1]
, and bets in the following way:

–

places her capitals
p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}
portion to A

–

places her capitals
1-p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}
portion to B

The parameters
(P^{*}_{\gamma},\{p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}\})
can be determined from the parameters of the game
(N,K_{A},K_{B},M,\gamma)
:

p_{k}(A)=\frac{\binom{K_{A}}{k}\binom{M-K_{A}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}},\quad p_{k}(B)=\frac{\binom{K_{B}}{k}\binom{M-K_{B}}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}}

(188)

p^{\prime}_{k}(P)=\frac{\left(P\ p_{k}(A)\right)^{1/\gamma}}{\left(P\ p_{k}(A)\right)^{1/\gamma}+\left((1-P)\ p_{k}(B)\right)^{1/\gamma}}

(189)

p^{\prime*}_{k}=p^{\prime}_{k}(P^{*}_{\gamma})

(190)

while
P^{*}_{\gamma}
is the unique minimum of the expected utility:

U_{\gamma}(P)=P\ \left(\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)u_{\gamma}(p^{\prime}_{k}(P))\right)+(1-P)\left(\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)u_{\gamma}(1-p^{\prime}_{k}(P))\right)

(191)

where the utility function for a given relative risk aversion
\gamma>0,\gamma\neq 1
is:

u_{\gamma}(c)=\frac{c^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(192)

∎

For the proof, see Appendix
F
.

5.4
Examples and Visualization

Figure 39
:
Splitting strategies,
p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}
for
{\swabfamily SGame}(N=4,K_{A}=5,K_{B}=8,M=14)
as a function of
\gamma
(relative risk aversion parameter). Horizontal gridlines are placed at
\{1/2,14/39\approx 0.359\}
.

5.5
Unification of Fisher and Statistical games

Theorem 5.2
.

The equilibrium strategies of a Statistical game
{\swabfamily SGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M,\gamma)
in the
\gamma\to 0
limit can be mapped to the symmetric equilibrium strategies of a Fisher game
{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)

39
39

39

if
\nu^{*}\neq 0
with the following identification:

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k^{*}}=\nu^{*}

(193)

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}P^{*}_{\gamma}=P^{*}_{0}

(194)

Where
(P^{*}_{\gamma},\{p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}\})
are the equilibrium parameters of

{\swabfamily SGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M,\gamma)
,
while
(k^{*},\nu^{*},P^{*}_{0})
are the equilibrium parameters of

{\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M)
.

∎

For the proof, see Appendix
F
.

5.6
Unification by generalized entropies

5.6.1
Expected Utility-based entropy

Generalized entropy for probability vectors:

\text{
{\vphantom{$H$}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$H$\cr\kern 1.00006pt\vrule height=5.21387pt,depth=-4.7833pt,width=7.3pt\cr}}}}_{\gamma}(\underline{p})=H^{\mathrm{EU}}_{\gamma}(\underline{p})=\frac{1-\left(\sum_{k}p_{k}^{1/\gamma}\right)^{\gamma}}{1-\gamma}=\frac{1-||\underline{p}||_{1/\gamma}}{1-\gamma}

(195)

Generalized conditional entropy:

H^{\mathrm{EU}}_{\gamma}[Y|X]=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim X}H^{\mathrm{EU}}_{\gamma}[Y|X=x]

(196)

Generalized entropy and Expected Utility:

The expected utility in eq. (
191
) can be expressed by the generalized entropy in the following way:

U_{\gamma}(P)=-H^{\mathrm{EU}}_{\gamma}[\Pi|X]

(197)

5.6.2
Connection with other generalized entropies

•

Connection with Rényi
[
155
,
1
]
and Tsallis
[
191
,
125
,
168
]
entropy

–

The order parameter
\alpha
and deformation parameter
q
, can be identified with
1/\gamma

•

In the limit
\gamma\to 0
, all 3 entropies give the same equilibrium strategy, meaning all could unify Bayesian and “Frequentist” statistics.

•

However, the proposed entropy (
195
) can be derived from expected utility theory with an isoelastic utility function. Its parameter has an intuitive interpretation, namely
\gamma
representing the relative risk aversion of the deciding agent.

–

Brave or reckless as
\gamma\to 0
, balanced when
\gamma\to 1
, and cautious, shy or anxious as
\gamma\to\infty

Rényi entropy:

[
155
]

H_{\alpha}(\underline{p})=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\left(\sum_{k}p_{k}^{\alpha}\right)

(198)

Conditional Rényi entropy:

[
186
]

H_{\alpha}(Y|X)=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim X}H_{\alpha}(Y|X=x)

(199)

Tsallis entropy:

[
191
]

S_{q}(\underline{p})=\frac{\sum_{k}p_{k}^{q}-1}{1-q}

(200)

Conditional Tsallis entropy:

[
125
,
112
]

S_{q}(Y|X)=\frac{S_{q}(X,Y)-S_{q}(X)}{1+(1-q)S_{q}(X)}

(201)

Connecting with
\gamma
parameter:

H^{\mathrm{R}}_{\gamma}(\underline{p})=H_{\alpha=1/\gamma}(\underline{p}),\quad H^{\mathrm{T}}_{\gamma}(\underline{p})=S_{q=1/\gamma}(\underline{p})

(202)

H^{\mathrm{R}}_{\gamma}(Y|X)=H_{\alpha=1/\gamma}(Y|X),\quad H^{\mathrm{T}}_{\gamma}(Y|X)=S_{q=1/\gamma}(Y|X)

(203)

5.7
Generalized quantities

Generalized divergence:

D_{\mathrm{EU}}^{\gamma}(\underline{p}||\underline{q})=\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\sum_{k}q_{k}\left(\frac{p_{k}}{q_{k}}\right)^{1/\gamma}\right)=\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\sum_{k}p_{k}\left(\frac{p_{k}}{q_{k}}\right)^{1/\gamma-1}\right)

(204)

Using this divergence, we can make an analogous statement to equation (
130
), for
\Phi(.)
(defined in (
721
), (
722
)):

\mathbb{G}_{n+1}=\Phi(\mathbb{G}_{n}),\quad\mathbb{G}_{0}=\mathbb{R}

(205)

\mathbb{G}_{1}=[-D_{\mathrm{EU}}^{\gamma}(\underline{p}(A)||\underline{p}(B)),D_{\mathrm{EU}}^{\gamma}(\underline{p}(B)||\underline{p}(A))]

(206)

Direct calculation shows that:

\lim_{\gamma\to 1}D_{\mathrm{EU}}^{\gamma}(\underline{p}||\underline{q})=D_{KL}(\underline{p}||\underline{q})

(207)

Remark
.

This expected utility-based divergence is related to the Rényi divergence
[
45
,
155
]

D_{\mathrm{EU}}^{\gamma}(\underline{p}||\underline{q})=D_{\alpha=1/\gamma}(\underline{p}||\underline{q})

(208)

∎

6
Game theoretical framework for statistics

6.1
Motivation

“To us, probability is the very guide of life.”
[
24
]

However, the exact interpretation of the concept is still debated among statisticians, philosophers, economists, and various other practitioners who wish to live by its guidance.
Probability theory and statistics are tremendously successful disciplines, providing a plethora of useful tools both for theoretical research and real-world problems.
Nevertheless, for many students and professionals, some techniques or the interpretation of results might cause unease because of the lack of a unified, coherent structure.

This work attempts to take a step back and look at the discipline with a fresh eyes.
It aims to identify the tasks in which probabilistic and statistical concepts might arise and approach these problems with a broader scope.
Hopefully, this more general approach will provide a philosophically simpler and more coherent framework in which most of the successful techniques and concepts of statistics can be derived and interpreted.
The present work collects the first and simplest building blocks and begins the construction of such a framework. It can hopefully provide tools in the form of concepts, formulas, theorems, computable algorithms, controlled approximations and executable code to empower those who genuinely care about making decisions in the face of uncertainty.

(For a more detailed and personal narrative about the questions which motivated this work, see Appendix
H
.)

6.2
Scope

6.2.1
Scope of the problem

The present work attempts to discuss a more general problem than statistics itself, which is decision-making in the face of uncertainty.
The pressure of acting without being able to collect all necessary information is a fundamental part of any organism. Therefore, mechanisms and heuristics to collect and process data and form actionable strategies accordingly are more ancient than abstract thinking and reasoning.
[
27
]

Historically, many important problems and concepts of such decision problems came from economics, which can be viewed as the science and art of making choices under scarcity
40
40

40

and information can be a very real resource which is usually not fully available
.
In the first half of the 20
th
century, various kinds of uncertainties were discussed in foundational works by Knight
[
100
]
and Keynes
[
98
]
.
The concept of risk (where we can in some way associate probabilities to possibilities), is central in modern economics. However, the concept of Knightian uncertainty or ambiguity (in which case only the set of possibilities is known, but we have no further information which might help to associate probabilities to them) seems to fell out of favour
[
102
]
.

It might be possible that there is no universal theory for such fundamentally uncertain situations, and only isolated heuristics can be identified, adopted by various agents.
However, I will argue that a surprisingly general framework can be built by adopting a small set of assumptions.

We have seen in previous sections that constructing a toy model in which decisions under uncertainty can be introduced and analysed does not require the external introduction of probabilities and stochastic variables. All these concepts can be born out from the setup of the dilemma (modelled by a game with deterministic rules) and are interpretable as instrumental intermediate concepts emerging in the construction of equilibrium strategies.

6.2.2
Scope of this work

For the sake of precise and rigorous mathematical statements, the scope of this present work has been limited to the simplest statistical problems, in which the fundamental concept of uncertainty and the proposed decision-making process can be demonstrated.
Thus the framework presented in the previous sections is far from complete.
There are various directions on how this work could be naturally extended. For an incomplete list of future directions, see Section
7
.

The work builds mainly on the results of mathematical explorations. It can be viewed as a pursuit of coherence and philosophical simplicity rather than a new theory which explains and predicts experimental observations.
41
41

41

Although there are real-world phenomena in the realm of experimental economics, such as Ellsberg paradox
[
44
]
related to Ambiguity aversion
[
111
,
54
]
, which are hard to interpret by applying mainstream interpretations of probability, but could be a natural phenomenon in a game theoretic framework.

A secondary aim of this work was an attempt to collect related concepts from diverse disciplines that might be understood with a refined definition of uncertainty.

Hopefully, this handful of ideas can serve as the foundation for broader joint work, which can restart the serious investigation of the framework and its applications.
From the point of view of developed methods applicable to real-life statistical problems, the current work is a humble achievement; however, hopefully, it can serve as a solid foundation demonstrating the consistency and applicability of the presented game theoretic framework.

6.2.3
Problems outside of scope

To avoid the false impression that the presented refined concept of uncertainty could encompass all unknowns, which real-life agents need to face, I explicitly spell out some limitations of the framework.

A major assumption that has been made is that the agent knows all possibilities of an unknown parameter of her environment. This is never a finite set in the real world when unpredictable, unexpected events can alter the environment and make previously imagined categorisation impossible.

42
42

42

This could be relaxed in a way that we only assume that future consequences will be describable by arbitrary long strings. A future generalisation possibility is addressed in Section
7.10
; however, if rewards could take arbitrary values, then the framework seems to break down and fail to converge.

Another general assumption is that agents know all the utilities for all possible future consequences. This is not realistic, both for complex or numerous consequences.
In particular, this means, for example that we assume that the agent’s utilities do not change as new information has been gathered, actions have been made, or simply time elapsed.
There are numerous cases where these assumptions do not hold.

6.3
Proposed framework

The proposed framework is fundamentally built on Game Theory
[
97
,
117
,
32
,
128
]
.
Not because of any ‘‘rationality’’ assumption but on the contrary, because this looks like the most natural framework to model the behaviour of adaptable agents, whose primary goal is to become and stay successful in their environment.
43
43

43

As in evolutionary game theory
[
176
,
188
]
, where we do not assume “rationally” contemplated strategic behaviour from the agents, but a long evolutionary history can produce action patterns
as if
it would come from a game theoretic reasoning.

In this way, this is not primarily a prescriptive framework demanding some logical or other consistency but a partially descriptive theory that tries to draw general conclusions about agents and their behaviour, which successfully achieved their goals.

Experimenter and Nature:

The “Experimenter” (in previous sections called
Player 1
) can face a dilemma, which can be characterized by possible actions, uncertain possible states (or parameter values) of “Nature”, and the consequences of possible action-state pairs.
In the game-theoretic framework, the Experimenter is advised to take the following steps to determine her strategy in this situation:
Evaluate her utilities for all consequences, and form a strategy
as if
, the unknown parameters had been chosen by an animated “Nature” (in previous sections
Player 2
played this role), whose goal is to maximize the Experimenters expected regret
44
44

44

for a given state of “Nature”, and action of Experimenter, the regret of the Experimenter is the difference between the maximal utility which could have been achieved – by the best action for that state – and the actually achieved utility
.
The game theoretic framework suggests the strategy to the Experimenter, which is equivalent to the equilibrium strategy in the previously constructed imaginary game.
[
118
,
197
,
169
]

6.3.1
Normative attitude

The framework does not aim to be prescriptive but suggestive in a way that it is slightly more ambitious than a descriptive theory and dares to suggest a default choice among possible policies.

6.3.2
Alternative frameworks

Acting in realistic environments is inherently an art and not a task which could be carried out by following a formal prescription.
Expected utility theory
[
19
]
is not the only normative framework
[
23
]
. This is important to point out to avoid the possibility of a dogmatic solidification of the theory.

•

Heuristics in the context of bounded “rationality”
[
202
]
, also know as behavioral economics
[
192
,
36
]

•

Imprecise probabilities
[
54
,
53
,
17
]

•

Heuristics in mathematics
[
145
,
58
]

•

Algorithmic probability theory
[
153
,
195
,
126
]

•

Berge equilibrium
[
120
]

•

Change of the definition of the agent, i.e. identity constructions

•

Hierarchical constructions allow different types of opponents, while the type (being an unknown parameter) could be chosen by a “higher level” player.
Similar to Bayesian hierarchical models
[
51
]

•

Yet unknown or unimaginable construction (for example, a formalization of “wise” Optimism)

6.3.3
History of the approach

For historical background see a quote from the textbook
Theoretical statistics
by D.R. Cox and D.V. Hinkley:

“Many of the basic ideas of statistical decision theory were stated by
Neyman and Pearson (1933b). A systematic theory for the situation
when there is no prior distribution was developed by Wald and set out
in a book (Wald, 1950) completed just before his death; this included
a formulation of common statistical problems in decision theory
terms. Important contributions continuing Wald’s work are by
Girshick and Savage (1951) and Stein (1956 and unpublished
lecture notes). A detailed introduction is given by Ferguson (1967).
This work lead to the view, widely held for a period, that all statistical
problems should be formulated in terms of decision making.
von Neumann and Morgenstern developed an axiomatic approach
to subjective utilities in connexion with their work on game theory
(von Neumann and Morgenstern, 1953, 1st ed. 1935); see DeGroot
(1970), Fishburn (1969) and, for an elementary account, Chernoff
and Moses (1959).
In the late 1950’s and early 1960’s theoretical interest shifted from
the situation without prior distributions to Bayesian decision theory
in which personalistic prior distributions and utilities are central to
the argument; an authoritative account was given by Raiffa and
Schlaifer (1961). There are now a number of excellent elementary
introductions (Aitchison, 1970a; Lindley, 1971b; Raiffa, 1968).
There is also an extensive economic, psychological, sociological and
operational research literature on decision making. A review on the
practical measurement of utilities is given by Hull, Moore and Thomas”
[
33
]

7
Future work and extension

7.1
Assumptions about the unknown

When we face uncertainty, there is an important metaphysical question: what assumptions do we make about the unknown?

In this work, we considered only “Balanced games”, where the utility of guessing correctly does not depend on the scenario (A or B).
However, in a general dilemma – where all 4 possible choosing-guessing pairs can have different utilities – different assumptions can lead to different behaviour. In the following I list four different potential metaphysical constructions, each yielding different prototypical games for such a dilemma:

•

鬼
45
45

45

Demon, evil spirit or ghost. For context see
Chinese Character Database
,
Wiktionary
or
[
42
]
:

U_{1,2}=(U_{1},-U_{1})

Assuming that the unknown is a “malevolent demon”, playing a zero-sum game with us.
[
169
,
33
]

•

♓
46
46

46

Pisces, symbolizing Alchemical final transmutation (Projection)
[
72
]
. (The analogy came from my impression that changing an agent’s utility function is hard, such as changing
base metals to gold
, or mental transmutation.)
:

U_{1,2}=(-R_{1},R_{1})

This construction assumes that we can somehow change our utility function to regret
[
97
]
, and then the unknown is involved in a zero-sum game not regarding our utility but regarding our regret.

•

A “wisely optimistic”
[

]
metaphysical construction, which might be based on some kind of relatedness or sharing between the agents, and the forces influencing the uncertain parameters.

: yet unexplored possibilities.

7.2
Target of the Inference

It can be shown that the equilibrium of such statistical games depends on the target of our inference, i.e. the unknown quantities we are interested in.
Regarding this aspect of statistical games, I mention 3 main categories:

•

“Platonian” Inference, focusing and betting on parameters

–

This current work can be viewed as a purely “Platonian” Inference.

–

In the case of a continuous parameter space, further complications can occur, such as a default measure or the “density of states” on the parameter space.

–

the “Platonian” framework seems to be closer to previous works on Objective or Non-informative priors such as the Reference prior
[
13
,
88
]

•

“Aristotelian” Inference, focusing and betting on future observable data

–

For example, the work of R. L. Kashyap
[
94
,
93
]

–

In this framework, not only priors and probabilities can be considered imaginary, but models as well. In this context, a model can be viewed as an abstract construction connecting collected data with potential future data.

–

In some cases, “Platonian” Inference can be viewed as the limiting case of an “Aristotelian” Inference.

•

General Inference: betting on a general Target space.

–

A natural example might be the case when we can only observe indirect data to our interests.

–

A further example might be data compression and the choice of coding protocols.

7.3
Correspondence with Bayesians and Frequentists

The correspondence principle
[
16
,
165
]
, or more specifically, the general correspondence principle
[
16
]
requires that a more general scientific framework has to incorporate a more specific previous framework:

“The most important heuristic restriction is the General Correspondence Principle. Roughly speaking, this is the requirement that any acceptable new theory L should account for the success of its predecessor S by ‘degenerating’ into that theory under those conditions under which S has been well confirmed by tests.”
[
64
]

Decision theory might even provide natural quantitative tools by which theories can be compared. A natural measure of divergence of policies could be the cost for which an agent with different preferences (and thus different optimal policy) is willing to adopt another policy.

•

“Delegation premium” / “Independence tax”

–

How much tax would a gambler be willing to pay for being able to play her own optimal policy instead of adopting another decision-making policy?

•

Policy-(Believes/Preferences/Priors) acceptance matrix

7.4
Effective approximative numerical methods

•

Finding an effective approximative numerical method such as Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)
[
35
]

–

MCMC can be combined with Blahut–Arimoto algorithm
[
104
,
105
]
, which, with some modifications, might yield a general and scalable stochastic approximation for the equilibrium prior.

7.5
Embedding into larger game Contests

In Appendix
E
, we introduced a Contest view (Section
E.4
), and we were able to argue that in this individualistic and competitive framework, the logarithmic utility function is aligned with evolutionary fitness.

However, when we defined Bayesian games, we independently assumed that it is zero-sum.
This assumption could be relaxed by embedding the individual game into a competitive contest, where, among other gamblers, one insider can not only play but influence the model parameters.
Such an extended individualistic and competitive framework might naturally align with Bayesian games and generalise well for unbalanced games.

7.6
Generalizing Game Theory with a player representing uncertainty

If multiple “rational” agents face each other and uncertainty simultaneously, then the uncertain parameters could be modelled by a “regret maximising” player.

A natural question might be: Whose regret ought to be maximised? A somewhat unusual answer might be that all rational players can project their own personal ghosts, maximising their own personal regrets, resulting in their own personal priors for the uncertain parameters.
(Without mentioning these weakly inconsistent priors) this framework has been suggested in the context of Bayesian games and types
[
77
,
76
]
. However, the concept has the potential to be further generalised.

A simple toy example could be a battle situation, where two opposing generals have to plan their strategies, while their positions might be weather-dependent in different way. Suppose the generals have absolutely no information about the uncertain weather. In that case, both may prepare more for different weather conditions, even if they know they will face the same reality on the day of battle.

7.7
Using the concept in Reinforcement Learning

A toy model in Reinforcement Learning
[
162
]
, which could be analysed in depth using this framework, might be a “one-armed bandit” type problem
[
107
]
:

•

The bandit has one stochastic arm, which can give
\{-1,1\}
reward.

–

if it is a type A bandit, we gain
1
with
p_{A}=20\%
chance and
-1
with
1-p_{A}=80\%
chance,

–

if it is a type B bandit, we gain
1
with
p_{B}=80\%
chance and
-1
with
1-p_{B}=20\%
chance.

•

In every round, we can decide if we pull the stochastic arm or refuse to play (in which case we gain a
0
reward).

•

We have no prior knowledge about the type of the bandit
\theta\in\Theta=\{A,B\}
, but we can assume a game theoretical setup to find optimal policies for such a gamble.

7.8
Foundations of Statistical Physics

Statistical physics is a tremendously successful branch of physics, even if its foundations are debatable (and sometimes are debated).
I would mention two main frameworks in which this branch of physics is usually introduced:

•

Counting microstates and introducing ensembles

–

Boltzmann’s principle and counting of microstates
[
59
,
150
,
154
,
178
]

–

Gibbs entropy and H-Theorem
[
199
]

–

Boltzmann’s law
[
161
,
46
]

•

Ergodicity in dynamical systems
[
131
,
79
,
106
]

A game theoretic foundation in a nutshell would look the following:
We define a statistical physics game where
Player 1
needs to bet on (a possibly coarse-grained partition of) the state of the system, but she can choose the time (or multiple instances) of observation.

Player 2
, on the other hand, can choose an initial condition for the system (possibly with some constraints).

A natural conjecture is that under specific properties of the dynamical system (ergodicity, chaos, topological mixing, etc.) and in a specific limit (the observation time interval goes to infinity), the equilibrium strategy of
Player 2
choosing initial states will converge to the invariant measure.
While the equilibrium betting strategy of
Player 1
for finding the system in specific domains will be computable by an ensemble average for this invariant measure.

In this framework, we would not need to say that without knowing the initial condition, we somehow deduced some properties of the system after the ‘‘thermalisation’’ time
49
49

49

“although the motion of systems with a very large number of
degrees of freedom obeys the same laws of mechanics as that of systems consisting of a small number of particles, the existence of many degrees of freedom results in laws of a different kind.”
[
106
]
. However, we could say that if we can freely pick the time of observation, then because of certain properties of a dynamical system, we can come up with a suitable strategy by which we can bet on the value of some observable quantities.

7.9
Quantum metrology

The concept can be generalised to find an optimal set of measurements for a quantum state with unknown parameters.

A natural generalisation of Bayesian games is to use the Quantum relative entropy
[
115
,
127
]
(between the chosen density matrix by
Player 2
and the estimated density matrix by
Player 1
) to quantify the result of our estimate.
(In spirit this would be very similar to the game suggested by Kashyap for classical bits
[
93
,
94
]
.)

7.10
Universal Inductive Inference

We can also formulate the framework as a data compression game.
Imagine the following game:
First,
Player 2
chooses an arbitrary
M
-long binary sequence
D
.

Player 1
can take an
N
-long sample from the sequence
and propose a coding/compressing protocol for the whole
M
-long sequence based on the gathered data.
To describe the coding/compressing protocol
C_{1}
, she can use a Universal Turing Machine, i.e. her code will be represented as a
|C_{1}|
-long input tape.

After this, the whole
M
-long binary sequence will be compressed by the protocol suggested by
Player 1
, resulting in a
|C_{1}(D)|
long string. To be able to decompress the data, both the protocol and the compressed data have to be sent or saved; therefore, the real length of the encoding is
L_{1}=|C_{1}|+|C_{1}(D)|
.
50
50

50

In this way, the framework is related to the Minimum description length principle
[
140
,
60
]
as well.

If
Player 1
would know data
D
in advance, she could
∗
find the shortest coding protocol
C^{D}
for the data, which simply outputs
D
for an empty input.
The length of this optimal code is, by definition, the data’s Kolmogorov complexity
[
195
]

K(D)=|C^{D}|
.

If we assume that
Player 1
’s utility function is the length of her compression, then her regret is the difference between her protocol’s compression length and the optimal compression length, i.e. the Kolmogorov complexity of the data:

\Delta L=|C_{1}|+|C_{1}(D)|-K(D)
.

If we assume such a game between
Player 1
and
Player 2
, in which
Player 2
tries to maximise
Player 1
’s regret, then the equilibrium coding protocols can be linked to probabilities very similar to those which would be suggested by the Solomonoff induction formula
[
153
,
75
]
.

7.11
Poisson games

Limiting case, where all
N,K_{A},K_{B},M\to\infty
, while:

\lim_{i\to\infty}N_{i}\frac{K_{A,i}}{M_{i}}=\lambda_{A}\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad\lim_{i\to\infty}N_{i}\frac{K_{B,i}}{M_{i}}=\lambda_{B}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

(209)

[

,

Appendix A
Proof of the symmetric equilibrium

The proof belongs to Theorem
2.1
.

Proof.

To prove that this is indeed a Nash equilibrium, we need to show that:

•

For
Player 1
:

–

Player 1
can expect the same winning rate
v
for all actions which are present with non-zero probability in her strategy. (The expectation is calculated with respect to
Player 2
’s mixed strategy.)

–

For all actions excluded from the equilibrium strategy, the expected utility can not be greater than
v
.

•

Player 2
can expect the same winning rate for all possible sequences (this is because
Player 1
’s mixed sequence choosing strategy contains all possible sequences).

Formally:

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{2}\sim\sigma_{2}}\left[u_{1}[a_{1},\alpha_{2}]\right]=v,\quad\forall a_{1}\in\supp(\sigma_{1})=\{a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}|\sigma_{1}(a_{1})>0\}

(210)

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{2}\sim\sigma_{2}}\left[u_{1}[a_{1},\alpha_{2}]\right]\leq v,\quad\forall a_{1}\in\supp(\sigma_{1})^{\complement}=\{a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}|\sigma_{1}(a_{1})=0\}

(211)

Since we assume a zero-sum game, the winning rate of
Player 2
is proportional to the winning rate of
Player 1
.

\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{1}\sim\sigma_{1}}\left[u_{1}[\alpha_{1},a_{2}]\right]=v,\quad\forall a_{2}\in\supp(\sigma_{2})=\mathcal{A}_{2}

(212)

It is easy to see that because of the randomized sampling of
Player 1
, the expected utility for
Player 2
depends only on the choice of A or B and does not depend on which sequence has been chosen from
\mathcal{K}_{A}
or
\mathcal{K}_{B}
.

Similarly, because of the mixing of
Player 2
,
Player 1
’s utility is independent of the concrete choice of the sampling.

Some useful notation:

p_{k^{*}}(A)=p^{*}_{A},\quad p_{k^{*}}(B)=p^{*}_{B}

(213)

\sum_{k<k^{*}}p_{k}(A)=\Sigma_{A},\quad\sum_{k>k^{*}}p_{k}(B)=\Sigma_{B}

(214)

First, we show that if
Player 1
would change her policy not to guess B when
k=\ell>k^{*}
, then she would decrease her chance of winning.

This is equivalent to the statement:

\ell>k^{*}\implies P^{*}\ p_{\ell}(A)<(1-P^{*})\ p_{\ell}(B)

(215)

\ell>k^{*}\implies\frac{p_{\ell}(A)}{p_{\ell}(B)}<\frac{1-P^{*}}{P^{*}}

(216)

To prove this, we show that if
k,k-1\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}
:

\frac{p_{k}(A)}{p_{k}(B)}<\frac{p_{k-1}(A)}{p_{k-1}(B)}

(217)

\frac{p_{k}(A)}{p_{k-1}(A)}\frac{p_{k-1}(B)}{p_{k}(B)}<1

(218)

By direct simplification of the binomial factors in Theorem
40
, we get:

\frac{(K_{A}-k+1)(k-K_{B}+M-N)}{(K_{B}-k+1)(k-K_{A}+M-N)}<1

(219)

If
k\leq K_{B}
and
N-k<M-K_{A}

(K_{B}-K_{A})(M-N+1)>0

(220)

This is true for all
K_{B}>K_{A}
and
M\geq N
cases.

From the definition of
P^{*}
we see that:

\frac{1-P^{*}}{P^{*}}=\frac{p_{k^{*}}(A)}{p_{k^{*}}(B)}

(221)

Equation (
221
) together with equation
217
proves
215
.

With essentially the same line of reasoning, we can see that:

\ell<k^{*}\implies P^{*}\ p_{\ell}(A)>(1-P^{*})\ p_{\ell}(B)

(222)

This means that if
Player 1
would change her policy not to guess A when
k=\ell<k^{*}
, then she would decrease her chance of winning.
This proves requirement (
211
).

After this point, the proof can be split into two cases:

•

the case, when
\nu^{*}>0
,

•

and the case when
\nu^{*}=0
.

Mixed policy i.e.
\nu^{*}>0
:

Player 1
is mixing between two policies. We will call the policy Up when she guesses A if
k=k^{*}
, and we will call Down if she guesses B if
k=k^{*}
.

It is useful to introduce 4 possibly different values:

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\leq k^{*}}p_{k}(A)=\Sigma_{A}+p^{*}_{A},

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>k^{*}}p_{k}(B)=\Sigma_{B}

(223)

\displaystyle\check{v}_{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k<k^{*}}p_{k}(A)=\Sigma_{A},

\displaystyle\check{v}_{B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq k^{*}}p_{k}(B)=\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}

(224)

To prove requirement (
210
) we need to show that the expected winning rates for both Up and Down strategies are equal:

\hat{v}=P^{*}\ \hat{v}_{A}+(1-P^{*})\ \hat{v}_{B}

(225)

\check{v}=P^{*}\ \check{v}_{A}+(1-P^{*})\ \check{v}_{B}

(226)

Taking their difference results:

\begin{split}\hat{v}-\check{v}&=P^{*}(\hat{v}_{A}-\check{v}_{A})+(1-P^{*})(\hat{v}_{B}-\check{v}_{B})\\
&=P^{*}\ p^{*}_{A}-(1-P^{*})p^{*}_{B}\\
&=\frac{p^{*}_{B}}{p^{*}_{A}+p^{*}_{B}}\ p^{*}_{A}-\frac{p^{*}_{A}}{p^{*}_{A}+p^{*}_{B}}\ p^{*}_{B}\\
&=0\end{split}

(227)

The final step is to prove requirement (
212
). In order to do this, we need to show that the chance of winning the game for
Player 1
is the same if
Player 2
chooses A or B.

v_{A}=\nu^{*}\ \hat{v}_{A}+(1-\nu^{*})\ \check{v}_{A}=\check{v}_{A}+\nu^{*}(\hat{v}_{A}-\check{v}_{A})=\check{v}_{A}+\nu^{*}\ p^{*}_{A}

(228)

v_{B}=\nu^{*}\ \hat{v}_{B}+(1-\nu^{*})\ \check{v}_{B}=\check{v}_{B}+\nu^{*}(\hat{v}_{B}-\check{v}_{B})=\check{v}_{B}-\nu^{*}\ p^{*}_{B}

(229)

Recalling expression
42
for
\nu^{*}
, and using the new notation:

\nu^{*}=\frac{\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}-\Sigma_{A}}{p^{*}_{A}+p^{*}_{B}}

(230)

v_{A}=\Sigma_{A}+\frac{\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}-\Sigma_{A}}{p^{*}_{A}+p^{*}_{B}}\ p^{*}_{A}

(231)

v_{A}=\frac{p^{*}_{B}\Sigma_{A}+p^{*}_{A}\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{A}\ p^{*}_{B}}{p^{*}_{A}+p^{*}_{B}}

(232)

v_{B}=\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}-\frac{\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}-\Sigma_{A}}{p^{*}_{A}+p^{*}_{B}}\ p^{*}_{B}

(233)

v_{B}=\frac{p^{*}_{A}\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}\Sigma_{A}+p^{*}_{A}\ p^{*}_{B}}{p^{*}_{A}+p^{*}_{B}}

(234)

v^{*}=v_{A}=v_{B}

(235)

Pure policy i.e.
\nu^{*}=0
:

Based on
42
,
\nu^{*}
can be
0
, only if there is a
k^{*}
, for which:

\sum_{k\geq k^{*}}p_{k}(B)-\sum_{k<k^{*}}p_{k}(A)=0

(236)

or

\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}-\Sigma_{A}=0

(237)

In this case,
P^{*}
is not fixed by an equality but only bounded by an inequality:

\begin{split}P^{*}\ p_{k^{*}}(A)&\leq(1-P^{*})\ p_{k^{*}}(B)\\
P^{*}\ p_{k^{*}-1}(A)&\geq(1-P^{*})\ p_{k^{*}-1}(B)\end{split}

(238)

\frac{p_{k^{*}-1}(B)}{p_{k^{*}-1}(A)+p_{k^{*}-1}(B)}\leq P^{*}\leq\frac{p_{k^{*}}(B)}{p_{k^{*}}(A)+p_{k^{*}}(B)}

(239)

The choice in Theorem
2.1
satisfies this inequality. (However, the proof shows that there are other choices which would give an equilibrium in this case.)

Player 1
is not mixing. Therefore, there is no requirement for her indifference about the policies
\hat{\phi}
and
\check{\phi}
. When she adopts a strategy with
\nu^{*}=0
, she uses only
\check{\phi}
.

Therefore, the only further requirement is that
Player 2
has to be indifferent about choosing A or B:

v_{A}=v_{B}

(240)

\check{v}_{A}=\check{v}_{B}

(241)

\Sigma_{A}=\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}

(242)

Which is satisfied because of equation
237
.

∎

A.0.1
Intermediacy lemma

The following proof belongs to the intermediacy lemma of the median
2.1
:

Proof.

Let
\xi
and
\eta
be real-valued random variables, and
\zeta=\lambda\xi+(1-\lambda)\eta
be their mixture with
\lambda\in[0,1]
.

We can define positive and negative
p
–quantile
[
170
,
15
]
tail sets for real-valued random variables in the following way:

\mathbb{T}^{-}_{p}[\xi]=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \Pr(\xi\leq x)<p\}

(243)

\mathbb{T}^{+}_{p}[\xi]=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \Pr(\xi\geq x)<p\}

(244)

In general, the definition of the
p
-quantiles and the median (which can be viewed as the
0.5
-quantile) does not guarantee a unique value; therefore, it is better to define them as a set-valued function:

c\in\mathbb{M}_{p}[\xi]\iff\Pr(\xi\leq c)\geq p\ \wedge\ \Pr(\xi\geq c)\geq p

(245)

The
p
–quantile set-valued function can be expressed by the tail sets in the following way:

\mathbb{M}_{p}[\xi]=\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathbb{T}^{-}_{p}[\xi]\cup\mathbb{T}^{+}_{p}[\xi])

(246)

Because
\zeta
is a mixture,
\Pr(\zeta\leq x)=\lambda\Pr(\xi\leq x)+(1-\lambda)\Pr(\eta\leq x)
. It is easy to see that:

\mathbb{T}^{-}_{p}[\xi]\cap\mathbb{T}^{-}_{p}[\eta]\subset\mathbb{T}^{-}_{p}[\zeta]

(247)

and analogously that:

\mathbb{T}^{+}_{p}[\xi]\cap\mathbb{T}^{+}_{p}[\eta]\subset\mathbb{T}^{+}_{p}[\zeta]

(248)

Therefore
c\in\mathbb{M_{p}}[\zeta]
can not be in
\mathbb{T}^{-}_{p}[\xi]
or
\mathbb{T}^{-}_{p}[\eta]
i.e. less than any median point in
\mathbb{M}_{p}[\xi]
or
\mathbb{M}_{p}[\eta]
;
and
c
can not be in
\mathbb{T}^{+}_{p}[\xi]
or
\mathbb{T}^{+}_{p}[\eta]
i.e. greater than any median point in
\mathbb{M}_{p}[\xi]
or
\mathbb{M}_{p}[\eta]
.

If the
0.5
-quantiles i.e. the medians are unique values and
\mathbbm{m}[\xi]\leq\mathbbm{m}[\eta]
then we have:

\mathbbm{m}[\xi]\leq\mathbbm{m}[\zeta]\leq\mathbbm{m}[\eta]

(249)

This completes the proof.

∎

Appendix B
Proof of the Bayesian equilibrium

The proof belongs to Theorem
3.2
.

Proof.

It is straightforward to see that because of
Player 2
’s mixing strategy,
Player 1
has the same expected utility for any specific sampling.
Similarly, because of the randomized sampling of
Player 1
, the expected utility of
Player 2
depends only on the chosen scenario (A or B) and not the specific sequence (chosen from
\mathcal{K}_{A}
or
\mathcal{K}_{B}
).

The expected growth rate difference can be expressed in the following way:

\Delta G(P,\{p^{\prime}\})=P\ \sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{A}}p_{k}(A)\log(p^{\prime}_{k})+(1-P)\ \sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{B}}p_{k}(B)\log(1-p^{\prime}_{k})

(250)

This expression has to be maximized by a set of splitting ratios described by
\{p^{\prime}_{k}\}
, which is surprisingly simple. After taking the partial derivative respect to
p^{\prime}_{\ell}
:

P\ p_{\ell}(A)\frac{1}{p^{\prime*}_{\ell}}-(1-P)\ p_{\ell}(B)\frac{1}{1-p^{\prime*}_{\ell}}=0\quad\forall\ell\in\mathbb{K}_{A}\cap\mathbb{K}_{B}

(251)

Which can be satisfied by setting:

p^{\prime*}_{\ell}=\frac{P\ p_{\ell}(A)}{P\ p_{\ell}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{\ell}(B)}

(252)

When
\ell\in\mathbb{K}_{A}\setminus\mathbb{K}_{B}
, the derivative is:

P\ p_{\ell}(A)\frac{1}{p^{\prime}_{\ell}}

(253)

which is positive on the whole
p^{\prime*}_{\ell}\in(0,1]
domain. This implies that in this case,
\Delta G
is maximized if:

p^{\prime*}_{\ell}=1,\quad\forall\ell\in\mathbb{K}_{A}\setminus\mathbb{K}_{B}

(254)

Using the same reasoning, we get:

p^{\prime*}_{\ell}=0,\quad\forall\ell\in\mathbb{K}_{B}\setminus\mathbb{K}_{A}

(255)

To formally prove that this is indeed a global maximum, we can check the second derivative with respect to
p^{\prime}_{m}
:

\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{m}\partial p^{\prime}_{\ell}}\Delta G(P,\{p^{\prime}\})\Bigr|_{\{p^{\prime}\}=\{p^{\prime*}\}}&=-\delta_{m,\ell}\left(\frac{P\ p_{\ell}(A)}{{p^{\prime*}_{\ell}}^{2}}+\frac{(1-P)\ p_{\ell}(B)}{(1-p^{\prime*}_{\ell})^{2}}\right)\\
&=-\delta_{m,\ell}\frac{(P\ p_{\ell}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{\ell}(B))^{3}}{P\ (1-P)\ p_{\ell}(A)\ p_{\ell}(B)}\end{split}

(256)

which gives a strictly negative definite Hessian for all
P\in(0,1)
,
p_{\ell}(A)>0
,
p_{\ell}(B)>0
. Ensuring that this is a global maximum.

Finding
P^{*}
:

To find the global saddle point of the expression (
250
), we can take its partial derivative with respect to
P
.
By introducing the following notation:

\Delta G_{A}(P)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{A}}p_{k}(A)\log\left(\frac{P\ p_{k}(A)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}\right)

(257)

\Delta G_{B}(P)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{B}}p_{k}(B)\log\left(\frac{(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}\right)

(258)

The partial derivative can be expressed as
52
52

52

we use “the apparatus of partial derivatives, in which even the notation is ambiguous”
[
194
]
, which is hopefully still more useful than confusing
:

\frac{\partial}{\partial P}\Delta G(P,\{p^{\prime}\})\Bigr|_{\{p^{\prime}\}=\{p^{\prime*}\}}=\Delta(P)=\Delta G_{A}(P)-\Delta G_{B}(P)

(259)

Direct calculation shows that:

\Delta(P)=\frac{d}{dP}\Delta G(P,\{p^{\prime*}(P)\})=\Delta G^{\prime}(P)

(260)

\Delta(P)=0\iff\frac{d}{dP}\Delta G(P,\{p^{\prime*}(P)\})=\Delta G^{\prime}(P)=0

(261)

To show the existence and uniqueness of
P^{*}
, for which
\Delta(P^{*})=0
, it is useful to change variables from
P\in(0,1)
to log-odds
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
, using the following transformation
53
53

53

also known as “logit”
[
190
]
. “Evidence”
[
41
]
as a measure of the Bayes factor is also a related concept.
:

\vartheta=\log\left(\frac{P}{1-P}\right),\quad P=\frac{e^{\vartheta/2}}{e^{\vartheta/2}+e^{-\vartheta/2}}

(262)

Using the convention
p_{k}(A)=0
if
k\notin\mathbb{K}_{A}
, and
p_{k}(B)=0
if
k\notin\mathbb{K}_{B}
, we can rewrite
\Delta
as:

\begin{split}\Delta(\vartheta)=&\vartheta+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{A}}p_{k}(A)\log(p_{k}(A))-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{B}}p_{k}(B)\log(p_{k}(B))-\\
&\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}}(p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B))\log\left(e^{\vartheta/2}p_{k}(A)+e^{-\vartheta/2}p_{k}(B)\right)\end{split}

(263)

or by the entropy difference
\Delta H=H_{A}-H_{B}
:

\begin{split}\Delta(\vartheta)=&\vartheta-\Delta H-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}}(p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B))\log\left(e^{\vartheta/2}p_{k}(A)+e^{-\vartheta/2}p_{k}(B)\right)\end{split}

(264)

The root of
\Delta(\vartheta)
can be redefined as the iterative fixed point of the function
F(\vartheta)
:

\vartheta_{n+1}=F(\vartheta_{n})

(265)

F(\vartheta)=\Delta H+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}}(p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B))\log\left(e^{\vartheta/2}p_{k}(A)+e^{-\vartheta/2}p_{k}(B)\right)

(266)

First, we show that
F
is a contraction:

\frac{d}{d\vartheta}F(\vartheta)=F^{\prime}(\vartheta)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}}(p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B))\left(\frac{e^{\vartheta/2}p_{k}(A)-e^{-\vartheta/2}p_{k}(B)}{e^{\vartheta/2}p_{k}(A)+e^{-\vartheta/2}p_{k}(B)}\right)

(267)

Using Hölder’s inequality
[
2
]
, and that
|(x-y)/(x+y)|\leq 1
for all
x,y\geq 0
,
x+y>0

|F^{\prime}(\vartheta)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}}|p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B)|

(268)

This is the total variation distance
[
37
]
of probability measures
p_{k}(A)
and
p_{k}(B)
, which is strictly less then
1
if there exists an index
k
, for which
p_{k}(A)>0\wedge p_{k}(B)>0
i.e.
\mathbb{K}_{A}\cap\mathbb{K}_{B}=\mathbb{K}_{AB}\neq\emptyset
.

|F^{\prime}(\vartheta)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}}|p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B)|=q<1

(269)

This implies that
F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a contraction, with
q<1
:

|F(\vartheta_{2})-F(\vartheta_{1})|=\left|\int_{\vartheta_{1}}^{\vartheta_{2}}F^{\prime}(\vartheta)d\vartheta\right|\leq\int_{\vartheta_{1}}^{\vartheta_{2}}|F^{\prime}(\vartheta)|d\vartheta\leq q\ |\vartheta_{2}-\vartheta_{1}|

(270)

The Banach fixed point theorem
[
147
]
guarantees a unique fixed point for this contraction:

\exists!\ \vartheta^{*}\in\mathbb{R},\quad F(\vartheta^{*})=\vartheta^{*}

(271)

This completes the proof.

∎

Appendix C
Proof of the Convergence of Binomial games

For the convergence of both Binomial Fisher games and Binomial Bayesian games, the following lemma
C.1
is essential:

Lemma C.1

(Binomial approximation of Hypergeometric distribution)
.

For any sequences of natural numbers
\{M_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
,
\{K_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
, if
\ M_{i}\to\infty
and
K_{i}/M_{i}\to x\in(0,1)
, then

\forall k\in\{0,\dots,N\},\ \lim_{i\to\infty}p_{k}(N,K_{i},M_{i})=p_{k}(N,x)

(272)

where
p_{k}(N,K,M)
is the Hypergeometric distribution:

p_{k}(N,K,M)=\frac{\binom{K}{k}\binom{M-K}{N-k}}{\binom{M}{N}}

(273)

and
p_{k}(N,x)
stands for the Binomial distribution:

p_{k}(N,x)=\binom{N}{k}x^{k}(1-x)^{N-k}

(274)

∎

Proof.

p_{k}(N,K,M)=\frac{\frac{K(K-1)\dots(K-(k-1))}{k!}\frac{(M-K)(M-K-1)\dots(M-K-(k-1))}{(N-k)!}}{\frac{M(M-1)\dots(M-(k-1))\ (M-k)(M-k-1)\dots(M-(N-1))}{N!}}

(275)

\begin{split}p_{k}(N,K,M)=&\frac{N!}{k!(N-k)!}\left(\frac{K}{M}\frac{K-1}{M-1}\dots\frac{K-(k-1)}{M-(k-1)}\right)\\
&\left(\frac{M-K}{M-k}\frac{M-K-1}{M-k-1}\dots\frac{M-K-(N-k-1)}{M-(N-1)}\right)\end{split}

(276)

which simplifies term by term to:

\lim_{i\to\infty}p_{k}(N,K_{i},M_{i})=\frac{N!}{k!(N-k)!}x^{k}(1-x)^{N-k}=p_{k}(N,x)

(277)

∎

The proof can be found in standard textbooks on probability theory
[
15
]
or is frequently left to the reader as an exercise
[
156
]
.

Remark
.

Explicit upper and lower bounds for the total variation distance can be obtained between Hypergeometric and Binomial distributions:

\frac{1}{28}\frac{N-1}{M-1}\leq||\underline{p}(N,K,M)-\underline{p}(N,K/M)||_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\frac{N-1}{M-1}

(278)

(see the proof in the following
derivation
.)

∎

C.1
Binomial Fisher games

Notation:

We introduce a
2\times(N+1)
dimensional array of positive real numbers, which will represent general “Off-shell”
[
198
]
“probabilities” or “weights” (which does not have to be normalized to
1
):

\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}=\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2\times(N+1)}

(279)

We can represent normalized “On-shell” probability distributions by the following identification:

p_{A,k}=p_{k}(A),\quad p_{B,k}=p_{k}(B)

(280)

We can rewrite the requirement for
k^{*}
in Theorem
43
in the following way:

\sum_{k\leq k^{*}}p_{k}(A)+p_{k}(B)>1\iff\sum_{k\leq k^{*}}p_{k}(A)>\sum_{k>k^{*}}p_{k}(B)

(281)

Introducing an indexed difference function:

\delta_{i}(\underline{\underline{p}})=\sum_{k>i}p_{B,k}-\sum_{k\leq i}p_{A,k}

(282)

We can define a function, which gives an integer for a general set of weights
\underline{\underline{p}}

54
54

54

this can be viewed as an “Off-shell”
[
198
]
generalizations of equilibrium parameters, where
\underline{p}_{A}
and
\underline{p}_{B}
are not necessarily normalized to 1, but are positive.

k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})=\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{N}\mathds{1}\left(\delta_{k^{\prime}}(\underline{\underline{p}})>0\right)

(283)

Where
\mathds{1}(\varphi)
is the indicator function, which gives
1
if the logical formula
\varphi
is true, and 0 if
\varphi
is false.

We can promote the expression for
\nu^{*}
in Theorem
42
to a general function as well:

\nu^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})=\frac{\sum_{k\geq k^{*}}p_{B,k}-\sum_{k<k^{*}}p_{A,k}}{p_{A,k^{*}}+p_{B,k^{*}}},\quad k^{*}\leftarrow k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})

(284)

which we can rewrite to:

\nu^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})=\frac{\delta_{k^{*}}(\underline{\underline{p}})+p_{A,k^{*}}+p_{B,k^{*}}}{p_{A,k^{*}}+p_{B,k^{*}}},\quad k^{*}\leftarrow k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})

(285)

The generalization of the expression for
P^{*}
in Theorem
41
is straightforward:

P^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})=\frac{p_{B,k^{*}}}{p_{A,k^{*}}+p_{B,k^{*}}},\quad k^{*}\leftarrow k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})

(286)

Finally, we can write down the strategy parameter, which describes the equilibrium strategy of
Player 1
:

s^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})=\frac{1}{N+1}\left(k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})+\nu^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})\right)

(287)

Lemma C.2

(Smoothness in the bulk)
.

If for some
\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}=\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2\times(N+1)}
there is no
k^{*}\in\{0,\dots,N\}
for which
\delta_{k^{*}}(\underline{\underline{p}})=0
, then there is some open neighborhood of
\underline{\underline{p}}
,
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{W}_{2}
where the functions:

k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})=k^{*},\quad\text{if }\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathcal{U}

(288)

and all equilibrium functions are smooth:

\nu^{\circ}_{N},P^{\circ}_{N},s^{\circ}_{N}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R})

(289)

∎

Proof.

We can define a strictly positive quantity:

\Delta\delta=\min\{|\delta_{k}(\underline{\underline{p}})|k\in\{0,\dots,N\}\}

(290)

if we take any different
\underline{\underline{q}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2\times(N+1)}
, where

|q_{\theta,k}-p_{\theta,k}|<\Delta\delta/(N+1)

(291)

then for all
k\in\{0,\dots,N\}

\delta_{k}(\underline{\underline{p}})
and
\delta_{k}(\underline{\underline{q}})
has the same sign, meaning that:

k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})=k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{q}})

(292)

this implies that
k^{\circ}_{N}
is constant on a finite open
p=\infty
norm “ball” (or rather hypercube):

\mathcal{U}=B_{\infty}(\underline{\underline{p}},\Delta\delta/(N+1))=\left\{\underline{\underline{q}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}|\ ||\underline{\underline{q}}-\underline{\underline{p}}||_{\infty}<\Delta\delta/(N+1))\right\}

(293)

k^{\circ}_{N}=k^{*}
is constant on
\mathcal{U}
, therefore
\nu^{\circ}_{N},P^{\circ}_{N},s^{\circ}_{N}
are only rational functions of
p_{\theta,k}
variables, which are all infinitely continuously differentiable, i.e., smooth.

\nu^{\circ}_{N},P^{\circ}_{N},s^{\circ}_{N}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R})

(294)

∎

Lemma C.3

(Continuity of policies on the scars)
.

If for some
\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}
there is a
k^{*}\in\{0,\dots,N\}
for which
\delta_{k^{*}}(\underline{\underline{p}})=0
, then

s^{\circ}_{N}
is continuous at
\underline{\underline{p}}
:

s^{\circ}_{N}\in C^{0}(\underline{\underline{p}})

(295)

∎

Proof.

We will show that for any sequence
\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
, which converges to
\underline{\underline{p}}
,
s^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i)})
will converge to
(k^{*}+1)/(N+1)
:

\forall\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=0}^{\infty},\ \lim_{i\to\infty}\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i)}=\underline{\underline{p}}\implies\lim_{i\to\infty}s^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i)})=s^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}})=(k^{*}+1)/(N+1)

(296)

To show this, we split a sequence
\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
to two subsequences,
\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(i)}\right\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
and
\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(i)}\right\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
:

\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)}=\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i_{j})},\ \delta_{k^{*}}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i_{j})})>0,\ i_{j+1}>i_{j}

(297)

\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)}=\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i_{j})},\ \delta_{k^{*}}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i_{j})})\leq 0,\ i_{j+1}>i_{j}

(298)

Greater than zero case:

For any
\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(i)}\right\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
sequence:

\lim_{j\to\infty}k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)})=k^{*}+1

(299)

and

\lim_{j\to\infty}\nu^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)})=\frac{\delta_{k^{*}+1}(\underline{\underline{p}})+p_{1,k^{*}+1}+p_{2,k^{*}+1}}{p_{1,k^{*}+1}+p_{2,k^{*}+1}}

(300)

In general:

\delta_{k+1}(\underline{\underline{p}})-\delta_{k}(\underline{\underline{p}})=-(p_{A,(k+1)}+p_{B,(k+1)})

(301)

because of
\delta_{k^{*}}(\underline{\underline{p}})=0
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\nu^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)})=\frac{-(p_{A,(k^{*}+1)}+p_{B,(k^{*}+1)})+p_{A,k^{*}+1}+p_{B,k^{*}+1}}{p_{A,k^{*}+1}+p_{B,k^{*}+1}}=0

(302)

Therefore, the limit for
s^{\circ}_{N}
:

\lim_{j\to\infty}s^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)})=\left(\lim_{j\to\infty}k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)})+\lim_{j\to\infty}\nu^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)})\right)/(N+1)=\frac{k^{*}+1}{N+1}

(303)

Less or equal to zero case:

For any
\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(i)}\right\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
sequence:

\lim_{j\to\infty}k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)})=k^{*}

(304)

and

\lim_{j\to\infty}\nu^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)})=\frac{\delta_{k^{*}}(\underline{\underline{p}})+p_{A,k^{*}}+p_{B,k^{*}}}{p_{A,k^{*}}+p_{B,k^{*}}}

(305)

because of
\delta_{k^{*}}(\underline{\underline{p}})=0
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\nu^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)})=\frac{0+p_{A,k^{*}}+p_{B,k^{*}}}{p_{A,k^{*}}+p_{B,k^{*}}}=1

(306)

Therefore, the limit for
s^{\circ}_{N}
:

\lim_{j\to\infty}s^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)})=\left(\lim_{j\to\infty}k^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)})+\lim_{j\to\infty}\nu^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)})\right)/(N+1)=\frac{k^{*}+1}{N+1}

(307)

Summary:

While
k^{\circ}_{N}
and
\nu^{\circ}_{N}
have different limits for

\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)}\right\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
and

\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)}\right\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
sequences, their combination in
s^{\circ}_{N}
converges to the same limit, meaning that the only accumulation point (or limit points
[
167
]
) of the
\left\{s^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i)})\right\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
is
\{(k^{*}+1)/(N+1)\}
.

∎

Remark
.

In a Fisher game, the strategy of
Player 1
is fully determined by the pair
\{k^{*},\nu^{*}\}
. In the original construction we allowed only
\nu^{*}\in[0,1)
, but if we formally allow
\nu^{*}=1
values as well, then the strategies:
\{k^{*}=k^{*}_{1},\nu^{*}=0\}
and
\{k^{*}=k^{*}_{1}+1,\nu^{*}=1\}
are identical.

Therefore two strategies for
Player 1

\{k^{*}_{1},\nu^{*}_{1}\}
,
\{k^{*}_{2},\nu^{*}_{2}\}
are identical, if the composed
s^{*}_{1}=(k^{*}_{1}+\nu^{*}_{1})/(N+1)
and
s^{*}_{2}=(k^{*}_{2}+\nu^{*}_{2})/(N+1)
are identical.

\mu_{\{k^{*}_{1},\nu^{*}_{1}\}}=\mu_{\{k^{*}_{2},\nu^{*}_{2}\}}\iff s^{*}_{1}=s^{*}_{2}

(308)

∎

Lemma C.4

(Finite jump of the prior)
.

If for some
\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}
there is a
k^{\bullet}\in\{0,\dots,N\}
for which
\delta_{k^{\bullet}}(\underline{\underline{p}})=0
, then

P^{\circ}_{N}
has a discontinuity at
\underline{\underline{p}}
, but is bounded by
\underline{P}^{\bullet}
and
\overline{P}^{\bullet}
:

\underline{P}^{\bullet}=\frac{p_{B,k^{\bullet}}}{p_{A,k^{\bullet}}+p_{B,k^{\bullet}}},\quad\overline{P}^{\bullet}=\frac{p_{B,k^{\bullet}+1}}{p_{A,k^{\bullet}+1}+p_{B,k^{\bullet}+1}}

(309)

∎

Proof.

For the proof, we can use the same constructions of

\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)}\right\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
and

\left\{\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)}\right\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
as in the previous proof, and observe that:

\lim_{j\to\infty}P^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{>(j)})=\frac{p_{B,k^{\bullet}+1}}{p_{A,k^{\bullet}+1}+p_{B,k^{\bullet}+1}}=\overline{P}^{\bullet}

(310)

\lim_{j\to\infty}P^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{\leq(j)})=\frac{p_{B,k^{\bullet}}}{p_{A,k^{\bullet}}+p_{B,k^{\bullet}}}=\underline{P}^{\bullet}

(311)

This shows that only these two accumulation points (or limit points
[
167
]
) are possible for the Symmetric equilibrium sequence
\left\{P^{\circ}_{N}(\underline{\underline{p}}^{(i)})\right\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
, which are
\{\underline{P}^{\bullet},\overline{P}^{\bullet}\}
.

∎

Now we have all the ingredients to prove Theorem
4.1
about the Symmetrical equilibrium of Binomial Fisher games:

Proof.

For any converging sequence of finite games:

{\swabfamily G}_{i}={\swabfamily Game}(N,K_{A,i},K_{B,i},M_{i})

(312)

\begin{split}\{{\swabfamily G}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\in\overline{{\swabfamily Game}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})\iff&\lim_{i\to\infty}M_{i}=\infty\ \wedge\\
&\lim_{i\to\infty}K_{A,i}/M_{i}=x_{A}\ \wedge\\
&\lim_{i\to\infty}K_{B,i}/M_{i}=x_{B}\end{split}

(313)

Based on lemma
C.1
, Hypergeometric probability distributions converge to Binomial distributions:

\begin{split}\underline{p}(N,K_{A,i},M_{i})\to\underline{p}(N,x_{A})\\
\underline{p}(N,K_{B,i},M_{i})\to\underline{p}(N,x_{B})\end{split}

(314)

Limit policy for
Player 1
:

The consequence of lemma
C.2
and lemma
C.3
, is that:

s^{\circ}_{N}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathbb{R})

(315)

Therefore, the expression:

s^{*}_{\infty}=\lim_{i\to\infty}s^{*}(N,K_{A,i},K_{B_{i}},M_{i})=\lim_{i\to\infty}s^{\circ}_{N}((\underline{p}(N,K_{A,i},M_{i}),\underline{p}(N,K_{B,i},M_{i})))

(316)

can be simplified to

\lim_{i\to\infty}s^{\circ}_{N}((\underline{p}(N,K_{A,i},M_{i}),\underline{p}(N,K_{B,i},M_{i})))=s^{\circ}_{N}((\underline{p}(N,x_{A}),\underline{p}(N,x_{B})))

(317)

s^{\circ}_{N}((\underline{p}(N,x_{A}),\underline{p}(N,x_{B})))=s^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(318)

meaning that:

s^{*}_{\infty}=s^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(319)

Where
s^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
can be composed from
k^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
and
\nu^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
, which can be calculated using the criterion
155
and formula
154
.

s^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})=(k^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})+\nu^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B}))/(N+1)

(320)

From
s^{*}_{\infty}
we can define the limit values of
k^{*}_{\infty}
and
\nu^{*}_{\infty}

55
55

55

If
k^{*}_{i}
and
\nu^{*}_{i}
fails to converge, then we choose this as the definition from the converging
s^{*}_{i}
sequence, and so we preserve the
\nu^{*}\in[0,1)
convention. Because of remark
Remark
, this is only a parametrization choice of equivalent policies.
:

k^{*}_{\infty}=\lfloor(N+1)\ s^{*}_{\infty}\rfloor,\quad\nu^{*}_{\infty}=(N+1)\ s^{*}_{\infty}-\lfloor(N+1)s^{*}_{\infty}\rfloor

(321)

resulting that we can identify the limit strategy parameters by the parameters, which can be calculated using the limiting Binomial distribution:

k^{*}_{\infty}=k^{*}_{\text{Bin}},\quad\nu^{*}_{\infty}=\nu^{*}_{\text{Bin}}

(322)

This means that the limit policy of
Player 1
is well defined for all
0<x_{A}<x_{B}<1
, and can be calculated using
155
and
154
.

Limit policy for
Player 2
:

First we can observe, based on (
282
), (
285
) and (
156
) that:

(x_{A},x_{B})\in\mathbb{S}_{N}\iff\exists k\in\{0,\dots,N\},\ \delta_{k}((\underline{p}(N,x_{A}),\underline{p}(N,x_{B})))=0

(323)

If
(x_{A},x_{B})\notin\mathbb{S}_{n}
, then because of lemma
C.2
the smoothness of
P^{\circ}_{N}
guarantees that we can use the Binomial limit distributions to obtain the limit value of
P^{*}_{\infty}
:

P^{*}_{\infty}=\lim_{i\to\infty}P^{*}(N,K_{A,i},K_{B_{i}},M_{i})=\lim_{i\to\infty}P^{\circ}_{N}((\underline{p}(N,K_{A,i},M_{i}),\underline{p}(N,K_{B,i},M_{i})))

(324)

\lim_{i\to\infty}P^{\circ}_{N}((\underline{p}(N,K_{A,i},M_{i}),\underline{p}(N,K_{B,i},M_{i})))=P^{\circ}_{N}((\underline{p}(N,x_{A}),\underline{p}(N,x_{B})))

(325)

P^{\circ}_{N}((\underline{p}(N,x_{A}),\underline{p}(N,x_{B})))=P^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(326)

where
P^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
can be calculated by (
157
), meaning that we can identify the limit prior by the prior that can be calculated using the limiting Binomial distribution:

P^{*}_{\infty}=P^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(327)

If, however,
(x_{A},x_{B})\in\mathbb{S}_{N}
, then
\delta_{k^{*}-1}((\underline{p}(N,x_{A}),\underline{p}(N,x_{B})))=0
the sequence
\{P^{*}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
can have two accumulation points:

\mathrm{Acc}\left(\{P^{*}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}\right)\subset\{\underline{P}^{*},\overline{P}^{*}\}

(328)

By using equation
309
, while substituting

k^{\bullet}=k^{*}-1
we get:

\underline{P}^{*}=\frac{p_{B,k^{*}-1}}{p_{A,k^{*}-1}+p_{B,k^{*}-1}},\quad\overline{P}^{*}=\frac{p_{B,k^{*}}}{p_{A,k^{*}}+p_{B,k^{*}}}

(329)

However, as
i\to\infty
, the game sequence
\{\mathfrak{G}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
“converges to a degenerate game”, meaning that if
Player 1
uses the strategy
\{k^{*}_{\infty},\nu^{*}_{\infty}\}
, then the winning rate does not change, if
Player 2
chooses any
P^{*}\in[\underline{P}^{*},\overline{P}^{*}]
.

This completes the proof.

∎

C.2
Binomial Bayesian games

Notation:
