We can define a function, which gives the growth rate difference for a general set of weights
\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}=\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2\times(N+1)}

56
56

56

this can be viewed as the “Off-shell”
[
198
]
generalizations of the growth rate difference function, where
\underline{p}_{A}
and
\underline{p}_{B}
are not necessarily normalized to 1, but are positive.

\begin{split}\Delta G^{\circ}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})=&P\sum_{k}p_{A,k}\log\left(\frac{P\ p_{A,k}}{Pp_{A,k}+(1-P)p_{B,k}}\right)+\\
&(1-P)\sum_{k}p_{B,k}\log\left(\frac{(1-P)p_{B,k}}{Pp_{A,k}+(1-P)p_{B,k}}\right)\end{split}

(330)

Its first derivative with respect to
P
:

\begin{split}\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})=&\log(P)\sum_{k}p_{A,k}-\log(1-P)\sum_{k}p_{B,k}-\Delta H(\underline{\underline{p}})+\\
&-\sum_{k}(p_{A,k}-p_{B,k})\log(Pp_{A,k}+(1-P)p_{B,k})\end{split}

(331)

where:

\Delta H(\underline{\underline{p}})=-\sum_{k}p_{A,k}\log(p_{A,k})+\sum_{k}p_{B,k}\log(p_{B,k})

(332)

and the growth rate difference’s second derivative with respect to
P
:

\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})=\sum_{k}\frac{p_{A,k}\ p_{B,k}}{P(1-P)(Pp_{A,k}+(1-P)p_{B,k})}

(333)

The generalized splitting ratio function looks the following:

p^{\prime\circ}_{k}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})=\frac{P\ p_{A,k}}{P\ p_{A,k}+(1-P)p_{B,k}}

(334)

Definition C.1
.

The generalized
P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})
is defined implicitly:

\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}}),\underline{\underline{p}})=0

(335)

∎

Lemma C.5

(Existence and unicity of the solution)
.

The implicitly defined
P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})
has a unique solution on the interval
(0,1)
for any
\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}
.

∎

Proof.

The same convexity arguments can be used for a general
\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}
as in Section
3.6

∎

Lemma C.6

(Differentiability of the prior)
.

P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})
is continuously differentiable on the whole
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2\times(N+1)}
domain:

P^{\circ}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{W}_{2},(0,1))

(336)

∎

Proof.

The statement is essentially the consequence of the Implicit function theorem
[
139
]
.

\frac{\partial}{\partial P}\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})\Bigr|_{P=P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})}\ dP+\frac{\partial}{\partial\underline{\underline{p}}}\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})\Bigr|_{P=P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})}\ d\underline{\underline{p}}=0

(337)

\frac{\partial P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})}{\partial\underline{\underline{p}}}=-\frac{1}{\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})}\frac{\partial}{\partial\underline{\underline{p}}}\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})\Bigr|_{P=P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})}

(338)

or by components:

\frac{\partial P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})}{\partial p_{\theta,\ell}}=-\frac{1}{\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\theta,\ell}}\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})\Bigr|_{P=P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})}

(339)

\begin{split}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{A,\ell}}\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})=&\log(P)-\log(p_{A,\ell})-1+\\
&-\log(P\ p_{A,\ell}+(1-P)p_{B,\ell})-P\frac{p_{A,\ell}-p_{B,\ell}}{P\ p_{A,\ell}+(1-P)p_{B,\ell}}\end{split}

(340)

\begin{split}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{B,\ell}}\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})=&-\log(1-P)+\log(p_{B,\ell})+1+\\
&\log(P\ p_{A,\ell}+(1-P)p_{B,\ell})-(1-P)\frac{p_{A,\ell}-p_{B,\ell}}{P\ p_{A,\ell}+(1-P)p_{B,\ell}}\end{split}

(341)

while
\Delta G^{\circ}{}^{\prime\prime}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})
is strictly positive for all
P\in(0,1),\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}
, therefore all partial derivatives of
P^{\circ}(\underline{\underline{p}})
exists, and are continuous.

∎

Lemma C.7

(Differentiability of the splitting ratios)
.

p^{\prime\circ}_{k}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})
is continuously differentiable on the whole
(0,1)\times\mathbb{W}_{2}
domain:

p^{\prime\circ}_{k}\in C^{1}((0,1)\times\mathbb{W}_{2},(0,1))

(342)

∎

Proof.

Explicit calculation shows that:

\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{A,\ell}}p^{\prime\circ}_{k}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})=\delta_{k,\ell}\frac{p_{B,k}\ P(1-P)}{(P\ p_{A,k}+(1-P)\ p_{B,k})^{2}}

(343)

\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{B,\ell}}p^{\prime\circ}_{k}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})=-\delta_{k,\ell}\frac{p_{A,k}\ P(1-P)}{(P\ p_{A,k}+(1-P)\ p_{B,k})^{2}}

(344)

\frac{\partial}{\partial P}p^{\prime\circ}_{k}(P,\underline{\underline{p}})=\frac{p_{A,k}\ p_{B,k}}{(P\ p_{A,k}+(1-P)\ p_{B,k})^{2}}

(345)

therefore all derivatives exists and are continuous for any
P\in(0,1),\underline{\underline{p}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}
.

∎

Now we have all the ingredients to prove Theorem
4.3
about the equilibrium of Binomial Bayesian games:

Proof.

For any converging sequence of finite Bayesian games:

{\swabfamily BG}_{i}={\swabfamily BGame}(N,K_{A,i},K_{B,i},M_{i})

(346)

\begin{split}\{{\swabfamily BG}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\in\overline{{\swabfamily BGame}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})\iff&\lim_{i\to\infty}M_{i}=\infty\ \wedge\\
&\lim_{i\to\infty}K_{A,i}/M_{i}=x_{A}\ \wedge\\
&\lim_{i\to\infty}K_{B,i}/M_{i}=x_{B}\end{split}

(347)

Based on lemma
C.1
, Hypergeometric probability distributions converge to Binomial distributions:

\begin{split}\underline{p}(N,K_{A,i},M_{i})\to\underline{p}(N,x_{A})\\
\underline{p}(N,K_{B,i},M_{i})\to\underline{p}(N,x_{B})\end{split}

(348)

Prior convergence:

The consequence of lemma
C.6
is that:

P^{\circ}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{W}_{2},(0,1))

(349)

Therefore, the expression:

P^{*}_{\infty}=\lim_{i\to\infty}P^{*}(N,K_{A,i},K_{B_{i}},M_{i})=\lim_{i\to\infty}P^{\circ}((\underline{p}(N,K_{A,i},M_{i}),\underline{p}(N,K_{B,i},M_{i})))

(350)

can be simplified to

\lim_{i\to\infty}P^{\circ}((\underline{p}(N,K_{A,i},M_{i}),\underline{p}(N,K_{B,i},M_{i})))=P^{\circ}((\underline{p}(N,x_{A}),\underline{p}(N,x_{B})))

(351)

P^{\circ}((\underline{p}(N,x_{A}),\underline{p}(N,x_{B})))=P^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(352)

meaning that:

P^{*}_{\infty}=P^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(353)

where
P^{*}_{\text{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
can be calculated using the implicit formula (
165
).

Splitting ratio convergence:

The same continuity argument based on lemma
C.7
gives that for all
k\in\{0,\dots,N\}
the splitting ratios converge to:

p^{\prime*}_{k,\infty}=p^{\prime*}_{k,\mathrm{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})

(354)

where
p^{\prime*}_{k,\mathrm{Bin}}(N,x_{A},x_{B})
can be calculated using the formula (
164
).

This completes the proof.

∎

Appendix D
Derivation of limiting priors

Most calculations presented in this section do not yield a solid proof, but hopefully, they can show the path for a more careful derivation, which will lead to exact statements.

Notation:

For the derivation of both Binomial Fisher and Bayesian limiting priors, the following notation will be useful:

I(x,y)
or
I(x,x_{\theta})=I_{\theta}(x)
will denote the so called rate function
[
200
,
3
,
189
]
of a Binomial distribution:

I(x,x_{\theta})=I_{\theta}(x)=x\log\left(\frac{x}{x_{\theta}}\right)+(1-x)\log\left(\frac{1-x}{1-x_{\theta}}\right)\geq 0,\quad x\in[0,1]

(355)

I(1-x,x-x_{\theta})=I(x,x_{\theta})

(356)

Its derivative:

I^{\prime}(x,x_{\theta})=I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x)=\log\left(\frac{x}{x_{\theta}}\frac{1-x_{\theta}}{1-x}\right)=\log\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)-\log\left(\frac{x_{\theta}}{1-x_{\theta}}\right)

(357)

and its second derivative:

I^{\prime\prime}(x,x_{\theta})=I^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}(x)=\frac{1}{x(1-x)}=\kappa(x)>0,\quad x\in(0,1)

(358)

Another useful quantity will be:

\beta=\log\left(\frac{x_{B}(1-x_{A})}{x_{A}(1-x_{B})}\right)>0

(359)

D.1
Binomial Fisher limiting policy

Lemma D.1
.

For all
0<x_{A}<x_{B}<1

x_{A}\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{k^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})}{N}\leq x_{B}

(360)

∎

Proof.

The statement follows from Theorem
4.2
:

x_{A}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\lfloor Nx_{A}\rfloor}{N}\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{k^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})}{N}\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\lceil Nx_{B}\rceil}{N}=x_{B}

(361)

∎

The proof below will belong to Theorem
4.4
.

Proof.

attempt:

The proof will rely on Chernoff bound
[
30
]
and a lower bound on tail distribution
[
6
]
for Binomial random variables:

Notation:

Tail distribution:

T_{N}(k^{\bullet},x)=\sum_{k=k^{\bullet}}^{N}p_{k}(N,x)

(362)

its upper and lower bounds if
0<x<k^{\bullet}/N<1

[
6
]
:

\frac{e^{-N\cdot I(k^{\bullet}/N,x)}}{\sqrt{8N\cdot k^{\bullet}/N(1-k^{\bullet}/N)}}\leq T_{N}(k^{\bullet},x)\leq e^{-N\cdot I(k^{\bullet}/N,x)}

(363)

\underline{T}_{N}(k^{\bullet},x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2N}}e^{-N\cdot I(k^{\bullet}/N,x)}\leq T_{N}(k^{\bullet},x)\leq e^{-N\cdot I(k^{\bullet}/N,x)}=\overline{T}_{N}(k^{\bullet},x)

(364)

where
I(.,.)
is the so called rate function introduced in (
355
).
It has a positive second derivative for all
x\in(0,1)
, so the increment of
k^{\bullet}
in the
T(k^{\bullet},x)
expression can be estimated in the following way:

\underline{T}^{+}_{N}(k^{\bullet},x)=\frac{e^{-I^{\prime}(k^{\bullet}/N,x)}}{\sqrt{2N}}e^{-N\cdot I(k^{\bullet}/N,x)}\leq T_{N}(k^{\bullet}+1,x)\leq e^{-N\cdot I(k^{\bullet}/N,x)}=\overline{T}^{+}_{N}(k^{\bullet},x)

(365)

Left and right tails:

For Binomial distributions, the left and right tails are connected:

\sum_{k\leq k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(N,x)=\sum_{k\geq N-k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(N,1-x)=T_{N}(N-k^{\bullet},1-x)

(366)

because of the symmetry of the rate function (
356
), formally the following inequalities hold:

\underline{T}_{N}(k^{\bullet},x)\leq T_{N}(N-k^{\bullet},1-x)\leq\overline{T}_{N}(k^{\bullet},x)

(367)

For the increment:

\underline{T}^{+}_{N}(N-k^{\bullet},1-x)\leq T_{N}(N-(k^{\bullet}+1),1-x)\leq\overline{T}^{+}_{N}(N-k^{\bullet},1-x)

(368)

Requirements for
k^{*}
:

Recalling equation
155
and expressing it in terms of Binomial tail distributions, we get:

\sum_{k\leq k^{*}}p_{k}(A)+p_{k}(B)>1\implies T_{N}(N-k^{*},1-x_{B})>T_{N}(k^{*}+1,x_{A})

(369)

\sum_{k<k^{*}}p_{k}(A)+p_{k}(B)\leq 1\implies T_{N}(N-(k^{*}+1),1-x_{B})\leq T_{N}(k^{*},x_{A})

(370)

Recalling that in the limit
x_{A}\leq k^{*}/N\leq x_{B}
, we can use the tail inequalities for the Binomial distribution, resulting in the following inequalities:

\overline{T}_{N}(N-k^{*},1-x_{B})>\underline{T}^{+}_{N}(k^{*},x_{A})

(371)

\underline{T}^{+}_{N}(N-k^{*},1-x_{B})\leq\overline{T}_{N}(k^{*},x_{A})

(372)

After taking the logarithm and dividing by
N
, we get:

-I(k^{*}/N,x_{B})>-I(k^{*}/N,x_{A})+\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\frac{e^{-I^{\prime}(k^{*}/N,x_{A})}}{\sqrt{2N}}\right)

(373)

-I(k^{*}/N,x_{B})+\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\frac{e^{I^{\prime}(k^{*}/N,x_{B})}}{\sqrt{2N}}\right)\leq-I(k^{*}/N,x_{A})

(374)

Together, the inequalities give:

\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\frac{e^{-I^{\prime}(k^{*}/N,x_{A})}}{\sqrt{2N}}\right)<I(k^{*}/N,x_{A})-I(k^{*}/N,x_{B})\leq-\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\frac{e^{I^{\prime}(k^{*}/N,x_{B})}}{\sqrt{2N}}\right)

(375)

And by using that
x_{A}\leq k^{*}/N\leq x_{B}
again, we get:

\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\frac{e^{-I^{\prime}(x_{B},x_{A})}}{\sqrt{2N}}\right)<I(k^{*}/N,x_{A})-I(k^{*}/N,x_{B})\leq-\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\frac{e^{I^{\prime}(x_{A},x_{B})}}{\sqrt{2N}}\right)

(376)

Squeeze theorem:

This means that in the limit
N\to\infty
, the two rate functions have to be equal:

\lim_{N\to\infty}I(k^{*}_{N}/N,x_{A})-I(k^{*}_{N}/N,x_{B})=0

(377)

Therefore:

I(x_{0}^{*},x_{A})-I(x_{0}^{*},x_{B})=0

(378)

Direct calculation shows that this is satisfied by:

x_{0}^{*}=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1-x_{A}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}{\log\left(\frac{(1-x_{A})x_{B}}{(1-x_{B})x_{A}}\right)}

(379)

This attempts to complete the proof.

∎

D.1.1
Binomial Fisher limiting prior bounds

The derivation supports conjecture
4.1
.

Notation:

Left and right tail distributions:

T_{A}(k^{\bullet},N)=\sum_{k\geq k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(A),\quad T_{B}(k^{\bullet},N)=\sum_{k\leq k^{\bullet}}p_{k}(B)

(380)

Derivation:

Recalling equation (
157
) we have (if
\nu^{*}\neq 0
):

P^{*}_{N}\ p_{k^{*}_{N}}(A)=(1-P^{*}_{N})\ p_{k^{*}_{N}}(B)

(381)

which can be expressed by the left and right tail distributions:

P^{*}_{N}\left(T_{A}(k^{*}_{N},N)-T_{A}(k^{*}_{N}+1,N)\right)=(1-P^{*}_{N})\left(T_{B}(k^{*}_{N},N)-T_{B}(k^{*}_{N}-1,N)\right)

(382)

From the expression for
\nu^{*}
in Theorem
154
(or more directly from (
228
) and (
229
)) follows:

1-T_{A}(k^{*}_{N})+\nu^{*}\ p_{k^{*}_{N}}(A)=1-T_{B}(k^{*}_{N})+(1-\nu^{*})\ p_{k^{*}_{N}}(B)

(383)

which can be rearranged to:

(1-\nu^{*})T_{A}(k^{*}_{N})+\nu^{*}\ T_{A}(k^{*}_{N}+1,N)=\nu^{*}\ T_{B}(k^{*}_{N})+(1-\nu^{*})T_{B}(k^{*}_{N}-1,N)

(384)

Dividing equation (
382
) with equation (
384
) results in the following expression for
P^{*}_{N}
:

\frac{P^{*}_{N}}{1-P^{*}_{N}}=\frac{T_{B}(k^{*}_{N},N)-T_{B}(k^{*}_{N}-1,N)}{T_{A}(k^{*}_{N},N)-T_{A}(k^{*}_{N}+1,N)}\frac{(1-\nu^{*})T_{A}(k^{*}_{N})+\nu^{*}\ T_{A}(k^{*}_{N}+1,N)}{\nu^{*}\ T_{B}(k^{*}_{N})+(1-\nu^{*})T_{B}(k^{*}_{N}-1,N)}

(385)

Lemma D.2
.

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{T_{A}(k^{*}_{N}+1,N)}{T_{A}(k^{*}_{N},N)}=\lim_{N\to\infty}e^{-I_{A}^{\prime}(k^{*}_{N}/N)},\quad\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{T_{B}(k^{*}_{N}-1,N)}{T_{B}(k^{*}_{N},N)}=\lim_{N\to\infty}e^{I_{B}^{\prime}(k^{*}_{N}/N)}

(386)

∎

Proof.

In
[
21
]
, the following asymptotic expansion has been obtained for the tail of Binomial distribution:

\frac{\sum_{k=n}^{rn}p_{n}(rn,p)}{p_{k}(rn,p)}=\frac{p^{-1}-1}{p^{-1}-r}+\mathcal{O}(1/n)

(387)

Substituting:

N=rn,\quad n=k^{*}=x^{*}_{0}N,\quad p=x_{A}

(388)

we get:

\frac{T_{A}(k^{*},N)}{p_{k^{*}}(N,x_{A})}=\frac{x_{A}^{-1}-1}{x_{A}^{-1}-N/{k^{*}}}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)

(389)

Because of

p_{k^{*}}(N,x_{A})=T_{A}(k^{*},N)-T_{A}(k^{*}+1,N)

(390)

we get:

\frac{T_{A}(k^{*},N)}{T_{A}(k^{*},N)-T_{A}(k^{*}+1,N)}=\frac{k^{*}/N-x_{A}k^{*}/N}{k^{*}/N-x_{A}}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)

(391)

We can express the
T_{A}(k^{*}+1,N)/T_{A}(k^{*},N)
ratio:

\frac{T_{A}(k^{*}_{N}+1,N)}{T_{A}(k^{*}_{N},N)}=\frac{x_{A}}{1-x_{A}}\frac{1-k^{*}/N}{k^{*}/N}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)

(392)

Recalling the expression for
I^{\prime}_{A}(k^{*}/N)
in (
357
) we get:

e^{-I^{\prime}_{A}(k^{*}/N)}=\frac{x_{A}}{1-x_{A}}\Big/\frac{k^{*}/N}{1-k^{*}/N}=\frac{x_{A}}{1-x_{A}}\frac{1-k^{*}/N}{k^{*}/N}

(393)

This proves that:

\frac{T_{A}(k^{*}_{N}+1,N)}{T_{A}(k^{*}_{N},N)}=e^{-I^{\prime}_{A}(k^{*}/N)}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)

(394)

A similar reasoning shows that:

\frac{T_{B}(k^{*}_{N}-1,N)}{T_{B}(k^{*}_{N},N)}=e^{I_{B}^{\prime}(k^{*}_{N}/N)}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)

(395)

∎

Using lemma
D.2
we can simplify the expression for
P^{*}_{N}

\frac{P^{*}_{N}}{1-P^{*}_{N}}=\frac{1-e^{I_{B}^{\prime}(k^{*}_{N}/N)}}{1-e^{-I_{A}^{\prime}(k^{*}_{N}/N)}}\frac{(1-\nu^{*})+\nu^{*}\ e^{-I_{A}^{\prime}(k^{*}_{N}/N)}}{\nu^{*}+(1-\nu^{*})e^{I_{B}^{\prime}(k^{*}_{N}/N)}}+o(1)

(396)

(In fact because of the proof of
D.2
the
o(1)
factor could be strengthened to
\mathcal{O}(1/N)
.)
Recalling that
\nu^{*}
represents a probability,
\nu^{*}\in[0,1)
and introducing the following quantities:

\alpha_{A}=I^{\prime}_{A}(x_{0}^{*})/\beta,\quad\alpha_{B}=I^{\prime}_{B}(x_{0}^{*})/\beta

(397)

we get the following bounds for
P^{*}_{N}
:

-\alpha_{A}\beta\lesssim\log\left(\frac{P^{*}_{N}}{1-P^{*}_{N}}\right)-\log\left(\frac{1-e^{\alpha_{B}\beta}}{1-e^{-\alpha_{A}\beta}}\right)\lesssim-\alpha_{B}\beta

(398)

where we used asymptotic notation
o()
and
\lesssim
in accordance with
[
9
]
.
The results, in a more straightforward notation, look the following:

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\ \log\left(\frac{P^{*}_{N}}{1-P^{*}_{N}}\right)\geq\log\left(\frac{1-e^{\alpha_{B}\beta}}{1-e^{-\alpha_{A}\beta}}\right)-\alpha_{A}\beta

(399)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\ \log\left(\frac{P^{*}_{N}}{1-P^{*}_{N}}\right)\leq\log\left(\frac{1-e^{\alpha_{B}\beta}}{1-e^{-\alpha_{A}\beta}}\right)-\alpha_{B}\beta

(400)

Conjecture
4.1
states that these upper and lower bounds are sharp, i.e. the inequalities become equalities:

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\ \log\left(\frac{P^{*}_{N}}{1-P^{*}_{N}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{1-e^{\alpha_{B}\beta}}{1-e^{-\alpha_{A}\beta}}\right)-\alpha_{A}\beta

(401)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\ \log\left(\frac{P^{*}_{N}}{1-P^{*}_{N}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{1-e^{\alpha_{B}\beta}}{1-e^{-\alpha_{A}\beta}}\right)-\alpha_{B}\beta

(402)

D.2
Binomial Bayesian limiting prior approximation

The derivation supports conjecture
4.2
, but it starts with an approximation appearing in remark
Remark
.

D.2.1
Notation

Recalling eq. (
153
):

p_{k}(A)=\binom{N}{k}x_{A}^{k}(1-x_{A})^{N-k},\quad p_{k}(B)=\binom{N}{k}x_{B}^{k}(1-x_{B})^{N-k}

(403)

We introduce some notation useful in the continuum (
N\to\infty
) limit:

x=k/N,\quad k=[xN]

(404)

To express
p^{\prime*}_{k}
with continuum quantities, we introduce a new variable
\tau
:

p^{\prime*}_{k}=\frac{P\ p_{k}(A)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)p_{k}(B)}

(405)

\tau=-\log\left(\frac{p^{\prime*}_{k}}{1-p^{\prime*}_{k}}\right)

(406)

\tau=-\log\left(\frac{P}{1-P}\right)-\log\left(\frac{x_{A}^{k}(1-x_{A})^{N-k}}{x_{B}^{k}(1-x_{B})^{N-k}}\right)

(407)

introducing a positive “slope” quantity (
359
):

\beta=\log\left(\frac{x_{B}(1-x_{A})}{x_{A}(1-x_{B})}\right)

(408)

and log-odds
\vartheta
, instead of
P
:

\vartheta=\log\left(\frac{P}{1-P}\right),\quad P=\frac{e^{\vartheta}}{1+e^{\vartheta}}

(409)

\tau
can be expressed as a linear function of
x
:

\tau=-\vartheta+N\beta(x-x_{0}^{*})

(410)

where
x_{0}^{*}
equals to:

x_{0}^{*}=\log\left(\frac{1-x_{A}}{1-x_{B}}\right)/\beta=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1-x_{A}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}{\log\left(\frac{x_{B}(1-x_{A})}{x_{A}(1-x_{B})}\right)}

(411)

Remarkably, this is the same expression as equation (
379
).
Conversely
x
can be expressed by
\tau
:

x=x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}

(412)

D.2.2
Expression for the growth factor difference

In the Binomial case, we can simplify the expression from eq. (
257
) and (
258
):

\Delta G_{A}(P)=\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)\log\left(\frac{P\ p_{k}(A)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}\right)

(413)

\Delta G_{B}(P)=\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)\log\left(\frac{(1-P)p_{k}(B)}{P\ p_{k}(A)+(1-P)\ p_{k}(B)}\right)

(414)

\Delta G_{A}(P)=\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)\log\left(p^{\prime*}_{k}\right)

(415)

\Delta G_{B}(P)=\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)\log\left(1-p^{\prime*}_{k}\right)

(416)

Loss function:

The expressions
\log\left(p^{\prime*}_{k}\right)
,
\log\left(1-p^{\prime*}_{k}\right)
can be interpreted as a loss function and expressed as functions of
\tau
:

L_{A}(\tau)=\log\left(p^{\prime*}_{k}\right)=\log\left(\frac{e^{-\tau}}{1+e^{-\tau}}\right)=-\tau-\log\left(1+e^{-\tau}\right)

(417)

L_{B}(\tau)=\log\left(1-p^{\prime*}_{k}\right)=\log\left(\frac{e^{\tau}}{1+e^{\tau}}\right)=\tau-\log\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)

(418)

\Delta G_{\theta}(P)=\sum_{k}p_{k}(\theta)L_{\theta}(\tau_{k}),\quad\theta\in\{A,B\}

(419)

D.2.3
Approximations

Stirling’s formula:

In the continuum limit, the discrete probability distributions
p_{k}(A)
and
p_{k}(B)
can be approximated by a density function, using Stirling’s formula
[
164
]

57
57

57

for notation see also a paper on Large deviation principle
[
189
]
:

\frac{p_{[Nx]}(\theta)}{1/N}\approx f_{\theta}(x)=\frac{\sqrt{N}}{\sqrt{2\pi x(1-x)}}e^{-NI(x,x_{\theta})},\quad\theta\in\{A,B\}

(420)

where
I(x,x_{\theta})=I_{\theta}(x)
is the so called rate function introduced in (
355
).

I(x,x_{\theta})=I_{\theta}(x)=x\log\left(\frac{x}{x_{\theta}}\right)+(1-x)\log\left(\frac{1-x}{1-x_{\theta}}\right)

(421)

This results in an approximate formula for the growth rate difference:

\Delta G_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S}}(\vartheta)=\sum_{k}\frac{1}{N}f_{\theta}(k/N)L_{\theta}(\tau_{k})

(422)

Euler-Maclaurin formula:

To transform the summation to an analytically more tractable integration, we can use the Euler-Maclaurin formula
[
113
,
137
]
:

\Delta G_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,E}}(\vartheta)=\int_{0}^{1}f_{\theta}(x)L_{\theta}(\tau(x))dx

(423)

Change of variables:

Finally we change the integration variable
x
to
\tau
, and substitute the limits of integration
[-\vartheta-N\beta x_{0}^{*},-\vartheta+N\beta(1-x_{0}^{*})]
with
(-\infty,\infty)
:

\Delta G_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,E,C}}(\vartheta)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\theta}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L_{\theta}(\tau)\frac{d\tau}{N\beta}

(424)

Approximated gains:

In the next sections, we will perform calculations based on these approximated expressions:

\Delta G_{A}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta)=\Delta G_{A}^{\mathrm{S,E,C}}(\vartheta)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{N\beta}f_{A}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L_{A}(\tau)d\tau

(425)

\Delta G_{B}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta)=\Delta G_{B}^{\mathrm{S,E,C}}(\vartheta)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{N\beta}f_{B}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L_{B}(\tau)d\tau

(426)

D.2.4
Performing the Integral

Notations:

Exponent related to the growth rate difference decrease:

\varepsilon(x_{A},x_{B})=I(x_{0}^{*},x_{A})=I(x_{0}^{*},x_{B})>0

(427)

A symmetric expression for
\varepsilon(x_{A},x_{B})
can look like:

\varepsilon(x_{A},x_{B})=x_{0}^{*}\log\left(\frac{x_{0}^{*}}{\sqrt{x_{A}x_{B}}}\right)+(1-x_{0}^{*})\log\left(\frac{1-x_{0}^{*}}{\sqrt{(1-x_{A})(1-x_{B})}}\right)

(428)

Further exponents:

\alpha_{A}=I^{\prime}_{A}(x_{0}^{*})/\beta>0,\quad\alpha_{B}=I^{\prime}_{B}(x_{0}^{*})/\beta<0

(429)

Leading order expression:

By taking the first order Taylor expansion of
I_{\theta}(x)
around
x_{0}^{*}
,
I_{\theta}(x)=\varepsilon(x_{A},x_{B})+I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x_{0}^{*})(x-x_{0}^{*})+\mathcal{O}((x-x_{0}^{*})^{2})
we get:

\Delta G_{\theta}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta)=\frac{e^{-\alpha_{\theta}\vartheta}}{\sqrt{2\pi x_{0}^{*}(1-x_{0}^{*})}}\frac{e^{-N\varepsilon(x_{A},x_{B})}}{\sqrt{N}\beta}\left(C_{\theta}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)\right)

(430)

Where the constants
C_{A}
and
C_{B}
are the results of the following integrals:

C_{A}=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha_{A}\tau}(\tau+\log\left(1+e^{-\tau}\right))d\tau

(431)

C_{B}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha_{B}\tau}(\tau-\log\left(1+e^{\tau}\right))d\tau

(432)

Remarkably, these integrals can be expressed in closed form:

C_{A}=-\frac{\pi}{\alpha_{A}}\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\alpha_{A})},\quad\text{if }\alpha_{A}\in(0,1)

(433)

C_{B}=-\frac{\pi}{\alpha_{B}}\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\alpha_{B})},\quad\text{if }\alpha_{B}\in(-1,0)

(434)

The results can be obtained by symbolic integration
[
158
]
available in
Mathematica 13.0
, and are formally calculated in Section
D.2.7
.

D.2.5
Obtaining the prior

In equilibrium, the growth rate difference has to be the same for scenarios A and B.
The equation for the approximated equilibrium quantity
\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}
, looks the following:

\Delta G_{A}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit})=\Delta G_{B}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit})

(435)

which simplifies to:

\frac{e^{-\alpha_{A}\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}}}{\alpha_{A}\sin(\pi\alpha_{A})}=\frac{e^{-\alpha_{B}\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}}}{\alpha_{B}\sin(\pi\alpha_{B})}

(436)

\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}=\frac{\log\left(\frac{\alpha_{B}\sin(\pi\alpha_{B})}{\alpha_{A}\sin(\pi\alpha_{A})}\right)}{\alpha_{A}-\alpha_{B}}

(437)

D.2.6
Simplifying the result

Notation:

x_{0}^{*}=\log\left(\frac{1-x_{A}}{1-x_{B}}\right)/\beta,\quad 1-x_{0}^{*}=\log\left(\frac{x_{B}}{x_{A}}\right)/\beta

(438)

Recalling
I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x)
from (
357
):

I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x)=\log\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\frac{1-x_{\theta}}{x_{\theta}}\right)

(439)

\alpha_{\theta}=I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x)/\beta

(440)

\alpha_{\theta}=\frac{\log\left(\frac{x_{0}^{*}}{1-x_{0}^{*}}\frac{1-x_{\theta}}{x_{\theta}}\right)}{\beta}=\frac{\log\left(\frac{x_{0}^{*}}{1-x_{0}^{*}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{1-x_{\theta}}{x_{\theta}}\right)}{\beta}

(441)

Identities:

First, we can observe that:

\alpha_{A}-\alpha_{B}=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1-x_{A}}{x_{A}}\right)-\log\left(\frac{1-x_{B}}{x_{B}}\right)}{\beta}=1

(442)

Therefore, the expression, containing sinus terms, simplifies to:

\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha_{B})}{\sin(\pi\alpha_{A})}=\frac{\sin(\pi(\alpha_{A}-1))}{\sin(\pi\alpha_{A})}=-1

(443)

This simplifies the result:

\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}=\log\left(\frac{-\alpha_{B}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)=\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}=\log\left(\frac{\log\left(\frac{1-x_{0}^{*}}{x_{0}^{*}}\frac{x_{B}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}{\log\left(\frac{x_{0}^{*}}{1-x_{0}^{*}}\frac{1-x_{A}}{x_{A}}\right)}\right)

(444)

Prior probability:

Recalling equation (
409
), we can express the approximated prior probability
P^{*}_{\clubsuit}
:

P^{*}_{\clubsuit}=\frac{e^{\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}}}{1+e^{\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}}}=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1-x_{0}^{*}}{x_{0}^{*}}\frac{x_{B}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}{\log\left(\frac{x_{0}^{*}}{1-x_{0}^{*}}\frac{1-x_{A}}{x_{A}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{1-x_{0}^{*}}{x_{0}^{*}}\frac{x_{B}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}

(445)

or

P^{*}_{\clubsuit}=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1-x_{0}^{*}}{x_{0}^{*}}\frac{x_{B}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}{\beta}=\frac{\log\left(\frac{1-x_{0}^{*}}{x_{0}^{*}}\frac{x_{B}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}{\log\left(\frac{x_{B}}{x_{A}}\frac{1-x_{A}}{1-x_{B}}\right)}

(446)

Remark
Remark
states that for
0<x_{A}<x_{B}<1
values,

P^{\approx}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}(x_{A},x_{B})=P^{*}_{\clubsuit}(x_{A},x_{B})

(447)

is a “good approximation” of
P_{N}^{*}
as
N\to\infty
.

D.2.7
Formal calculation of the Integrals

In this section we derive the expressions (
433
),(
434
) from (
431
),(
432
):

Lemma D.3
.

J=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{at}}{\cosh(t)}dt=\frac{\pi}{\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}a\right)},\quad\text{if }a\in(-1,1)

(448)

∎

Proof.

The complex function
1/\cosh(z):\mathbb{C}\mapsto\mathbb{C}
is antiperiodic on the imaginary axis, with antiperiod
\pi

Figure 42
:
Complex plot
[
157
]
of
f_{0}:\mathbb{C}\mapsto\mathbb{C},f_{0}(z)=1/\cosh(z)
.

\frac{1}{\cosh(z+i\pi)}=\frac{2}{e^{z+i\pi}+e^{-z-i\pi}}=\frac{2}{e^{i\pi}e^{z}+e^{-i\pi}e^{-z}}=\frac{-2}{e^{z}+e^{-z}}=\frac{-1}{\cosh(z)}

(449)

and the absolute value of
|1/\cosh(x+iy)|
goes to
0
as
|x|
tends to infinity:

\frac{1}{|\cosh(z)|}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\cosh(z)\overline{\cosh(z)}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\cosh(z)\cosh(\overline{z})}}

(450)

\frac{1}{|\cosh(x+iy)|^{2}}=\frac{4}{(e^{x+iy}+e^{-x-iy})(e^{x-iy}+e^{-x+iy})}

(451)

\frac{1}{|\cosh(x+iy)|^{2}}=\frac{4}{e^{2x}+e^{-2iy}+e^{2iy}+e^{-2x}}

(452)

\frac{1}{|\cosh(x+iy)|^{2}}=\frac{4}{e^{2x}+2\cos(2y)+e^{-2x}}

(453)

meaning that

\frac{1}{|\cosh(x+iy)|}<2e^{-|x|}

(454)

Now we can define the parametric complex function:

f_{a}(z)=\frac{e^{az}}{\cosh(z)},\quad a\in\mathbb{R}

(455)

This function is shifted by an extra
a
-dependent phase, while
z
is shifted by
i\pi
in the imaginary axis:

f_{a}(z+i\pi)=-e^{ia\pi}f_{a}(z)

(456)

and due to eq. (
454
):

|f_{a}(z)|<2e^{-|x|+ax}

(457)

which goes to 0 as
|x|\to\infty
, if
a\in(-1,1)
.

f_{a}(z)
has poles only at
\{i\pi/2+i\pi k\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and is analytical in every other point.

Now, we can apply Cauchy’s residue theorem on the complex contour integral:

\begin{split}\Gamma_{1}(T)=[-T,T],&\quad\Gamma_{2}(T)=[T,T+i\pi]\\
\Gamma_{3}(T)=[T+i\pi,-T+i\pi],&\quad\Gamma_{4}(T)=[-T+i\pi,-T]\end{split}

(458)

\Gamma(T)=(\Gamma_{1}(T),\Gamma_{2}(T),\Gamma_{3}(T),\Gamma_{4}(T))

(459)

\oint_{\Gamma(T)}f_{a}(z)dz=2\pi i\ \mathrm{Res}(f_{a},i\pi/2)

(460)

\lim_{T\to\infty}\oint_{\Gamma(T)}f_{a}(z)dz=2\pi i\ \mathrm{Res}(f_{a},i\pi/2)

(461)

J+0+(-1)(-e^{ia\pi}J)+0=2\pi i\ \mathrm{Res}(f_{a},i\pi/2)

(462)

To determine the residue of
f_{a}(z)
, we can approximate the expression in the denominator around
z_{0}=i\pi/2
:

\cosh(z)=0+\sinh(i\pi/2)(z-i\pi/2)+\mathcal{O}((z-i\pi/2)^{2})

(463)

\cosh(z)=0+i(z-i\pi/2)+\mathcal{O}((z-i\pi/2)^{2})

(464)

meaning that the residue of
f_{a}(z)
at
z_{0}=i\pi/2
equals to:

\mathrm{Res}(f_{a},i\pi/2)=\frac{e^{ia\pi/2}}{i}

(465)

resulting:

J+(-1)(-e^{ia\pi}J)=2\pi i\frac{e^{ia\pi/2}}{i}

(466)

J=2\pi\frac{e^{ia\pi/2}}{1+e^{ia\pi}}=2\pi\frac{1}{e^{-ia\pi/2}+e^{ia\pi/2}}=\frac{\pi}{\cos(\pi a/2)}

(467)

∎

Similar integrals can also be found in standard complex analysis textbooks such as
[
43
]
.

Lemma D.4
.

C(\alpha)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha\tau}(\tau+\log\left(1+e^{-\tau}\right))d\tau=\frac{\pi}{\alpha}\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\alpha)},\quad\text{if }\alpha\in(0,1)

(468)

∎

Proof.

Integration by parts results (because the boundary terms go to 0 if
\alpha\in(0,1)
):

C=0-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\alpha\tau}}{-\alpha}\left(1-\frac{e^{-\tau}}{1+e^{-\tau}}\right)d\tau

(469)

C=\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\alpha\tau}}{1+e^{-\tau}}d\tau

(470)

C=\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{(1/2-\alpha)\tau}}{e^{\tau/2}+e^{-\tau/2}}d\tau

(471)

Substituting the variables:

t=\tau/2,\quad\tau=2t

(472)

a=(1/2-\alpha)\ 2=1-2\alpha,\quad a\in(-1,1)

(473)

C=\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{at}}{2\cosh{t}}2dt

(474)

using lemma
D.3
we get:

C=\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{\pi}{\cos(\pi a/2)}

(475)

C=\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{\pi}{\cos(\pi/2-\pi\alpha)}

(476)

C=\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}

(477)

∎

with lemma
D.4
the expressions (
433
),(
434
) follow from (
431
),(
432
).

Remark
.

The function
C(\alpha)
can be extended to complex
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
values, if
0<\Re(\alpha)<1
.

∎

For further use we introduce
C_{A}(.),C_{B}(.)
functions as well:

C_{A}(\alpha_{A})=-\frac{\pi}{\alpha_{A}}\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\alpha_{A})},\quad\text{if }\Re(\alpha_{A})\in(0,1)

(478)

C_{B}(\alpha_{B})=-\frac{\pi}{\alpha_{B}}\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\alpha_{B})},\quad\text{if }\Re(\alpha_{B})\in(-1,0)

(479)

D.3
Asymptotic expansion of Binomial Bayesian prior approximation

The derivation supports the approximation in remark
Remark
.

Notation:

Normalized growth factor difference:

\Delta g_{\theta}^{\clubsuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)=\frac{\Delta G_{\theta}^{\clubsuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi x_{0}^{*}(1-x_{0}^{*})}}\frac{e^{-N\varepsilon(x_{A},x_{B})}}{\sqrt{N}\beta}}

(480)

First-order asymptotic expansion:

We need to solve the following equation to obtain a first-order asymptotic expansion:

\Delta g_{A}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}+\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}/N)-\Delta g_{B}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}+\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}/N)=0+\mathcal{O}(1/N^{2})

(481)

D.3.1
Ingredients

Stirling series:

For further coefficients and more context, see
[
25
,
136
,
80
]

\log(n!)=n\log(n)-n+\frac{1}{2}\log(n)+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)+\frac{1}{12}\frac{1}{n}+\mathcal{O}(1/n^{2})

(482)

Taylor expansion:

I_{\theta}(x)=I_{\theta}(x_{0}^{*})+I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x)(x-x_{0}^{*})+\frac{1}{2}I^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}(x)(x-x_{0}^{*})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((x-x_{0}^{*})^{3})

(483)

Perturbative approach:

For appropriate
X(\tau)
functions:

e^{-\alpha\tau+\frac{1}{N}X(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(1/N^{2})}=e^{-\alpha\tau}\left(1+\frac{1}{N}X(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(1/N^{2})\right)

(484)

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
monomials of
\tau
:

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha\tau}\tau^{n}L(\tau)d\tau=(-1)^{n}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\alpha^{n}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha\tau}L(\tau)d\tau

(485)

D.3.2
Derivation

First-order approximation of density function:

f_{\theta}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi N}}e^{-NI_{\theta}(x)+n(x)+\frac{1}{N}b_{1}(x)}

(486)

where:

n(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\log(x(1-x)),\quad b_{1}(x)=\frac{1}{12}\left(1-\frac{1}{x}-\frac{1}{1-x}\right)

(487)

Introducing:

J_{\theta}(x)=-NI_{\theta}(x)+n(x)+\frac{1}{N}b_{1}(x)

(488)

and

x=x_{0}^{*}+\frac{1}{N}\Delta x+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}

(489)

we get:

\begin{split}J_{\theta}(x)=&-NI_{\theta}(x_{0}^{*})+\\
&n(x_{0}^{*})-I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x_{0}^{*})\Delta x+\\
&\frac{1}{N}\left(b_{1}(x_{0}^{*})+n^{\prime}(x_{0}^{*})\Delta x-I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x_{0}^{*})\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}-\frac{1}{2}I^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}(x_{0}^{*})(\Delta x)^{2}\right)+\\
&\mathcal{O}(1/N^{2})\end{split}

(490)

Introducing:

f_{0}^{*}=f_{A}(x_{0}^{*})=f_{B}(x_{0}^{*})

(491)

we get an expression for the “normalized” density function:

\frac{f_{\theta}(x)}{f_{0}^{*}}=e^{-I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x_{0}^{*})\Delta x}\left(1+\frac{1}{N}\left(n^{\prime}(x_{0}^{*})\Delta x-I^{\prime}_{\theta}(x_{0}^{*})\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}-\frac{1}{2}I^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}(x_{0}^{*})(\Delta x)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1/N^{2})\right)

(492)

Expressing the growth rates:

\Delta x=\frac{\tau+\vartheta}{\beta}

(493)

We can introduce a curvature factor euqal to (
358
):

\kappa(x)=I^{\prime\prime}_{A}(x)=I^{\prime\prime}_{B}(x)=\frac{1}{x}+\frac{1}{1-x}=\frac{1}{x(1-x)}

(494)

After solving the equation perturbatively
[
71
]
, up to the first term in
1/N
expansion:

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{A}(x(\tau))L_{A}(\tau)d\tau=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{B}(x(\tau))L_{B}(\tau)d\tau

(495)

and solving for
we get:

\begin{split}\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}=&\left(\frac{n^{\prime}(x_{0}^{*})}{\beta}-\frac{\kappa(x_{0}^{*})\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{C_{B}^{\prime}(\alpha_{B})}{C_{B}(\alpha_{B})}-\frac{C_{A}^{\prime}(\alpha_{A})}{C_{A}(\alpha_{A})}\right)+\\
&\frac{1}{2}\frac{\kappa(x_{0}^{*})}{\beta^{2}}\left(\frac{C_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{B})}{C_{B}(\alpha_{B})}-\frac{C_{A}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{A})}{C_{A}(\alpha_{A})}\right)\end{split}

(496)

After simplifications:

\left(\frac{C_{B}^{\prime}(\alpha_{B})}{C_{B}(\alpha_{B})}-\frac{C_{A}^{\prime}(\alpha_{A})}{C_{A}(\alpha_{A})}\right)=\frac{d}{d\alpha}\log\left(\frac{C_{B}(\alpha-1)}{C_{A}(\alpha)}\right)\Biggr|_{\alpha=\alpha_{A}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}(1-\alpha_{A})}

(497)

We can obtain an explicit expression for the first-order asymptotic approximation term:

\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}(1-\alpha_{A})}\left(\frac{n^{\prime}(x_{0}^{*})}{\beta}-\frac{\kappa(x_{0}^{*})\vartheta^{*}_{\clubsuit}}{\beta^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\kappa(x_{0}^{*})}{\beta^{2}}\left(\frac{C_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{B})}{C_{B}(\alpha_{B})}-\frac{C_{A}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{A})}{C_{A}(\alpha_{A})}\right)

(498)

\left(\frac{C_{B}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{B})}{C_{B}(\alpha_{B})}-\frac{C_{A}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{A})}{C_{A}(\alpha_{A})}\right)=-2\frac{1-2\alpha_{A}}{\alpha_{A}^{2}(1-\alpha_{A})^{2}}-\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{A}(1-\alpha_{A})}\frac{\cos(\pi\alpha_{A})}{\sin(\pi\alpha_{A})}

(499)

D.4
Limiting prior approximation for general Statistical games

A similar calculation can be performed for general statistical games
5.1
with an isoelastic utility function.
In this case, we can start the derivation by recalling the isoelastic equilibrium splitting ratios (
189
):

p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}=\frac{(P\ p_{k}(A))^{1/\gamma}}{(P\ p_{k}(A))^{1/\gamma}+((1-P)p_{k}(B))^{1/\gamma}}

(500)

Introducing variables similar to the ones in Section
D.2
:

\tau=-\log\left(\frac{p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}}{1-p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}}\right)

(501)

\tau=(-\vartheta+N\beta(x-x_{0}^{*}))/\gamma

(502)

x=x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\gamma\ \tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}

(503)

Recalling the isoelastic utility function from equation (
78
):

u_{\gamma}(c)=\frac{c^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(504)

We define the expected utilities for each scenario:

U_{A}(P)=\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)u_{\gamma}\left(p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}\right)

(505)

U_{B}(P)=\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)u_{\gamma}\left(1-p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}\right)

(506)

Expressions for the isoelastic loss functions:

L^{\gamma}_{A}(\tau)=u_{\gamma}\left(p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}\right)=\frac{\left(\frac{e^{-\tau}}{1+e^{-\tau}}\right)^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(507)

L^{\gamma}_{B}(\tau)=u_{\gamma}\left(1-p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}\right)=\frac{\left(\frac{e^{\tau}}{1+e^{\tau}}\right)^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(508)

The expected utilities expressed by the loss functions:

U_{\theta}(P)=\sum_{k}p_{k}(\theta)L^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\tau_{k}),\quad\theta\in\{A,B\}

(509)

After making the same approximations as in Section
D.2.3
we get:

U_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,E,C}}(\vartheta)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\theta}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\gamma\ \tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\tau)\frac{\gamma}{N\beta}d\tau

(510)

which can be expressed as:

U_{\theta}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta)=\frac{e^{-\alpha_{\theta}\vartheta}}{\sqrt{2\pi x_{0}^{*}(1-x_{0}^{*})}}\frac{e^{-N\varepsilon(x_{A},x_{B})}}{\sqrt{N}\beta}\left(C^{\gamma}_{\theta}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)\right)

(511)

where
C^{\gamma}_{A}
,
C^{\gamma}_{B}
are defined by the following integrals:

C^{\gamma}_{A}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma\alpha_{A}\tau}\frac{\left(\frac{e^{-\tau}}{1+e^{-\tau}}\right)^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}\gamma d\tau

(512)

C^{\gamma}_{B}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma\alpha_{B}\tau}\frac{\left(\frac{e^{\tau}}{1+e^{\tau}}\right)^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}\gamma d\tau

(513)

The result can be obtained by the symbolic integration
[
158
]
available in
Mathematica 13.0
, and are formally calculated in Section
D.4.2
.

C^{\gamma}_{A}=\gamma\frac{\Gamma(-\alpha_{A}\gamma)\Gamma(1-\gamma(1-\alpha_{A}))}{\Gamma(2-\gamma)}

(514)

if
0<\Re(\gamma\alpha_{A})<1
and
\Re(\gamma(1-\alpha_{A}))<1

C^{\gamma}_{B}=\gamma\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{B}\gamma)\Gamma(1-\gamma(1+\alpha_{B}))}{\Gamma(2-\gamma)}

(515)

if
-1<\Re(\gamma\alpha_{B})<0
and
\Re(\gamma(1+\alpha_{B}))<1

Where
\Gamma(z)
is the Gamma function
[
173
,
22
,
136
,
119
,
12
]
.

Gamma function identities:

Euler’s Reflection formula
[
173
]
:

\Gamma(z)\Gamma(1-z)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi z)}

(516)

Recursion formula
[
173
]
:

\Gamma(z+1)=z\ \Gamma(z)

(517)

Rewriting the result:

After using the identities for Gamma functions, we can get:

C^{\gamma}_{A}=-\frac{\pi}{\alpha_{A}\sin(\pi\gamma\alpha_{A})}\frac{\Gamma(1-\gamma(1-\alpha_{A}))}{\Gamma(2-\gamma)\Gamma(\gamma\alpha_{A})}

(518)

C^{\gamma}_{B}=-\frac{\pi}{\alpha_{B}\sin(\pi\gamma\alpha_{B})}\frac{\Gamma(1-\gamma(1+\alpha_{B}))}{\Gamma(2-\gamma)\Gamma(-\gamma\alpha_{B})}

(519)

Alternatively, we can express these formulas by the Beta function
[
12
,
173
,
22
,
136
,
119
]
:

C^{\gamma}_{A}=-\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}}B(1-\gamma\alpha_{A},1-\gamma(1-\alpha_{A}))

(520)

C^{\gamma}_{B}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{B}}B(1-\gamma(-\alpha_{B}),1-\gamma(1+\alpha_{B}))

(521)

For further use we introduce
C^{\gamma}_{A}(.),C^{\gamma}_{B}(.)
functions as well:

C^{\gamma}_{A}(\alpha_{A})=-\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}}B(1-\gamma\alpha_{A},1-\gamma(1-\alpha_{A}))

(522)

C^{\gamma}_{B}(\alpha_{B})=\frac{1}{\alpha_{B}}B(1-\gamma(-\alpha_{B}),1-\gamma(1+\alpha_{B}))

(523)

Limiting prior approximation:

We need to solve the following equation up to the zeroth-order:

U_{A}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma,\clubsuit})=U_{B}^{\clubsuit}(\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma,\clubsuit})

(524)

Resulting in the equation:

e^{-\alpha_{A}\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma,\clubsuit}}C^{\gamma}_{A}=e^{-\alpha_{B}\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma,\clubsuit}}C^{\gamma}_{B}

(525)

Which has a simple solution for
\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma,\clubsuit}
:

\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma,\clubsuit}=\frac{\log\left(\frac{C^{\gamma}_{A}}{C^{\gamma}_{B}}\right)}{\alpha_{A}-\alpha_{B}}

(526)

The expression simplifies remarkably, because
\alpha_{A}-\alpha_{B}=1
:

\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma,\clubsuit}=\log\left(\frac{-\alpha_{B}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{1-\alpha_{A}}{\alpha_{A}}\right),\quad\text{if }\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}<\alpha_{A}<\frac{1}{\gamma}

(527)

Remark
.

\alpha_{A}\in(0,1)
, therefore there is a critical value of relative risk aversion parameter
\gamma
, under which a finite equilibrium log-odds approximation can be made.

\gamma\in(0,\gamma^{\overline{\diamond}}),\quad\gamma^{\overline{\diamond}}=2

(528)

For general
0<x_{A}<x_{B}<1
values, the critical maximal relative risk aversion can be expressed by
\alpha_{A}(x_{A},x_{B})
:

\gamma^{\diamond}(x_{A},x_{B})=\min\left(\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}(x_{A},x_{B})},\frac{1}{1-\alpha_{A}(x_{A},x_{B})}\right)

(529)

1<\gamma^{\diamond}(x_{A},x_{B})\leq 2

(530)

∎

Remark
.

The obtained approximation
\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma,\clubsuit}
in eq. (
526
) is the same for all
\gamma<\gamma^{\diamond}(x_{A},x_{B})
, i.e. independent of the relative risk aversion
\gamma
.

∎

D.4.1
Visualization of the critical relative risk aversion

(a)

3D plot

(b)

Density plot

(c)

Density plot

Figure 43
:
Critical relative risk aversion
\gamma^{\diamond}(x_{A},x_{B})
.
The colour coding is the same as in figures
45
,
46
.
(Contour lines show
0.01
difference, and goes from
1.00
to
1.99
.)

All the 3D plot
58
58

58

Resembling a
Canterbury cap
or Oxford University
women’s soft cap
. The plot also features elements that may remind one of a Pagoda roof
[
143
,
95
]
(such as
Chureito Pagoda
in Fujiyoshida, Japan).
, Density plot and Contour plot have been extended to the whole
x_{A},x_{B}\in(0,1)
domain (in accordance with (
55
)).

D.4.2
Formal calculation of the integrals

Using the Beta function:

Lemma D.5
.

For all
\Re(z_{1}),\Re(z_{2})>0

B(z_{1},z_{2})=\int_{0}^{1}p^{z_{1}-1}(1-p)^{z_{2}-1}dp

(531)

where
B(z_{1},z_{2})
is the Beta function
[
12
,
173
,
22
,
136
,
119
]
, which can be expressed by the Gamma function:

B(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{\Gamma(z_{1})\Gamma(z_{2})}{\Gamma(z_{1}+z_{2})}

(532)

∎

We aim to calculate the following integral:

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma\alpha\tau}\frac{\left(\frac{e^{-\tau}}{1+e^{-\tau}}\right)^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}\gamma d\tau

(533)

After rearranging the terms, we get:

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma\alpha\tau}\frac{\left(\frac{1}{1+e^{\tau}}\right)^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}\gamma d\tau

(534)

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{-\gamma\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma\alpha\tau}\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}(-(1-\gamma))\frac{e^{\tau}}{\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{2-\gamma}}d\tau

(535)

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma\alpha\tau}\frac{e^{\tau}}{\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{2-\gamma}}d\tau

(536)

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\left(e^{\tau}\right)^{1-\gamma\alpha}}{\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{2-\gamma}}d\tau

(537)

After this, we can make the following change of variable:

\tau=\log\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right),\quad e^{\tau}=\frac{p}{1-p},\quad d\tau=\frac{dp}{p(1-p)}

(538)

Rewriting the terms to the
p
variable instead of
\tau
results in:

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{p^{1-\gamma\alpha}\left(1-p\right)^{\gamma\alpha-1}}{\left(1+\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{2-\gamma}}\frac{dp}{p(1-p)}

(539)

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}p^{1-\gamma\alpha}\left(1-p\right)^{\gamma\alpha-1+2-\gamma}\frac{dp}{p(1-p)}

(540)

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}p^{1-\gamma\alpha}\left(1-p\right)^{1-\gamma(1-\alpha)}\frac{dp}{p(1-p)}

(541)

Which is, by definition (or by recalling lemma
D.5
):

\boxed{C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}B(1-\gamma\alpha,1-\gamma(1-\alpha))}

(542)

or expressed by Gamma functions:

\boxed{C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{\Gamma(1-\gamma\alpha)\Gamma(1-\gamma(1-\alpha))}{\Gamma(2-\gamma)}}

(543)

Using Ramanujan’s formula:

Lemma D.6
.

For
a>0

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Gamma(a+it)\Gamma(a-it)e^{-i\xi t}dt=\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(a)\Gamma\left(a+\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\cosh\left(\frac{\xi}{2}\right)\right)^{-2a}

(544)

See the proof in
[
28
]
, and Ramanujan’s formula in
[
151
,
12
]
.

∎

The right-hand side of equation (
544
) can be rewritten according to the Legendre’s Duplication formula for the Gamma function
[
8
,
28
]
:

\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(2a)=2^{2a-1}\Gamma(a)\Gamma\left(a+\frac{1}{2}\right)

(545)

Resulting in:

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Gamma(a+it)\Gamma(a-it)e^{-i\xi t}dt=\frac{2\pi\Gamma(2a)}{4^{a}\left(\cosh\left(\frac{\xi}{2}\right)\right)^{2a}}

(546)

Performing an inverse Fourier transformation, we get the following formula:

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{2\pi\Gamma(2a)}{4^{a}\left(\cosh\left(\frac{\xi}{2}\right)\right)^{2a}}e^{i\xi t}d\xi=\Gamma(a+it)\Gamma(a-it)

(547)

After rearranging the terms and simplification:

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\left(\cosh\left(\frac{\xi}{2}\right)\right)^{2a}}e^{i\xi t}d\xi=4^{a}\frac{\Gamma(a+it)\Gamma(a-it)}{{\Gamma(2a)}}

(548)

The formula can be analytically continued to complex values of
t\in\mathbb{C}
, if
-a<\Im(t)<a
.

Recalling equation (
537
) for a rearranged version of the original
C^{\gamma}(\alpha)
expression in (
533
) gives:

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\left(e^{\tau}\right)^{1-\gamma\alpha}}{\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{2-\gamma}}d\tau=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{\tau(1-\gamma\alpha)}e^{-\tau(1-\gamma/2)}}{\left(e^{\tau/2}+e^{-\tau/2}\right)^{2-\gamma}}d\tau

(549)

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{1}{2^{2-\gamma}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{\tau(\gamma(1/2-\alpha))}}{\left(\cosh(\tau/2)\right)^{2-\gamma}}d\tau

(550)

By choosing the following parameters:

a=1-\frac{\gamma}{2},\quad t=\frac{1}{i}\gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-\alpha\right)

(551)

and recalling equation (
550
) and (
548
) we get:

C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{1}{2^{2-\gamma}}4^{1-\gamma/2}\frac{\Gamma(1-\gamma\alpha)\Gamma(1-\gamma(1-\alpha))}{\Gamma(2-\gamma)}

(552)

\boxed{C^{\gamma}(\alpha)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{\Gamma(1-\gamma\alpha)\Gamma(1-\gamma(1-\alpha))}{\Gamma(2-\gamma)}}

(553)

D.4.3
Asymptotic expansion of general statistical game prior approximation

After performing a similar calculation as in Section
D.3
, we get the following
explicit expression for the first-order asymptotic approximation term
for general isoelastic statistical games
\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}_{\gamma}
:

\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}(1-\alpha_{A})}\left(\frac{n^{\prime}(x_{0}^{*})}{\beta}-\frac{\kappa(x_{0}^{*})\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma,\clubsuit}}{\beta^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\kappa(x_{0}^{*})}{\beta^{2}}\left(\frac{{C_{B}^{\gamma}}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{B})}{C_{B}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{B})}-\frac{{C_{A}^{\gamma}}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{A})}{C_{A}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{A})}\right)

(554)

\left(\frac{{C_{B}^{\gamma}}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{B})}{C_{B}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{B})}-\frac{{C_{A}^{\gamma}}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{A})}{C_{A}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{A})}\right)=-2\frac{1-2\alpha_{A}}{\alpha_{A}^{2}(1-\alpha_{A})^{2}}-2\gamma\frac{\psi(1-\gamma\alpha_{A})-\psi(1-\gamma(1-\alpha_{A}))}{\alpha_{A}(1-\alpha_{A})}

(555)

where
\psi(z)
is the Digamma function
[
173
,
136
,
119
,
12
]
, the logarithmic derivative of the Gamma function:

\psi(z)=\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(z)}{\Gamma(z)}

(556)

D.5
Binomial Bayesian limiting prior asymptotics

The derivation supports conjecture
4.2
.

Previously, in Section
D.2.3
, we used the Euler-Maclaurin formula to approximate the summation with integration.
However, we can relax this assumption and introduce a “Dirac comb” measure for integration, which mimics the discrete summation.

To approximate the integrals with respect to the “Dirac comb” measure, we will use the Poisson summation formula
[
181
,
205
]
.

D.5.1
Approximations

Stirling’s formula:

After applying Stirling’s formula in the same way as in Section
D.2.3
, we get:

\Delta G_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S}}(\vartheta)=\sum_{k}\frac{1}{N}f_{\theta}(k/N)L_{\theta}(\tau_{k})=\int_{0}^{1}f_{\theta}(x)L_{\theta}(\tau(x))\mathop{\text{ш}}(Nx)dx

(557)

Where we substituted the summation over the discrete variable
k
with an integration with a Dirac comb (tempered-) distribution
\mathop{\text{ш}}(x)
, which is a periodic Dirac
\delta
-function
[
181
,
7
]
grid:

\mathop{\text{ш}}(y)=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\delta(y-k)

(558)

From the scaling of Dirac
\delta
-function:
\delta(ax)=1/|a|\delta(x)

[
7
]
we have:

\mathop{\text{ш}}(Nx)=\sum_{k}\delta(Nx-k)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k}\delta(x-k/N)

(559)

which justifies equation (
557
).

Poisson summation formula:

Formally, we can substitute the Dirac comb with its Fourier series representation:

\mathop{\text{ш}}(y)=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\delta(y-k)=\sum_{m=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{2\pi imy}

(560)

resulting:

\Delta G_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,P}}(\vartheta)=\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{m}f_{\theta}(x)L_{\theta}(\tau(x))e^{2\pi imNx}dx

(561)

Change of variables:

We do the same variable changes as in Section
D.2.3
:

\Delta G_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,P,C}}(\vartheta)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}f_{\theta}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L_{\theta}(\tau)e^{2\pi im\left(Nx_{0}^{*}+(\tau+\vartheta)/\beta\right)}\frac{d\tau}{N\beta}

(562)

Interchanging summation and integration:

\Delta G_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,P,C,I}}(\vartheta)=\sum_{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\theta}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L_{\theta}(\tau)e^{2\pi im\left(Nx_{0}^{*}+(\tau+\vartheta)/\beta\right)}\frac{d\tau}{N\beta}

(563)

D.5.2
Phase dependent expression

By introducing a phase parameter:

\varphi=2\pi Nx_{0}^{*}\mod 2\pi

(564)

we get the following expression:

\begin{split}\Delta G_{\theta}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)&=\Delta G_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,P,C,I}}(\vartheta)\\
&=\sum_{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\theta}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L_{\theta}(\tau)e^{im\varphi+2\pi im(\tau+\vartheta)/\beta}\frac{d\tau}{N\beta}\end{split}

(565)

Introducing normalized growth factors:

\Delta g_{\theta}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)=\frac{\Delta G_{\theta}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi x_{0}^{*}(1-x_{0}^{*})}}\frac{e^{-N\varepsilon(x_{A},x_{B})}}{\sqrt{N}\beta}}

(566)

we can get the following approximation:

\Delta g_{\theta}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)=e^{-\alpha_{\theta}\vartheta}\sum_{m}C_{\theta}(\alpha_{\theta}-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\varphi+2\pi im\vartheta/\beta}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)

(567)

where we analytically continued the
C_{A}(.)
,
C_{B}(.)
functions in (
478
), (
479
).
We can solve the following equation up to the zeroth-order to get a phase-dependent asymptotic expression for the equilibrium log-odds
\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\spadesuit}
:

\Delta g_{A}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\spadesuit})=\Delta g_{B}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\spadesuit})

(568)

yielding the following implicit equation:

\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\spadesuit}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}-\alpha_{B}}\log\left(\frac{\sum_{m}C_{A}(\alpha_{A}-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\varphi+2\pi im\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\spadesuit}/\beta}}{\sum_{m}C_{B}(\alpha_{B}-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\varphi+2\pi im\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\spadesuit}/\beta}}\right)

(569)

Introducing:

\widetilde{C}_{\theta}(\alpha,\omega)=\sum_{m}C_{\theta}(\alpha-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\omega}

(570)

we can define a quantity:

\Xi^{\varphi}(\vartheta)=\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}-\alpha_{B}}\log\left(\frac{\widetilde{C}_{A}(\alpha_{A},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}{\widetilde{C}_{B}(\alpha_{B},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}\right)

(571)

The solution of equation (
569
) can be defined as a fixed point equation:

\boxed{\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\spadesuit}=\Xi^{\varphi}(\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\spadesuit})}

(572)

where:

\Xi^{\varphi}(\vartheta)=\log\left(\frac{\sum_{m}\frac{\pi}{\alpha-2\pi im/\beta}\frac{e^{im(\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}}{\sin(\pi\alpha-2\pi^{2}im/\beta)}}{\sum_{m}\frac{\pi}{1-\alpha+2\pi im/\beta}\frac{e^{im(\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}}{\sin(\pi\alpha+2\pi^{2}im/\beta)}}\right)

(573)

or alternatively:

\Xi^{\varphi}(\vartheta)=\log\left(\frac{\frac{\pi}{\alpha}\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}+2\ \Re\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\pi}{\alpha-2\pi im/\beta}\frac{e^{im(\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}}{\sin(\pi\alpha-2\pi^{2}im/\beta)}\right)}{\frac{\pi}{1-\alpha}\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}+2\ \Re\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\pi}{1-\alpha+2\pi im/\beta}\frac{e^{im(\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}}{\sin(\pi\alpha+2\pi^{2}im/\beta)}\right)}\right)

(574)

Statement of the conjecture:

Conjecture
4.2
claims that:

\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}=\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\spadesuit}

(575)

More precisely:

\vartheta^{*}_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})=\vartheta^{*,\varphi_{N}(x_{A},x_{B})}_{\spadesuit}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)

(576)

Remark
.

The limiting prior approximation
P^{\approx}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}
in (
447
), (
446
) and the related approximative log-odds
\vartheta^{\approx}_{\text{\raisebox{-0.27905pt}{\includegraphics[height]{img/logos/LoopedSquare.pdf}}}}
can be interpreted as the fixed point of (
572
) if we take the zeroth harmonic-order approximation of the expression (
574
)

∎

D.6
Limiting prior asymptotics for general Statistical games

A similar calculation can be performed for general statistical games
5.1
, with an isoelastic utility function.

D.6.1
Approximations

Stirling’s formula:

U_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S}}(\vartheta)=\sum_{k}\frac{1}{N}f_{\theta}(k/N)L^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\tau_{k})=\int_{0}^{1}f_{\theta}(x)L^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\tau(x))\mathop{\text{ш}}(Nx)dx

(577)

Poisson summation formula:

U_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,P}}(\vartheta)=\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{m}f_{\theta}(x)L^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\tau(x))e^{2\pi imNx}dx

(578)

Change of variables:

U_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,P,C}}(\vartheta)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}f_{\theta}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\gamma\ \tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\tau)e^{2\pi im\left(Nx_{0}^{*}+(\gamma\tau+\vartheta)/\beta\right)}\frac{\gamma}{N\beta}d\tau

(579)

Interchanging summation and integration:

U_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,P,C,I}}(\vartheta)=\sum_{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\theta}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\gamma\ \tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\tau)e^{2\pi im\left(Nx_{0}^{*}+(\gamma\tau+\vartheta)/\beta\right)}\frac{\gamma}{N\beta}d\tau

(580)

D.6.2
Phase dependent expression

\begin{split}U_{\theta}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)&=U_{\theta}^{\mathrm{S,P,C,I}}(\vartheta)\\
&=\sum_{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\theta}\left(x_{0}^{*}+\frac{\gamma\ \tau+\vartheta}{N\beta}\right)L^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\tau)e^{im\varphi+2\pi im(\gamma\tau+\vartheta)/\beta}\frac{\gamma}{N\beta}d\tau\end{split}

(581)

Introducing normalized expected utility:

u_{\theta}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)=\frac{U_{\theta}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi x_{0}^{*}(1-x_{0}^{*})}}\frac{e^{-N\varepsilon(x_{A},x_{B})}}{\sqrt{N}\beta}}

(582)

we can get the following approximation:

u_{\theta}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta)=e^{-\alpha_{\theta}\vartheta}\sum_{m}C^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\alpha_{\theta}-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\varphi+2\pi im\vartheta/\beta}+\mathcal{O}(1/N)

(583)

where we analytically continued the
C^{\gamma}_{A}(.)
,
C^{\gamma}_{B}(.)
functions in (
522
), (
523
).
We can solve the following equation up to the zeroth-order to get a phase-dependent asymptotic expression for the equilibrium log-odds
\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}
:

u_{A}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit})=u_{B}^{\spadesuit,\varphi}(\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit})

(584)

yielding the following implicit equation:

\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}-\alpha_{B}}\log\left(\frac{\sum_{m}C^{\gamma}_{A}(\alpha_{A}-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\varphi+2\pi im\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta}}{\sum_{m}C^{\gamma}_{B}(\alpha_{B}-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\varphi+2\pi im\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta}}\right)

(585)

Introducing:

\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\alpha,\omega)=\sum_{m}C^{\gamma}_{\theta}(\alpha-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\omega}

(586)

we can define a quantity:

\Xi_{\gamma}^{\varphi}(\vartheta)=\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}-\alpha_{B}}\log\left(\frac{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{A}(\alpha_{A},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{B}(\alpha_{B},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}\right)

(587)

The solution of equation (
585
) can be defined as a fixed point equation:

\boxed{\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}=\Xi_{\gamma}^{\varphi}(\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit})}

(588)

Remark
.

Typically, the implicit expression might have multiple solutions for smaller
\gamma
and large
\beta
.
However, it may have only one stable solution.
Further analysis is needed to explore the proposed implicit equation’s validity range.

∎

Remark
.

This phase-dependent asymptotic expression does depend on
\gamma
, signalling that it has the potential to interpolate between Binomial Fisher and Binomial Bayesian limiting priors.

∎

By taking the
\gamma\to 0
limit, we attempt to recover the Binomial Fisher limiting prior bounds in Section
401
,
402
.

D.6.3
Taking the
\gamma\to 0
limit

In this limit, the
C^{\gamma}_{A}(.)
,
C^{\gamma}_{B}(.)
functions in (
522
), (
523
) simplifies radically:

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}C_{A}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{A})=-\frac{1}{\alpha_{A}},\quad\lim_{\gamma\to 0}C_{B}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{B})=\frac{1}{\alpha_{B}}

(589)

Lemma D.7
.

For any
\omega\in(0,2\pi)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\sum_{m=-M}^{M}\frac{1}{a-im}e^{im}=\frac{2\pi}{e^{2\pi a}-1}e^{a\omega}

(590)

∎

Proof.

For any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
:

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{a\omega}e^{-i\omega m}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{e^{2\pi(a-im)}-1}{a-im}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{e^{2\pi a}-1}{a-im}

(591)

Therefore

c_{m}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{2\pi}{e^{2\pi a}-1}e^{a\omega}e^{-i\omega m}=\frac{1}{a-im}

(592)

According to the theory of Fourier series
[
203
,
62
,
103
,
205
]
59
59

59

in this case, for example Dini’s test can guarantee the pointwise convergence in all points, where the function is not discontinuous
, this implies that for
\omega\mod 2\pi\neq 0
:

\lim_{M\to\infty}\sum_{m=-M}^{M}c_{m}e^{i\omega m}=\frac{2\pi}{e^{2\pi a}-1}e^{a(\omega\mod 2\pi))}

(593)

∎

We can continue by evaluating the expression:

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{\sum_{m}C^{\gamma}_{A}(\alpha_{A}-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\omega}}{\sum_{m}C^{\gamma}_{B}(\alpha_{B}-2\pi im/\beta)e^{im\omega}}=\frac{\sum_{m}\frac{-1}{\alpha_{A}-2\pi im/\beta}e^{im\omega}}{\sum_{m}\frac{1}{\alpha_{B}-2\pi im/\beta}e^{im\omega}}

(594)

\frac{\sum_{m}\frac{-1}{\alpha_{A}-2\pi im/\beta}e^{im\omega}}{\sum_{m}\frac{1}{\alpha_{B}-2\pi im/\beta}e^{im\omega}}=\frac{\sum_{m}\frac{-1}{\frac{\alpha_{A}\beta}{2\pi}-im}e^{im\omega}}{\sum_{m}\frac{1}{\frac{\alpha_{B}\beta}{2\pi}-im}e^{im\omega}}

(595)

Using lemma
D.7
, and that
\alpha_{A}-\alpha_{B}=1
, we get:

\frac{\sum_{m}\frac{-1}{\frac{\alpha_{A}\beta}{2\pi}-im}e^{im\omega}}{\sum_{m}\frac{1}{\frac{\alpha_{B}\beta}{2\pi}-im}e^{im\omega}}=\frac{1-e^{\alpha_{B}\beta}}{e^{\alpha_{A}\beta}-1}e^{\frac{\beta}{2\pi}\omega}

(596)

Now we can take the limit of
\Xi^{\varphi}_{\gamma}(\vartheta)
:

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\Xi_{\gamma}^{\varphi}(\vartheta)=\log\left(\frac{1-e^{\alpha_{B}\beta}}{e^{\alpha_{A}\beta}-1}\right)+\frac{\beta}{2\pi}((\varphi+2\pi\vartheta)\mod 2\pi)

(597)

Resulting bounds for the equilibrium
\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}
for any
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
as
\gamma\to 0
:

\boxed{\log\left(\frac{1-e^{\alpha_{B}\beta}}{e^{\alpha_{A}\beta}-1}\right)\leq\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}\leq\log\left(\frac{1-e^{\alpha_{B}\beta}}{e^{\alpha_{A}\beta}-1}\right)+\beta}

(598)

Remark
.

The obtained bounds in (
598
) are equivalent to the Binomial Fisher limiting prior bounds in Section
401
,
402
.

∎

Remark
.

The expression (
595
) can be expressed by the Lerch transcendent
[
61
,
136
,
12
]

60
60

60

also know as Hurwitz-
Lerch
transcendent
:

\Phi_{L}(z,s,a)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{m}}{(m+a)^{s}}

(599)

\frac{\sum_{m}\frac{-1}{\frac{\alpha_{A}\beta}{2\pi}-im}e^{im\omega}}{\sum_{m}\frac{1}{\frac{\alpha_{B}\beta}{2\pi}-im}e^{im\omega}}=\frac{\sum_{m}\frac{-1}{m+i\frac{\alpha_{A}\beta}{2\pi}}e^{im\omega}}{\sum_{m}\frac{1}{m+i\frac{\alpha_{B}\beta}{2\pi}}e^{im\omega}}

(600)

\frac{\sum_{m}\frac{-1}{m+i\frac{\alpha_{A}\beta}{2\pi}}e^{im\omega}}{\sum_{m}\frac{1}{m+i\frac{\alpha_{B}\beta}{2\pi}}e^{im\omega}}=-\frac{\Phi_{L}(e^{i\omega},1,i\frac{\alpha_{A}\beta}{2\pi})-\Phi_{L}(e^{-i\omega},1,-i\frac{\alpha_{A}\beta}{2\pi})+i\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{A}\beta}}{\Phi_{L}(e^{i\omega},1,i\frac{\alpha_{B}\beta}{2\pi})-\Phi_{L}(e^{-i\omega},1,-i\frac{\alpha_{B}\beta}{2\pi})+i\frac{2\pi}{\alpha_{B}\beta}}

(601)

∎

D.7
First-order asymptotic expansion for general statistical games

The indicated derivation supports conjecture
4.3
.

Repeating essentially the same steps as in Section
D.3
, but adopting the approximations in Section
D.5.1
one can derive the following phase dependent first-order asymptotic term
\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}_{\gamma}^{\varphi}
:

\begin{split}\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}_{\gamma}^{\varphi}=&\left(\frac{n^{\prime}(x_{0}^{*})}{\beta}-\frac{\kappa(x_{0}^{*})\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{B}{}^{\prime}(\alpha_{B},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta)}{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{B}(\alpha_{B},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta)}-\frac{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{A}{}^{\prime}(\alpha_{A},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta)}{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{A}(\alpha_{A},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta)}\right)+\\
&\frac{1}{2}\frac{\kappa(x_{0}^{*})}{\beta^{2}}\left(\frac{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{B}{}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{B},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta)}{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{B}(\alpha_{B},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta)}-\frac{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{A}{}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{A},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta)}{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{A}(\alpha_{A},\varphi+2\pi\vartheta^{*,\varphi}_{\gamma,\spadesuit}/\beta)}\right)\end{split}

(602)

where
\widetilde{C}_{\theta}^{\gamma}(\alpha,\omega)
are defined in equation (
586
), and its derivatives are taken with respect to the first variable.

Remark
.

The result can be applied to conjecture
4.3
if we set
\gamma
to
1
:

\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}^{\varphi}=\includegraphics[width]{img/logos/sampi.pdf}^{\varphi}_{\gamma=1}

(603)

∎

Remark
.

In higher-order asymptotic expansions – and for further simplification of the results – the following quantity appears to be useful:

\Xi_{\gamma}^{\varphi}(\alpha,\vartheta)=\log\left(\frac{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{A}(\alpha,\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}{\widetilde{C}^{\gamma}_{B}(\alpha-1,\varphi+2\pi\vartheta/\beta)}\right)

(604)

∎

Appendix E
Natural multiplicative utility functions

The Bayesian game described in Section
3.1.1
defines the change of an agent’s capital. However, it can not specify how the players translate their amount of capital to
utility
.
Utility plays a central role in game theory because in the framework of expected utility theory
[
19
]
, this is, by definition, the quantity for which the expectation a “rational” agent ought to maximize.

In the following sections, we will see that different choices of the utility function can quantitatively change the agent’s strategy. Therefore, a careful adoption of the utility function is vital for suggesting optimal strategies for a gambling situation.

E.1
A case for a nontrivial utility function

Let us assume that there is an agent who tries to maximize her expected amount of capital after
n
rounds of multiplicative betting games, starting with an initial capital
c_{0}
.

Assuming that in all rounds the agent is placing her capitals
p^{\prime}
portion to A and
1-p^{\prime}
portion to B, after
n
rounds, her expected capital would be:

C_{n}(p^{\prime})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}p_{k}\ (2\ p^{\prime}\ c_{0})^{k}(2\ (1-p^{\prime})\ c_{0})^{n-k}

(605)

Assuming that outcomes A and B appear independently with probability
P
and
1-P
respectively, this expression can be simplified:

C_{n}(p^{\prime})=(2\ c_{0})^{n}\ \left(P\ p^{\prime}+(1-P)\ (1-p^{\prime})\right)^{n}

(606)

which can be maximized with
p^{\prime}=1
if
P>1/2
and
p^{\prime}=0
if
P<1/2
.
61
61

61

When
P=1/2
, all choices of
p^{\prime}
give the same expected capital. Because of continuity arguments, we will adopt the choice
p^{\prime}=1/2
when
P=1/2
Formally:

p^{\prime*}_{0}(P)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }P<1/2\\
1/2&\text{if }P=1/2\\
1&\text{if }P>1/2\end{cases}

(607)

Figure 44
:
Strategy plot for the capital maximizing agent,
p^{\prime*}_{0}(P)
.

However, following this strategy, the agent will be bankrupt after
n
rounds with probability
1-\max(P,1-P)^{n}
. This means that the agent’s capital will go to
0
almost surely in the long run, and only an almost never-occurring but stellar gain will compensate for her losses.

This extreme strategy might be unacceptable for most human players, signalling that alternative utility functions should be explored and considered if we want to keep the principle of expected utility.

E.2
Properties of utility functions

In general a utility function is a mapping from the consequences of a game
\mathcal{C}

62
62

62

or states in the context of Reinforcement Learning
[
162
]
to a utility set
\mathbb{U}
, which will be
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
or
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}=\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\cup\{-\infty\}
in the followings.

Formally, in general:

u:\mathcal{C}\mapsto\mathbb{U}

(608)

in particular:

u:[0,1]\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\cup\{-\infty\}

(609)

E.2.1
Equivalent utility functions

We will call two utility functions equivalent if they result in the same equilibrium strategies.
Positive affine transformations do not influence the strategy profiles in equilibrium, meaning that:

u_{1}\sim u_{2}\iff\exists C\in\mathbb{R},D\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\forall c\in[0,1]\ u_{1}(c)=C+D\ u_{2}(c)

(610)

If we denote the set of bounded utility functions by:

B_{\downmapsto}([0,1])=\{u:[0,1]\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}\ |\ \exists M\in\mathbb{R},\forall c\in[0,1],u(c)<M\}

(611)

then we can introduce the factorized set of utility functions with respect to this equivalence relation:

\tilde{B}_{\downmapsto}([0,1])=\left[B_{\downmapsto}([0,1])\right]_{\sim}

(612)

E.2.2
Possible properties of utility functions

Monotonicity

“more is better”:

Strict Monotonicity

c_{1}<c_{2}\implies u(c_{1})<u(c_{2})

Week Monotonicity

c_{1}<c_{2}\implies u(c_{1})\leq u(c_{2})

Burnability

there can be a “burning” operation, which can reduce a player’s capital by any amount. This operation can change any utility function to a weekly monotone utility:
u^{+}(c)=\sup_{c^{\prime}\leq c}u(c^{\prime})

Strict Concavity

“risk aversion”
\mathbb{E}[u(X)]<u(\mathbb{E}[X])

Continuity

“marginal gains results marginal increase of utility”
u\in C^{0}([0,1])

Differentiability

“marginal utilities are well defined”
u\in C^{1}[0,1]
(or
u\in C^{r}[0,1]
)

Diminishing returns

“Diminishing Marginal Returns”
c_{1}<c_{2}\implies u^{\prime}(c_{1})>u^{\prime}(c_{2})

Scale free

“the currency of the capital does not change the behavior”
\forall 0<r\leq 1\ \exists C\in\mathbb{R},D\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\forall c\in[0,1]\quad u(r\ c)=C+D\ u(c)

Isoelasticity

63
63

63

also known as constant relative risk aversion

for all degree of relative risk aversion
[
5
,
146
]

\gamma\geq 0
,
\gamma\neq 1

u_{\gamma}(c)=\frac{c^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(613)

Logarithmic utility

can also be viewed as a special case of the isoelastic utility, as
\gamma\to 1

u_{1}(c)=\log(c)

(614)

In the following, we will assume as little as possible about the inherently subjective utility functions. However, we will argue that isoelastic and, in particular, logarithmic utilities can emerge naturally as instrumental goals in a repeatable multiplicative gambling situation.

E.3
Derivation of instrumental goals

E.3.1
Repeatable multiplicative gambles

Let us assume that an agent can play a multiplicative “double or nothing” betting game with outcome probabilities
P
,
1-P
, for
n
rounds.
Initially, she starts with a small
2^{-n}
capital, meaning that in the final round, the range of her possible capital is the interval
[0,1]
.
We will make as few further assumptions about the agent’s final subjective utility values as possible. At this stage, we only assume that it is a function bounded from above:

u_{0}\in\tilde{B}_{\downmapsto}([0,1])

(615)

A natural approach to find the best strategy in this game is to search for a subgame perfect optimal strategy
[
117
]
.
In practice, this means that we can recursively solve the
game.
Assuming that one step before the last the agent has
c_{1}
capital, we can search for the optimal splitting ratio
p^{\prime}_{1}(c_{1})
.

If we can find all optimal strategies as a function of
c_{1}
, then we can calculate all the optimal expected utilities for the capitals one step before the end.
Formally, we can introduce the following operator:

\mathbfcal{B}[u](c)=\sup_{p^{\prime}\in[0,1]}\left(P\ u(2\ p^{\prime}\ c)+(1-P)\ u(2\ (1-p^{\prime})\ c)\right)

(616)

which is essentially a Bellman operator
[
162
]
in the framework of Reinforcement Learning.

By this operator, we can propagate the final subjective utilities back to the previous state, where the possible capital is in the interval
[0,1/2]
.

\mathbfcal{B}:\tilde{B}_{\downmapsto}([0,2^{-n}])\mapsto\tilde{B}_{\downmapsto}([0,2^{-n-1}])

(617)

If we introduce a scaling (or dilatation) operator:

\mathbfcal{D}_{r}[u](c)=u(r\ c)

(618)

then we can reduce our
n
step gamble to an
n-1
step gamble with a modified “instrumentalized”
[
82
]
utility function
u_{1}
:

\mathbfcal{I}=\mathbfcal{D}_{2}\circ\mathbfcal{B}

(619)

\mathbfcal{I}[u_{n}](c)=u_{n+1}(c)

(620)

This newly defined instrumentalisation operator
\mathbfcal{I}:\tilde{B}_{\downmapsto}([0,1])\mapsto\tilde{B}_{\downmapsto}([0,1])
has the following explicit definition:

\mathbfcal{I}[u](c)=\sup_{p^{\prime}\in[0,1]}\left(P\ u(p^{\prime}\ c)+(1-P)\ u((1-p^{\prime})\ c)\right)

(621)

In the following, we will argue that the instrumentalisation operator will converge to an isoelastic utility function for a wide range of initial subjective utility functions.

E.3.2
Properties of isoelastic utility functions

Isoelastic utility functions have several remarkable properties:

u_{\gamma}(c)=\begin{cases}\frac{c^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}&\text{if }\gamma\neq 1\\
\log(c)&\text{if }\gamma=1\end{cases}

(622)

Figure 45
:
Isoelastic utility functions, for several relative risk aversion (
\gamma
) parameters:
u_{\gamma}(c)
.

It is easy to observe that for all
\gamma>0
,
u_{\gamma}
is a strictly monotone, strictly concave, and infinitely differentiable.

Theorem E.1
.

Isoelastic utility functions are scale-free:

\mathbfcal{D}_{r}[u_{\gamma}]\sim u_{\gamma}

(623)

where
\mathbfcal{D}_{r}
is the scaling (or dilatation) operator defined in eq. (
618
).

∎

Proof.

Direct calculation shows that

\mathbfcal{D}_{r}[u_{\gamma}]=C+D\ u_{\gamma}(c)

(624)

with parameters:

C=\frac{r^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma},\quad D=r^{\gamma-1}

(625)

∎

Theorem E.2
.

All scale-free and twice differentiable utility functions are isoelastic.

∎

Proof.

To simplify the proof, it is better to change from capital variable (
c
) to growth-related variable (
g
):

g=\log(c),\quad c=e^{g}

(626)

And to introduce a new marginal utility-related quantity:

w(g)=\text{\bf\dh}[u](g)=\log(u^{\prime}(e^{g}))

(627)

u(c)=\text{\bf\c{S}}[w](c)=\int_{1}^{c}e^{w(\log(c^{\prime}))}dc^{\prime}

(628)

These modified differentiating (
ð
) and integrating (
Ş
) operators satisfy the following identities:

\left(\text{\bf\c{S}}\circ\text{\bf\dh}\right)[u]\sim u,\quad\left(\text{\bf\dh}\circ\text{\bf\c{S}}\right)[w]=w

(629)

A positive affine transformation on utility is only an additive transformation for
w
:

u(c)\to C+D\ u(c),\implies w(g)\to\log(D)+w(g)

(630)

in a similar fashion, scaling of the utility can be “pushforward”
[
108
]
to
w
:

u(c)\to\mathbfcal{D}_{r}[u](c)=u(rc)\implies w(g)\to\mathbfcal{D}^{\text{\tiny{\char 55\relax}}}_{r}[w](g)=\log(r)+w(g+\log(r))

(631)

The requirement for the scale invariance of the utility can be rewritten to the
w
variable:

\mathbfcal{D}^{\text{\tiny{\char 55\relax}}}_{r}[w](g)=\log(D(r))+w(g)

(632)

applying an infinitesimal scale transformation
r=1+\epsilon
and keeping only the first-order terms results in the following equation:

\epsilon+w(g)+w^{\prime}(g)\ \epsilon=\log(D(1))+\alpha\ \epsilon+w(g)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})

(633)

where
\alpha=D^{\prime}(1)/D(1)
.
This can be satisfied if
D(1)=1
(which is a natural requirement, meaning that if we do not scale at all, then we do not need to transform the utility function either) and if
w
fulfils the following simple differential equation:

w^{\prime}(g)=\alpha-1=-\gamma

(634)

This determines the utility up to two integration constants:

w(g)=-\gamma\ g+A

(635)

u(c)=B+\int_{1}^{c}e^{w(\log(c^{\prime}))}dc^{\prime}

(636)

u(c)=B+e^{A}\ \frac{c^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(637)

Which is, by definition, equivalent to an isoelastic function for any choice of
A
and
B
.

∎

Theorem E.3
.

Isoelastic functions are invariant under the instrumentalisation (and Bellmann) operator:

\mathbfcal{I}[u_{\gamma}]\sim u_{\gamma}

(638)

\mathbfcal{B}[u_{\gamma}]\sim u_{\gamma}

(639)

∎

Proof.

For isoelastic utility functions, there is an optimal
p^{\prime}_{\gamma}
for which the expected utility is maximal:

\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}}\left(P\ u_{\gamma}(p^{\prime}\ c)+(1-P)\ u_{\gamma}((1-p^{\prime})\ c)\right)\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}=p^{\prime}_{\gamma}}=0

(640)

By introducing:

v_{\gamma}(c)=\frac{\partial}{\partial c}u_{\gamma}(c)=c^{-\gamma}

(641)

We get:

c\ \left(P\ v_{\gamma}(p^{\prime}_{\gamma}c)-(1-P)\ v_{\gamma}((1-p^{\prime}_{\gamma})c)\right)=0

(642)

Assuming
c>0

\frac{P}{{p^{\prime}}_{\gamma}^{\gamma}}-\frac{1-P}{(1-p^{\prime}_{\gamma})^{\gamma}}=0

(643)

The solution for
p^{\prime}_{\gamma}
is:

p^{\prime}_{\gamma}=\frac{P^{1/\gamma}}{P^{1/\gamma}+(1-P)^{1/\gamma}}

(644)

To prove that it is indeed a maximum, one can check its second derivative:

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial{p^{\prime}}^{2}}\left(P\ u_{\gamma}(p^{\prime}\ c)+(1-P)\ u_{\gamma}((1-p^{\prime})\ c)\right)\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}=p^{\prime}_{\gamma}}=d_{\gamma}

(645)

Straightforward calculation gives that:

d_{\gamma}=-\frac{\gamma}{c^{\gamma+1}}\frac{\left(P^{1/\gamma}+(1-P)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{\gamma+2}}{\left(P(1-P)\right)^{1/\gamma}}

(646)

which is negative for all
\gamma>0
, signaling a maximum.

This means that:

\mathbfcal{I}[u_{\gamma}](c)=P\ u_{\gamma}(p^{\prime}_{\gamma}\ c)+(1-P)\ u_{\gamma}((1-p^{\prime}_{\gamma})\ c)

(647)

\mathbfcal{I}[u_{\gamma}](c)=\frac{c^{1-\gamma}\left(P^{1/\gamma}+(1-P)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(648)

For which one can find a positive affine transformation, making it equivalent to the original isoelastic utility
u_{\gamma}
:

\mathbfcal{I}[u_{\gamma}](c)=C_{\gamma}+D_{\gamma}\ u_{\gamma}(c)

(649)

C_{\gamma}=-\frac{1-\left(P^{1/\gamma}+(1-P)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{\gamma}}{1-\gamma}\quad D_{\gamma}=\left(P^{1/\gamma}+(1-P)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{\gamma}

(650)

∎

The relative risk aversion parameter,
\gamma
, influences
p^{\prime}
, the portion of capital placed to outcome A; therefore, different isoelastic utility functions can observably change the agent’s behaviour.

Figure 46
:
Strategy plot for expected utility maximizing agent, which adopt isoelastic utilities:
p^{\prime}_{\gamma}(P)
.

Theorem E.4
.

For any subjective utility function, which is twice continuously differentiable, strictly monotone increasing, strictly concave, its local relative risk aversion parameter is bounded, strictly positive and converges to a finite
\gamma_{\downarrow}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
as
c\to 0
, will converge to an isoelastic utility function under repeated application of the instrumentalisation operator:

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbfcal{I}^{n}[u_{0}]\sim u_{\gamma_{\downarrow}}

(651)

∎

Proof.

Introducing a few new functions and operators is useful to prove this theorem.

We will introduce the following growth-dependent relative risk aversion function
\gamma(g):(-\infty,0]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
:

\gamma(g)=-w^{\prime}(g)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial g}\log(u^{\prime}(e^{g}))=-e^{g}\ \frac{u^{\prime\prime}(e^{g})}{u^{\prime}(e^{g})}

(652)

this can be formulated using differential operators:

\gamma=-\bm{\partial}[w]=-\bm{\partial}\circ\text{\bf\dh}[u]=\mathbfcal{R}[u]

(653)

A nice property of the relative risk aversion function is that from the knowledge of
\gamma(g)
, one can recover a utility function, which is unique up to a linear affine transformation.

u(c)=\int_{1}^{c}e^{-\int_{1}^{\log(c^{\prime})}\gamma(g^{\prime})dg^{\prime}}dc^{\prime}\sim A+\int_{1}^{c}e^{B-\int_{1}^{\log(c^{\prime})}\gamma(g^{\prime})dg^{\prime}}dc^{\prime}

(654)

This can be formulated using integral operators:

u=\text{\bf\c{S}}[w]=\text{\bf\c{S}}\circ\left(-\text{\bf S}\right)[\gamma]=\mathbfcal{U}[\gamma]

(655)

The defined differential and integral operators satisfy the following properties:

\left(\mathbfcal{U}\circ\mathbfcal{R}\right)[u]\sim u,\quad\left(\mathbfcal{R}\circ\mathbfcal{U}\right)[\gamma]=\gamma

(656)

This construction allows us to prove the convergence of utility functions by showing the convergence of
\gamma_{n}(g)
functions.
To do this, we can define a “second order pushforward” instrumentalisation operator
\mathbfcal{J}
by the following commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbfcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbfcal{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbfcal{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbfcal{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbfcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbfcal{U}}

Construction of the
\mathbfcal{J}
operator:

As a first step, we can determine
p^{\prime}(c)
and
p^{\prime}(g)
functions:

\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}}\left(P\ u(p^{\prime}\ c)+(1-P)\ u((1-p^{\prime})\ c)\right)\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}=p^{\prime}(c)}=0

(657)

or in
v(c)=u^{\prime}(c)
variables:

P\ v(p^{\prime}(c)\ c)=(1-P)\ v((1-p^{\prime}(c))\ c)

(658)

Taking the logarithm and substituting the
w(g)
variables defined in eq. (
627
) we get:

\log(P)+w(\log(p^{\prime}(g))+g)=\log(1-P)+w(\log(1-p^{\prime}(g))+g)

(659)

which can be rearranged as an expression of
\gamma(g)
:

\log\left(\frac{P}{1-P}\right)=\int_{g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g))}^{g+\log(p^{\prime}(g))}\gamma(g)dg

(660)

We assume that
0<\underline{\gamma}\leq\gamma(g)\leq\overline{\gamma}
, in which case this implicit equation always has a solution:

F_{g}(p^{\prime})=\int_{g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g))}^{g+\log(p^{\prime}(g))}\gamma(g)dg

(661)

It is easy to see that
F_{g}(1/2)=0
, and that it goes to
+\infty
as
p^{\prime}\to 1
and
-\infty
as
p^{\prime}\to 0
. Because of the continuity of
F_{g}(.)
there will be always a
p^{\prime}(g)
where it intersects the value
\log(P/(1-P))
.

For every
g
, we can introduce an effective relative risk aversion:

\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}(g)=\frac{1}{\log(p^{\prime}(g))-\log(1-p^{\prime}(g))}\int_{g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g))}^{g+\log(p^{\prime}(g))}\gamma(g^{\prime})dg^{\prime}

(662)

which is an integral mean of
\gamma(g)
on the
[g+\log(p^{\prime}(g)),g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g)]
interval.

p^{\prime}(g)=\frac{P^{1/\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}(g)}}{P^{1/\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}(g)}+(1-P)^{1/\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}(g)}}

(663)

because
\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}(g)
is an integral mean, it is surely bounded as
\gamma(g)
:

0<\underline{\gamma}<\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}(g)<\overline{\gamma}
, which gives bounds for
p^{\prime}(g)
as well. If we assume that
P>1/2
:

\frac{P^{1/\overline{\gamma}}}{P^{1/\overline{\gamma}}+(1-P)^{1/\overline{\gamma}}}\leq p^{\prime}(g)\leq\frac{P^{1/\underline{\gamma}}}{P^{1/\underline{\gamma}}+(1-P)^{1/\underline{\gamma}}}

(664)

u_{1}(c)=P\ u(p^{\prime}(c)\ c)+(1-P)\ u((1-p^{\prime}(c))\ c)

(665)

Taking the derivative respect to
c
, we get:

\begin{split}v_{1}(c)=&P\ v(p^{\prime}(c)\ c)\ \left(p^{\prime}(c)+\frac{dp^{\prime}(c)}{dc}\right)+\\
&(1-P)\ v((1-p^{\prime}(c))\ c)\ \left((1-p^{\prime}(c))-\frac{dp^{\prime}(c)}{dc}\right)\end{split}

(666)

because of equation (
658
), this considerably simplifies to:

\begin{split}v_{1}(c)=&P\ v(p^{\prime}(c)\ c)\\
=&(1-P)\ v((1-p^{\prime}(c))\ c)\end{split}

(667)

Taking the logarithm and changing the variables from
c
to
g
:

\begin{split}w_{1}(c)=&\log(P)+w(g+\log(p^{\prime}(g)))\\
