=&\log(1-P)+w(g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g)))\end{split}

(668)

taking the derivative respect to
g
for both equations results:

\gamma_{1}(g)=-\frac{d}{dg}w_{1}(g)=-w^{\prime}(g+\log(p^{\prime}(g)))\ \left(1+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}(g)}\frac{dp^{\prime}(g)}{dg}\right)

(669)

\gamma_{1}(g)=-\frac{d}{dg}w_{1}(g)=-w^{\prime}(g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g)))\ \left(1-\frac{1}{1-p^{\prime}(g)}\frac{dp^{\prime}(g)}{dg}\right)

(670)

Combining the two equations, we get:

\frac{\gamma_{1}(g)}{\gamma(g+\log(p^{\prime}(g)))}\ p^{\prime}(g)+\frac{\gamma_{1}(g)}{\gamma(g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g)))}\ (1-p^{\prime}(g))=1

(671)

resulting:

\mathbfcal{J}[\gamma](g)=\gamma_{1}(g)=\frac{1}{\frac{p^{\prime}(g)}{\gamma(g+\log(p^{\prime}(g)))}+\frac{1-p^{\prime}(g)}{\gamma(g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g)))}}

(672)

which is the weighted harmonic mean of
\gamma(g+\log(p^{\prime}(g)))
and
\gamma(g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g)))
.

\mathbfcal{J}[\gamma](g)=H^{(p^{\prime}(g),1-p^{\prime}(g))}((\gamma(g+\log(p^{\prime}(g))),\gamma(g+\log(1-p^{\prime}(g)))))

(673)

where we used the notation for weighted harmonic mean:

H^{\underline{\alpha}}(\underline{x})=\frac{\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}}{\sum_{i}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{x_{i}}}

(674)

Because the instrumentalisation of
\gamma(g)
can be calculated as a mean, it can not increase the upper and lower bounds of
\gamma(g)
:

[\underline{\gamma}_{n+1},\overline{\gamma}_{n+1}]\subseteq[\underline{\gamma}_{n},\overline{\gamma}_{n}]\subseteq\dots\subseteq[\underline{\gamma},\overline{\gamma}]

(675)

This means that there will be an upper bound for
\underline{\Delta g}<0
, by which the instrumentalisation operator shifts the argument of
\gamma(.)
toward
-\infty
:

\underline{\Delta g}=\sup_{\gamma\in[\underline{\gamma},\overline{\gamma}]}\max\left(\log\left(\frac{P^{1/\gamma}}{P^{1/\gamma}+(1-P)^{1/\gamma}}\right),\log\left(\frac{(1-P)^{1/\gamma}}{P^{1/\gamma}+(1-P)^{1/\gamma}}\right)\right)

(676)

as all application of
\mathbfcal{J}
shifts the argument of
\gamma
at least by
\underline{\Delta g}
, the following two sided inequality holds:

\inf_{g\leq n\cdot\underline{\Delta g}}{\gamma(g)}\leq\mathbfcal{J}^{n}[\gamma](g)\leq\sup_{g\leq n\cdot\underline{\Delta g}}{\gamma(g)}

(677)

if
\lim_{g\to-\infty}\gamma(g)=\gamma_{\downarrow}
, then

\lim_{g_{M}\to-\infty}\inf_{g\leq g_{M}}{\gamma(g)}=\gamma_{\downarrow},\quad\lim_{g_{M}\to-\infty}\sup_{g\leq g_{M}}{\gamma(g)}=\gamma_{\downarrow}

(678)

resulting that:

\forall g\in(-\infty,0],\ \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbfcal{J}^{n}[\gamma](g)=\gamma_{\downarrow}

(679)

∎

Besides the mathematical proof, the intuitive understanding of the result is the following:
If the gambler values capital and is risk averse, then she will not commit all her resources to the more probable outcome but reserve some of her capital in case the less probable scenario comes out.
Suppose she associates the value of an intermediate stage with the maximally achievable expected utility from that state. In that case, she can deduce the value of all intermediate stages step by step from her subjective utility judgement at the final stage.

If we imagine gamblers playing more and more rounds, starting from less and less starting capital, their possible maximal gain will decrease (because of their successive careful choices).

This means that if an agent wants to evaluate the value of a state, she needs to consider only very small final capital values.

The degree of relative risk aversion is conserved in a multiplicative gamble; therefore, if the agent’s relative risk aversion converges to a finite value for very small final capitals, then the initial values of a state will be dominated by this limit of relative risk aversion.

Remark
.

Because of the averaging property of the instrumentalisation operator, the result can most probably be generalised further. In the proof, we principally used the property that instrumentalisation does not change the bounds of a relative risk aversion function and that it includes a finite “shift” if we construct a sequence of gambles with decreasing starting capital and increasing number of rounds.

However, because of the averaging, the convergence could probably be proved for a more general sequence of gambles (for instance, where the gambler starts with a unit capital, and her gain can grow exponentially) and more general subjective final utility functions.

Numerical evidence shows that very general utility functions (non-monotone, non-convex) can converge to an isoelastic utility, signalling a wider domain of validity of the fixed point result.

∎

The paper of Hakansson from 1974
[
65
]
might be relevant for investigating the convergence of instrumental utilities. (In his model, the initial capital is finite, so the terminal utilities are dominated by its behaviour around infinity.)

E.4
Contest view

A natural way to change the capital collecting game into a contest is to introduce another player with the same opportunities as our original agent. A natural criterion for winning this resource-collecting game is to have more capital after the final round.

E.4.1
Raffle contest

First, let us consider a game with two stages.

First stage:

two gamblers can decide what amount of their capital are placing to the two possible outcomes, A and B, in
n
rounds:

c_{1}=(2\ p^{\prime}_{1})^{\kappa}(2\ (1-p^{\prime}_{1}))^{n-\kappa},\quad c_{2}=(2\ p^{\prime}_{2})^{\kappa}(2\ (1-p^{\prime}_{2}))^{n-\kappa}

(680)

\kappa\sim\mathrm{Binom}(n,P)

(681)

Second stage:

The two players can spend their capital
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
to buy tickets in a Raffle (or a Lottery).
Player 1 can win with probability
c_{1}/(c_{1}+c_{2})
, and Player 2 can win with probability
c_{2}/(c_{1}+c_{2})
, resulting the following utility function:

u_{1}(c_{1},c_{2})=\frac{c_{1}}{c_{1}+c_{2}}u_{1}^{\mathrm{W}}+\frac{c_{2}}{c_{1}+c_{2}}u_{1}^{\mathrm{L}}

(682)

u_{2}(c_{1},c_{2})=\frac{c_{2}}{c_{1}+c_{2}}u_{2}^{\mathrm{W}}+\frac{c_{1}}{c_{1}+c_{2}}u_{2}^{\mathrm{L}}

(683)

where
u_{i}^{\mathrm{W}}
represents the utility associated with the
i
-th Player winning, while
u_{i}^{\mathrm{L}}
represents the utility associated with losing.
If all Players prefer winning more than losing (
u_{i}^{\mathrm{W}}>u_{i}^{\mathrm{L}}
), then by a suitable positive affine transformation, all
u_{i}^{\mathrm{W}}
can be changed to
1
and all
u_{i}^{\mathrm{L}}
to
0
.

This results in the following explicit final utility function for Player 1 and Player 2:

U_{1}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(2\ p^{\prime}_{1})^{\kappa}(2\ (1-p^{\prime}_{1}))^{n-\kappa}}{(2\ p^{\prime}_{1})^{\kappa}(2\ (1-p^{\prime}_{1}))^{n-\kappa}+(2\ p^{\prime}_{2})^{\kappa}(2\ (1-p^{\prime}_{2}))^{n-\kappa}}\right]

(684)

U_{1}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})+U_{2}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})=1

(685)

These expected utilities can be interpreted as winning rates after these transformations.

Figure 47
:
U_{1}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})
function for
P=2/3
,
n=1
.

Because of symmetry, we can search the equilibrium, i.e. the saddle point on the
p^{\prime}_{1}=p^{\prime}_{2}
line.
At the saddle point, the function has to fulfil the condition:

\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{1}}U_{1}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})|_{p^{\prime}_{1}=p^{\prime*},p^{\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime*}}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\kappa-np^{\prime*}}{4p^{\prime*}(1-p^{\prime*})}\right]=n\frac{P-p^{\prime*}}{4p^{\prime*}(1-p^{\prime*})}=0

(686)

Which has a very simple solution:

\boxed{p^{\prime*}=P}

(687)

Direct calculation of the Hessian gives:

H_{U_{1}}(P,P)=\frac{n}{4P(1-P)}\begin{bmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

(688)

Which confirms that we found a saddle point.

Illustration of the equilibrium:

To illustrate how gradual changes in the splitting ratios might lead to the equilibrium point, we show a stream plot of so-called “adaptive dynamics”
[
135
]
in figure
48
:

\overrightarrow{V}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{1}}U_{1}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})&\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{2}}U_{2}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})\end{bmatrix}

(689)

Figure 48
:
Adaptive dynamics stream plot generated from the vector field
\overrightarrow{V}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})
, for
P=2/3
.

E.4.2
Generalizations

This result can be generalized in multiple ways, signalling the robustness of the concept:

Arbitrary number of players:

The contest view can be generalized to multiple players. If the number of players is
m
, we get the following equation for equilibrium:

\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{1}}U(\underline{p^{\prime}})\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}_{i}=p^{\prime*}}=n\frac{m-1}{m^{2}}\frac{P-p^{\prime*}}{p^{\prime*}(1-p^{\prime*})}

(690)

Which has the same remarkably simple solution:

p^{\prime*}_{i}=P

(691)

Nonlinear ticket prices:

Remarkably, the equilibrium point remains the same if the raffle ticket prices scale nonlinearly.
The calculation gives the same result if for a collected capital
c
,
c^{\alpha}
amount of ticket can be purchased.

This would generalise the utility function of the second stage in the following way:

u_{1}^{\alpha}(c_{1},c_{2})=\frac{c_{1}^{\alpha}}{c_{1}^{\alpha}+c_{2}^{\alpha}}

(692)

\forall\alpha>0,n\geq 1,\quad p^{\prime*}_{\alpha,n}(P)=P

(693)

Allowing Behavioural strategies:

In the original construction of the raffle context, we assumed that the players were choosing a splitting ratio for all rounds in the beginning.
This requirement can be relaxed and allow players to choose their strategy according to their current capital and the game history they can recall. These history-dependent strategies are called Behavioural strategies
[
117
]
.

In this case, we can try to find the first so-called subgame perfect equilibrium
[
117
]
, which means that at every stage, we assume that in the future, everybody will play optimally.
In this framework, it is natural to introduce an instrumentalisation of the utility function, which associates the equilibrium expected utilities to nonterminal stages of the game when the players have the pair of collected capitals
(c_{1},c_{2})
:

\mathbfcal{I}[u](c_{1},c_{2})=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{c_{1}(p^{\prime}_{1})^{\kappa}(1-p^{\prime}_{1})^{n-\kappa}}{c_{1}(p^{\prime}_{1})^{\kappa}(1-p^{\prime}_{1})^{n-\kappa}+c_{2}(p^{\prime}_{2})^{\kappa}(1-p^{\prime}_{2})^{n-\kappa}}\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}_{1}=p^{\prime*}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime*}_{2}}\right]

(694)

This instrumentalisation results in a very simple form of the instrumental utilities in an intermediate stage:

\mathbfcal{I}[u](c_{1},c_{2})=\frac{c_{1}}{c_{1}+c_{2}}

(695)

This means that all potentially history-dependent subgame perfect equilibria have the same history-independent optimal splitting ratios:

p^{\prime[1]}_{i}=P,\quad p^{\prime[2]}_{i}(c^{[1]}_{1},c^{[1]}_{2})=P,\quad p^{\prime[3]}_{i}(c^{[2]}_{1},c^{[2]}_{2},c^{[1]}_{1},c^{[1]}_{2})=P,\dots

(696)

E.4.3
Capital contest:

An even simpler contest could be defined if we practically skip the second stage and declare that:

Whoever has more capital wins; if the capitals are equal, all players have the same chance of winning.

Figure 49
:
U_{1}(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})
function for a capital contest with
P=2/3
,
n=5
.

This simple contest results in a piecewise constant but discontinuous utility function, which depends on the splitting ratios
p^{\prime}_{1}
and
p^{\prime}_{2}
and the number of rounds
n
.
Figure
49
shows a concrete example of the utility function, which clearly shows that for this contest, the equilibrium solution can differ from the continuous case, where we found
p^{\prime*}_{i}=P
.

The utility function is not differentiable; therefore, we can not use partial derivatives to find an equilibrium, but there are other techniques by which we can find the solution, such as the iterative elimination of weakly dominated strategies
[
117
]
:

\rightarrow

\rightarrow

\rightarrow

(697)

Arguments based on the iterative elimination of weakly dominated strategies lead to a general formula for the equilibrium splitting ratios:

p^{\prime*}_{\text{{\char 32\relax}}n}(P)=\frac{[n\ P]}{n}

(698)

In some edge cases, there is only an interval in which the splitting ratio has to fall. These cases are also captured with the following general inequalities:

\frac{[n\ P]_{-}}{n}\leq p^{\prime*}_{\text{{\char 32\relax}}n}(P)\leq\frac{[n\ P]_{+}}{n}

(699)

Where
[x]_{-}
stands for “round half down”, in which case half-way values of
x
are always rounded down, and
[x]_{+}
stands for ‘‘round half up’’ rounding.
64
64

64

for more background about rounding conventions see
Rounding Algorithms Compared
,
Rounding Algorithms 101
by Clive Maxfield, or
Rounding on Wikipedia
.

Figure 50
:
p^{\prime*}_{\text{{\char 32\relax}}n}(P)
for several values of
n
, and the limit case as
n\to\infty
.

Remark
.

The defined discontinuous utility function can be viewed as the limit case of nonlinear raffle games. However, finding the equilibrium and taking the limit are not interchangeable in general:

\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}p^{\prime*}_{\alpha,n}(.)\nequiv p^{\prime*}_{\infty,n}(.)=p^{\prime*}_{\text{{\char 32\relax}}n}(.)

(700)

∎

Remark
.

However, for all
P\in[0,1]
the interchangeability is restored as
n\to\infty
:

\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}p^{\prime*}_{\alpha,n}(P)=P=\lim_{n\to\infty}p^{\prime*}_{\text{{\char 32\relax}}n}(P)

(701)

∎

E.4.4
Emergent isoelastic utility in contests

Remark
.

Less than
1
relative risk aversion can also emerge in contests if two or more players can play in independent gambles and have a capital-sharing agreement.
This can be a natural way for seemingly non-logarithmic utility functions to emerge and be aligned with an evolutionarily stable strategy.

∎

Illustrative calculation:

A simple back-of-the-envelope calculation can demonstrate the emergence of aligned isoelastic utility functions:

Suppose there are two independent environments or casinos
\aleph
and
\beth
, in which gamblers can bet on scenarios A and B.
In the first casino, A comes out with probability
P_{\aleph}
, while in the second casino, with
P_{\beth}
.
Let us assume that there are two cooperating players gambling in different casinos, both with a logarithmic utility function, who are sharing all their capital but can choose freely and independently their splitting ratios:

p^{\prime}_{\aleph},\quad p^{\prime}_{\beth}

(702)

There is an explicit expression for the expected utility of player
\aleph
, depending on both splitting ratios:

\begin{split}U_{\aleph}(p^{\prime}_{\aleph},p^{\prime}_{\beth})=&P_{\aleph}P_{\beth}\log((p^{\prime}_{\aleph}+p^{\prime}_{\beth})/2)\\
&(1-P_{\aleph})P_{\beth}\log((1-p^{\prime}_{\aleph}+p^{\prime}_{\beth})/2)\\
&P_{\aleph}(1-P_{\beth})\log((p^{\prime}_{\aleph}+1-p^{\prime}_{\beth})/2)\\
&(1-P_{\aleph})(1-P_{\beth})\log((1-p^{\prime}_{\aleph}+1-p^{\prime}_{\beth})/2)\end{split}

(703)

The two players share all their capital, and they have the same utility function; therefore, their utility function is always the same:

U_{\aleph}(p^{\prime}_{\aleph},p^{\prime}_{\beth})=U_{\beth}(p^{\prime}_{\aleph},p^{\prime}_{\beth})

(704)

To simplify the calculation, let us assume that chances in both casinos are identical, i.e.:

P=P_{\aleph}=P_{\beth}

(705)

In this case, the symmetry of the problem suggests that both players will have identical equilibrium splitting ratios:

p^{\prime*}=p^{\prime*}_{\aleph}=p^{\prime*}_{\beth}

(706)

Both players try to maximize their expected utility by choosing their own splitting ratio. Equilibrium is reached when:

\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{\aleph}}U_{\aleph}(p^{\prime}_{\aleph},p^{\prime}_{\beth})\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}_{\aleph}=p^{\prime*},p^{\prime}_{\beth}=p^{\prime*}}=0,\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{\beth}}U_{\beth}(p^{\prime}_{\aleph},p^{\prime}_{\beth})\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}_{\aleph}=p^{\prime*},p^{\prime}_{\beth}=p^{\prime*}}=0

(707)

The expression can be explicitly calculated for player
\aleph
:

\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\prime}_{\aleph}}U_{\aleph}(p^{\prime}_{\aleph},p^{\prime}_{\beth})\Bigr|_{p^{\prime}_{\aleph}=p^{\prime*},p^{\prime}_{\beth}=p^{\prime*}}=P^{2}\frac{1}{2p^{\prime*}}+(1-P)P(-1)+P(1-P)+(1-P)^{2}\frac{-1}{2(1-p^{\prime*})}

(708)

The equilibrium requirement simplifies to:

P^{2}\frac{1}{2p^{\prime*}}-(1-P)^{2}\frac{1}{2(1-p^{\prime*})}=0

(709)

Which results in the equilibrium splitting ratio:

p^{\prime*}=\frac{P^{2}}{P^{2}+(1-P)^{2}}

(710)

This shows that the behaviour of the capital sharing players looks
as if
they would maximize an isoelastic utility function with relative risk aversion parameter
\gamma=1/2
.

If there are more capital-sharing players
\aleph,\beth,\gimel,\dots
betting in independent casinos, then the emergent relative risk aversion – aligned with the optimal equilibrium strategy – is
\gamma=1/m
, where
m
is the number of cooperating players.

Remark
.

The construction can have a biological/evolutionary interpretation as well. In a framework where propagation of genes is the primary goal of biological organisms
[
39
,
86
]
, the following observation can be made:

If a biological actor has a twin or multiple copies having the same set of genes, and they are acting in similar but uncorrelated environments, then (from the gene’s point of view) the evolutionary optimal strategy is aligned with an isoelastic utility function with lowered relative risk aversion.

∎

Appendix F
Unification through relative risk aversion

F.1
Equilibrium of general Statistical games

The proof belongs to Theorem
5.1
.

Proof.

In a general Statistical game
{\swabfamily SGame}(N,K_{A},K_{B},M,\gamma)

Player 1
has an isoelastic utility function, with relative risk aversion
\gamma>0
,
\gamma\neq 1
:

u_{\gamma}(c)=\frac{c^{1-\gamma}-1}{1-\gamma}

(711)

Player 1
’s expected utility is:

U_{\gamma}(P)=P\ \left(\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)u_{\gamma}(p^{\prime}_{k}(P))\right)+(1-P)\left(\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)u_{\gamma}(1-p^{\prime}_{k}(P))\right)

(712)

This expression is maximized by the choice:

p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}(P)=\frac{(P\ p_{k}(A))^{1/\gamma}}{(P\ p_{k}(A))^{1/\gamma}+((1-P)\ p_{k}(B))^{1/\gamma}}

(713)

Introducing:

\begin{split}U_{A}(P)&=\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)u_{\gamma}(p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}(P))\\
U_{B}(P)&=\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)u_{\gamma}(1-p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k}(P))\end{split}

(714)

In equilibrium,
P^{*}_{\gamma}
has to satisfy the requirement:

U_{A}(P^{*}_{\gamma})=U_{B}(P^{*}_{\gamma})

(715)

or alternatively:

\frac{U_{A}(P^{*}_{\gamma})+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}{U_{B}(P^{*}_{\gamma})+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}=1

(716)

Taking the logarithm, we get:

\log\left(\frac{U_{A}(P^{*}_{\gamma})+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}{U_{B}(P^{*}_{\gamma})+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}\right)=0

(717)

Collecting the terms, we get:

\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}\log\left(\frac{P^{*}_{\gamma}}{1-P^{*}_{\gamma}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)p_{k}(A)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\left((P^{*}_{\gamma}p_{k}(A))^{1/\gamma}+((1-P^{*}_{\gamma})p_{k}(B))^{1/\gamma}\right)^{1-\gamma}}}{\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)p_{k}(B)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\left((P^{*}_{\gamma}p_{k}(A))^{1/\gamma}+((1-P^{*}_{\gamma})p_{k}(B))^{1/\gamma}\right)^{1-\gamma}}}\right)=0

(718)

Introducing log-odds:

\log\left(\frac{P^{*}_{\gamma}}{1-P^{*}_{\gamma}}\right)=\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma}

(719)

This expression can be rewritten as:

\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma}=\Phi(\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma})

(720)

where

\Phi(\vartheta)=-\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\frac{\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)p_{k}(A)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\left(e^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vartheta}{\gamma}}p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}+e^{-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vartheta}{\gamma}}p_{k}(B)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{1-\gamma}}}{\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)p_{k}(B)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}}\frac{1}{\left(e^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vartheta}{\gamma}}p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}+e^{-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vartheta}{\gamma}}p_{k}(B)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{1-\gamma}}}\right)

(721)

or

\Phi(\vartheta)=-\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\frac{\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}\frac{1}{\left(e^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vartheta}{\gamma}}p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}+e^{-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vartheta}{\gamma}}p_{k}(B)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{1-\gamma}}}{\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)^{1/\gamma}\frac{1}{\left(e^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vartheta}{\gamma}}p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}+e^{-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vartheta}{\gamma}}p_{k}(B)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{1-\gamma}}}\right)

(722)

It is straightforward but tedious to take the derivative of
\Phi(\vartheta)
with respect to
\vartheta
. The following notation will considerably simplify the results:

z=e^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vartheta}{\gamma}}

(723)

\begin{split}a_{k}&=z\ p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}+z^{-1}\ p_{k}(B)^{1/\gamma}\\
b_{k}&=z\ p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}-z^{-1}\ p_{k}(B)^{1/\gamma}\end{split}

(724)

\Phi^{\prime}(\vartheta)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}\frac{b_{k}}{a_{k}^{2-\gamma}}}{\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}\frac{1}{a_{k}^{1-\gamma}}}-\frac{\sum_{\ell}p_{\ell}(B)^{1/\gamma}\frac{b_{\ell}}{a_{\ell}^{2-\gamma}}}{\sum_{\ell}p_{\ell}(B)^{1/\gamma}\frac{1}{a_{\ell}^{1-\gamma}}}\right)

(725)

Introducing further notation:

\begin{split}x_{k}&=p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}\frac{1}{a_{k}^{1-\gamma}}\geq 0\\
y_{\ell}&=p_{\ell}(B)^{1/\gamma}\frac{1}{a_{\ell}^{1-\gamma}}\geq 0\end{split}

(726)

we get the following expression:

\Phi^{\prime}(\vartheta)=\frac{\sum_{k,\ell}x_{k}y_{\ell}\ \frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{b_{k}}{a_{k}}-\frac{b_{\ell}}{a_{\ell}}\right)}{\sum_{k,\ell}x_{k}y_{\ell}}

(727)

Using Hölder’s inequality
[
2
]
in the numerator gives an upper bound for the absolute value of the derivative:

|\Phi^{\prime}(\vartheta)|\leq\frac{\left(\sum_{k,\ell}|x_{k}y_{\ell}|\right)\ \max_{k,l}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|\frac{b_{k}}{a_{k}}-\frac{b_{\ell}}{a_{\ell}}\right|\right)}{\sum_{k,\ell}|x_{k}y_{\ell}|}

(728)

which simplifies to:

|\Phi^{\prime}(\vartheta)|\leq\max_{k,l}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|\frac{b_{k}}{a_{k}}-\frac{b_{\ell}}{a_{\ell}}\right|\right)

(729)

Remark
.

The expression
b_{k}/a_{k}
is a shifted hyperbolic tangent function of
\vartheta
:

\frac{b_{k}}{a_{k}}=\tanh(\vartheta-\Delta\vartheta_{k})

(730)

∎

Straightforward calculation shows that
\left|\frac{b_{k}}{a_{k}}-\frac{b_{\ell}}{a_{\ell}}\right|
has only one local (and global) maximum, in which point its value is:

\max_{z}\frac{1}{2}\left|\frac{b_{k}(z)}{a_{k}(z)}-\frac{b_{\ell}(z)}{a_{\ell}(z)}\right|=\left|\frac{\sqrt{p_{\ell}(A)p_{k}(B)}^{1/\gamma}-\sqrt{p_{k}(A)p_{\ell}(B)}^{1/\gamma}}{\sqrt{p_{\ell}(A)p_{k}(B)}^{1/\gamma}+\sqrt{p_{k}(A)p_{\ell}(B)}^{1/\gamma}}\right|

(731)

By introducing the following exponent:

\overline{\Lambda}=\max_{k,\ell}\left|\log\left(\sqrt{\frac{p_{k}(A)}{p_{\ell}(A)}\frac{p_{\ell}(B)}{p_{k}(B)}}\right)\right|

(732)

a global bound can be given:

|\Phi^{\prime}(\vartheta)|\leq\frac{1-e^{-\overline{\Lambda}/\gamma}}{1+e^{-\overline{\Lambda}/\gamma}},

(733)

which is strictly smaller than
1
if all
p_{k}(A),p_{\ell}(B)>0
.
This means that
\Phi
is a contraction for all
\gamma>0
,
\gamma\neq 1
.

The Banach fixed point theorem
[
147
]
guarantees the existence of a unique fixed point for this contraction:

\forall\gamma>0,\gamma\neq 1\quad\exists!\ \vartheta^{*}_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\Phi(\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma})=\vartheta^{*}_{\gamma}

(734)

∎

Remark
.

For the case when
\mathbb{K}_{A}\neq\mathbb{K}_{B}
the exponent in equation (
732
) can be undefined.
Therefore the existence and uniqueness of
P^{*}_{\gamma}
is given by a weaker convexity argument similar to the statement made in Section
3.6
, which maps the equilibrium finding problem to a convex optimization
[
180
]
.

∎

F.2
Fisher games as a limit case

Notation:

\vartheta_{0}=\log\left(\frac{p_{k^{*}}(B)}{p_{k^{*}}(A)}\right),\quad\tau_{0}=\log\left(\frac{\nu^{*}}{1-\nu^{*}}\right)

(735)

To obtain the
\gamma\to 0
limit of equilibrium parameters, it is useful to introduce the following limit function:

\Psi(\tau)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{(\vartheta_{0}+\gamma\tau)-\Phi(\vartheta_{0}+\gamma\tau)}{\gamma}

(736)

Some suitable notation and expressions to calculate this limit are:

\frac{p_{k}(A)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}}}{\left(z\ p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}+z^{-1}\ p_{k}(B)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{1-\gamma}}=z^{-(1-\gamma)}\left(1+z^{-2}\left(\frac{p_{k}(B)}{p_{k}(A)}\right)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}

(737)

\frac{p_{k}(B)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}}}{\left(z\ p_{k}(A)^{1/\gamma}+z^{-1}\ p_{k}(B)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{1-\gamma}}=z^{(1-\gamma)}\left(1+z^{2}\left(\frac{p_{k}(A)}{p_{k}(B)}\right)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}

(738)

\vartheta-\Phi(\vartheta)=\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\frac{\sum_{k}p_{k}(A)\left(1+z^{-2}\left(\frac{p_{k}(B)}{p_{k}(A)}\right)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}}{\sum_{k}p_{k}(B)\left(1+z^{2}\left(\frac{p_{k}(A)}{p_{k}(B)}\right)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}}\right)

(739)

Taking the limit:

Now, if we substitute
\vartheta=\vartheta_{0}+\gamma\tau
, then all parts of the sums converge to a well-defined limit as
\gamma\to 0
.

Introducing the notation:

\begin{split}c_{k}&=\left(1+z^{-2}\left(\frac{p_{k}(B)}{p_{k}(A)}\right)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}\\
d_{k}&=\left(1+z^{2}\left(\frac{p_{k}(A)}{p_{k}(B)}\right)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}\end{split}

(740)

c_{k}=\left(1+e^{-\tau}\left(\frac{p_{k^{*}}(A)}{p_{k^{*}}(B)}\frac{p_{k}(B)}{p_{k}(A)}\right)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}

(741)

d_{k}=\left(1+e^{\tau}\left(\frac{p_{k^{*}}(B)}{p_{k^{*}}(A)}\frac{p_{k}(A)}{p_{k}(B)}\right)^{1/\gamma}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}

(742)

c_{k}=\begin{cases}1+\mathcal{O}(e^{-|\lambda_{k}|/\gamma})&\text{if }k<k^{*}\\
\left(1+e^{-\tau}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}&\text{if }k=k^{*}\\
0+\mathcal{O}(e^{-|\lambda_{k}|/\gamma})&\text{if }k>k^{*}\\
\end{cases}

(743)

d_{k}=\begin{cases}0+\mathcal{O}(e^{-|\lambda_{k}|/\gamma})&\text{if }k<k^{*}\\
\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}&\text{if }k=k^{*}\\
1+\mathcal{O}(e^{-|\lambda_{k}|/\gamma})&\text{if }k>k^{*}\\
\end{cases}

(744)

where

\lambda_{k}=\log\left(\frac{p_{k^{*}}(A)}{p_{k^{*}}(B)}\frac{p_{k}(B)}{p_{k}(A)}\right)

(745)

Neglecting the
\mathcal{O}(e^{-|\lambda|/\gamma})
contributions for
\Psi(\tau)
, considerably simplifies the expression. Borrowing the notation from Appendix
A
, equations (
213
), (
214
) results:

\Psi(\tau)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\frac{\Sigma_{A}+p^{*}_{A}\left(1+e^{-\tau}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}}{\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}}\right)

(746)

\Sigma_{A}+p^{*}_{A}\nu^{*}=\Sigma_{B}+p^{*}_{B}(1-\nu^{*})=v^{*}

(747)

this further simplifies the expression:

\Psi(\tau)=\log\left(\frac{v^{*}+p^{*}_{A}\left(\left(1+e^{-\tau}\right)^{-1}-\nu^{*}\right)}{v^{*}+p^{*}_{B}\left(\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{-1}-(1-\nu^{*})\right)}\right)

(748)

or

\Psi(\tau)=\log\left(\frac{1+r_{A}\left(\left(1+e^{-\tau}\right)^{-1}-\nu^{*}\right)}{1+r_{B}\left(\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{-1}-(1-\nu^{*})\right)}\right)

(749)

where

r_{A}=p^{*}_{A}/v^{*},\quad r_{B}=p^{*}_{B}/v^{*}

(750)

Theorem F.1
.

For any
r_{A}>0
,
r_{B}>0
,
\nu^{*}\in(0,1)

\Psi(\tau_{0}+\Delta\tau)>0\text{ if }\Delta\tau>0,\quad\Psi(\tau_{0}+\Delta\tau)<0\text{ if }\Delta\tau<0

(751)

∎

Proof.

A slightly stronger set of inequalities hold:

\left(1+e^{-\Delta\tau}\frac{1-\nu^{*}}{\nu^{*}}\right)^{-1}-\nu^{*}>0,\quad\text{if }\Delta\tau>0

(752)

\left(1+e^{-\Delta\tau}\frac{1-\nu^{*}}{\nu^{*}}\right)^{-1}-\nu^{*}<0,\quad\text{if }\Delta\tau<0

(753)

\left(1+e^{\Delta\tau}\frac{\nu^{*}}{1-\nu^{*}}\right)^{-1}-(1-\nu^{*})<0,\quad\text{if }\Delta\tau>0

(754)

\left(1+e^{\Delta\tau}\frac{\nu^{*}}{1-\nu^{*}}\right)^{-1}-(1-\nu^{*})>0,\quad\text{if }\Delta\tau<0

(755)

All these inequalities can be directly checked. As an example, we can take the first one:

\left(1+e^{-\Delta\tau}\frac{1-\nu^{*}}{\nu^{*}}\right)^{-1}-\nu^{*}>0

(756)

1+e^{-\Delta\tau}\frac{1-\nu^{*}}{\nu^{*}}<\frac{1}{\nu^{*}}

(757)

e^{-\Delta\tau}\frac{1-\nu^{*}}{\nu^{*}}<\frac{1-\nu^{*}}{\nu^{*}}

(758)

e^{-\Delta\tau}<1,\quad\text{if }\Delta\tau>0

(759)

From these four inequalities, the theorem follows.

∎

Theorem F.2
.

\forall\varepsilon_{+},\varepsilon_{-}>0,\ \exists\underline{\gamma}>0
that:

\forall\gamma<\underline{\gamma},\quad\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)-\left(\log\left(\frac{p^{*}_{B}}{p^{*}_{A}}\right)+\gamma\log\left(\frac{\nu^{*}}{1-\nu^{*}}\right)\right)\in[-\gamma\epsilon_{-},\gamma\epsilon_{+}]

(760)

∎

Proof.

This follows from the uniqueness and existence of
\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)
for all
\gamma>0
and from the definition of
\Psi(\tau)
as a limit
\gamma\to 0
.

∎

Remark
.

To generate higher order approximations in
\gamma
for
\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)
one can iterate the expression:

\vartheta^{*}_{n+1}(\gamma)=\Phi(\vartheta^{*}_{n}(\gamma)),\quad\vartheta^{*}_{0}=\vartheta_{0}+\gamma\tau_{0}

(761)

For example, the first iteration gives:

\begin{split}\vartheta^{*}_{1}(\gamma)=&\log\left(\frac{p^{*}_{B}}{p^{*}_{A}}\right)+\gamma\log\left(\frac{\nu^{*}}{1-\nu^{*}}\right)+\\
&\gamma^{2}\left(r_{A}\nu^{*}\log(\nu^{*})-r_{B}(1-\nu^{*})\log(1-\nu^{*})\right)+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{3})\end{split}

(762)

However, as we will see, this does not give the exact second-order approximation of
\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)
.

∎

Remark
.

Remarkably, a similar derivation for the first derivative of
\Phi(\vartheta)
shows that it remains strictly smaller than 1 at
\vartheta_{0}
as
\gamma\to 0
.

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\Phi^{\prime}(\vartheta^{*}_{0})=1-(r_{A}+r_{B})\nu^{*}(1-\nu^{*})

(763)

which signals a much faster convergence than what is guaranteed by the global upper bound in (
733
).

∎

Remark
.

In fact, all derivatives of
\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)
can be determined as
\gamma\to 0
by using the “perturbative parts” in the definition of
\Psi
:

\Psi_{P}(\tau,\gamma)=\log\left(\frac{1+r_{A}\left(\left(1+e^{-\tau}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}-\nu^{*}\right)}{1+r_{B}\left(\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}-(1-\nu^{*})\right)}\right)

(764)

An implicit definition for
\tau^{\times}(\gamma)
:

\Psi_{P}(\tau^{\times}(\gamma),\gamma)=0

(765)

which is equivalent to finding the solution for
65
65

65

because for Fisher games
P^{*}_{0}=p^{*}_{B}/(p^{*}_{A}+p^{*}_{B})=r_{B}/(r_{A}+r_{B})
:

f(\tau,\gamma)=(1-P^{*}_{0})\left(\left(1+e^{-\tau}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}-\nu^{*}\right)-P^{*}_{0}\left(\left(1+e^{\tau}\right)^{-(1-\gamma)}-(1-\nu^{*})\right)

(766)

and

f(\tau^{\times}(\gamma),\gamma)=0

(767)

by which, one can define a perturbative approximation of
\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)
:

\vartheta^{\times}(\gamma)=\vartheta_{0}+\gamma\ \tau^{\times}(\gamma)

(768)

\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)
and
\vartheta^{\times}(\gamma)
are not the same, but their derivatives match upto every finite order
[
74
]

66
66

66

the
\mathcal{O}(e^{-|\lambda_{k}|/\gamma})
terms can remind someone to “Instanton contributions” in quantum field theories
[
73
]
:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)^{(n)}=\vartheta^{\times}(0)^{(n)}

(769)

For the difference, the
\mathcal{O}(e^{-|\lambda|/\gamma})
terms are responsible, which are not visible for the derivatives at 0.

∎

Remark
.

Using the previous formula, an exact second-order approximation can be obtained:

\begin{split}\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)=&\log\left(\frac{p^{*}_{B}}{p^{*}_{A}}\right)+\gamma\log\left(\frac{\nu^{*}}{1-\nu^{*}}\right)+\\
&\gamma^{2}\ \frac{P^{*}_{0}\nu^{*}\log(\nu^{*})-(1-P^{*}_{0})(1-\nu^{*})\log(1-\nu^{*})}{\nu^{*}(1-\nu^{*})}+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{3})\end{split}

(770)

∎

Remark
.

The quantity
\tau^{*}(\gamma)
is intimately related to
p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k^{*}}
, in the following way:

\frac{p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k^{*}}}{1-p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k^{*}}}=\left(\frac{P^{*}_{\gamma}}{1-P^{*}_{\gamma}}\right)^{1/\gamma}\left(\frac{p_{k^{*}}(A)}{p_{k^{*}}(B)}\right)^{1/\gamma}

(771)

\log\left(\frac{p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k^{*}}}{1-p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k^{*}}}\right)=\frac{1}{\gamma}\left(\vartheta^{*}(\gamma)-\vartheta_{0}\right)=\tau^{*}(\gamma)

(772)

p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k^{*}}=\frac{e^{\tau^{*}(\gamma)}}{1+e^{\tau^{*}(\gamma)}}

(773)

∎

Using the connection between
p^{\prime*}_{\gamma,k^{*}}
and
\tau^{*}(\gamma)
we can define a perturbative splitting ratio near
\gamma\approx 0
:

p^{\prime\times}_{\gamma,k^{*}}=\frac{e^{\tau^{\times}(\gamma)}}{1+e^{\tau^{\times}(\gamma)}}

(774)

Appendix G
Restricted iteration

This proof attempt belongs to Conjecture
3.1

Proof attempt.
We can take the derivative of
\invbreve{F}(\chi)
with respect to
\chi
:

\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{A}Z_{B}}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}\frac{(p_{k}(A)-p_{k}(B))^{2}}{P(\chi)p_{k}(A)+(1-P(\chi)p_{k}(B))}\left(\frac{d\chi}{dP}\right)^{-1}

(775)

where

\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{A}Z_{B}}}\left(\frac{d\chi}{dP}\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{Z_{A}/P(\chi)+Z_{B}/(1-P(\chi))}

(776)

We can introduce a new normalized set of variables:

\invbreve{p}_{k}(\theta)=\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}}p_{k}(\theta)

(777)

For any normalized “On-shell”
[
198
]
set of probabilities (
\sum_{k}\invbreve{p}_{\theta,k}=1
)

\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}\in[0,1]^{2\times|\mathbb{K}_{AB}|}

(778)

We will denote the set of all normalized probability measures on
\mathbb{K}_{AB}
by
\mathbb{P}
:

\mathbb{P}=\{p:\mathbb{K}_{AB}\mapsto[0,1]\ |\ ||p||_{1}=1\}

(779)

in this notation:

\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}_{2}

(780)

\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}})=\frac{1}{Z_{A}/P+Z_{B}/(1-P)}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}}\frac{(Z_{A}\ \invbreve{p}_{A,k}-Z_{B}\ \invbreve{p}_{B,k})^{2}}{P\ Z_{A}\invbreve{p}_{A,k}+(1-P)Z_{B}\invbreve{p}_{B,k}}

(781)

(Where
P
here means the function
P(\chi)
.)

It is easy to observe that this expression for the derivative is always positive:

\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}})>0

(782)

To show that this derivative is always less than
1
, we show that its value can take
1
only for an extreme normalized probability distribution (
\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}^{E}\in\mathbb{P}_{2}^{E}
) and that the value for a non-extreme distribution is always less than this maximal value.

\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}^{E}\in\mathbb{P}_{2}^{E}\iff\forall k\in\mathbb{K}_{AB},\ \invbreve{p}^{E}_{A,k}\cdot\invbreve{p}^{E}_{B,k}=0

(783)

For such extreme probability distribution pairs it can be seen that:

\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}^{E})=\frac{1}{Z_{A}/P+Z_{B}/(1-P)}\left(\frac{Z_{A}}{P}+\frac{Z_{B}}{1-P}\right)=1

(784)

To show that making normalized distributions more extreme increases the value of the derivative, we introduce the continuous elementary permutations or continuous swaps. For any
\lambda\in[0,1]
,
k,\ell\in\mathbb{K}_{AB}
,
\theta\in\Theta=\{A,B\}
we can define
\mathscr{P}^{\lambda}_{\theta,(k,\ell)}:\mathbb{P}_{2}\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{2}
in the following way:

\underline{\underline{\invbreve{q}}}=\mathscr{P}^{\lambda}_{\theta,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}})\iff\invbreve{q}_{\theta,k}=\lambda(\invbreve{p}_{\theta,k}+\invbreve{p}_{\theta,\ell}),\quad\invbreve{q}_{\theta,\ell}=(1-\lambda)(\invbreve{p}_{\theta,k}+\invbreve{p}_{\theta,\ell})

(785)

It can be shown that all
\underline{\underline{p}},\underline{\underline{q}}\in\mathbb{P}_{2}
pairs can be connected by some finite chain of continuous elementary permutations:
\underline{\underline{q}}=\mathscr{P}_{n}\circ,\dots,\circ\mathscr{P}_{1}(\underline{\underline{p}})
.
67
67

67

This is similar to the “Transposition Theorem”, which states that “any permutation of a finite set containing at least two
elements can be written as the product of transpositions”
[
90
]
.

To see, how the continuous permutation can change
\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}
, we can take its second derivative with respect to
\lambda
:

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\lambda^{2}}\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{\lambda}_{\theta,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}))

(786)

The second derivative can be calculated explicitly:

\frac{2\left(p_{A,k}+p_{A,\ell}\right)^{2}p_{B,k}^{2}}{\left(P\ \lambda\left(p_{A,k}+p_{A,\ell}\right)+(1-P)p_{B,k}\right)^{3}}+\frac{2\left(p_{A,k}+p_{A,\ell}\right)^{2}p_{B,\ell}^{2}}{\left(P(1-\lambda)\left(p_{A,k}+p_{A,\ell}\right)+(1-P)p_{B,\ell}\right)^{3}}

(787)

which shows that it is always greater or equal to
0
:

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\lambda^{2}}\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{\lambda}_{\theta,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}))\geq 0

(788)

This means that as a function of
\lambda
, the expression will take its maximum on the boundary, i.e.
\lambda\in\{0,1\}
.

Explicit calculation gives the difference between values at
\lambda=1
and
\lambda=0
:

\Delta\invbreve{F}^{\prime}=\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{1}_{\theta,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}))-\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{0}_{\theta,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}))

(789)

\Delta\invbreve{F}^{\prime}=\frac{\left(p_{A,k}+p_{A,\ell}\right)^{2}\left(p_{B,\ell}-p_{B,k}\right)}{(1-P)\left(P\left(p_{A,k}+p_{A,\ell}\right)+(1-P)p_{B,k}\right)\left(P\left(p_{A,k}+p_{A,\ell}\right)+(1-P)p_{B,\ell}\right)}

(790)

From which the following conditional equations follow:

\begin{split}\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{1}_{A,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}))=&\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{\lambda}_{A,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}})),\quad\text{if }\invbreve{p}_{B,k}\leq\invbreve{p}_{B,\ell}\\
\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{0}_{A,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}))=&\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{\lambda}_{A,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}})),\quad\text{if }\invbreve{p}_{B,k}\geq\invbreve{p}_{B,\ell}\\
\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{1}_{B,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}))=&\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{\lambda}_{B,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}})),\quad\text{if }\invbreve{p}_{A,k}\leq\invbreve{p}_{A,\ell}\\
\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{0}_{B,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}))=&\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\mathscr{P}^{\lambda}_{B,(k,\ell)}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}})),\quad\text{if }\invbreve{p}_{A,k}\geq\invbreve{p}_{A,\ell}\end{split}

(791)

We can now introduce the following extremization operators:

\mathscr{E}_{\theta,k\to\ell}=\mathscr{P}_{\theta,(k,\ell)}^{0},\quad\mathscr{E}_{\theta,\ell\to k}=\mathscr{P}_{\theta,(k,\ell)}^{1}

(792)

By successively applying appropriate extremization operators, we can get an extreme state from an initial state:

\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}^{E}=\mathscr{E}_{n}\circ\dots\circ\mathscr{E}_{1}(\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}),\quad\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}^{E}\in\mathbb{P}_{2}^{E},\ \underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}\in\mathbb{P}_{2}

(793)

where every step increases the value of
\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,.)
.
This implies that if
\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}
is not extreme:

\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}})<\invbreve{F}^{\circ}{}^{\prime}(\chi,\underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}^{E})=1,\quad\text{if}\ \underline{\underline{\invbreve{p}}}\notin\mathbb{P}_{2}^{E}

(794)

From this follows that:

\forall\chi\in\mathbb{R},\ \invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)<1

(795)

To show that the inequality uniformly holds for any range of
\chi
, it is beneficial to observe that:

\lim_{\chi\to-\infty}\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\chi\to\infty}\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)=0

(796)

This means that:

\forall\varepsilon>0,\exists\chi_{-},\chi{+},\quad\forall\chi<\chi_{-}\vee\chi>\chi_{+},\quad|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|<\varepsilon

(797)

We are interested in the smallest global upper bound of
|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|
:

\invbreve{q}=\sup_{\chi\in\mathbb{R}}|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|

(798)

The domain of
\chi
can be split to
3
subsets:

\invbreve{q}=\max\left\{\sup_{\chi<\chi_{-}}|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|,\sup_{\chi\in[\chi_{-},\chi_{+}]}|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|,\sup_{\chi>\chi_{+}}|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|\right\}

(799)

\invbreve{q}\leq\max\left\{\varepsilon,\sup_{\chi\in[\chi_{-},\chi_{+}]}|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|,\varepsilon\right\}

(800)

A continuous function takes its supremum and infinum on a compact interval, therefore:

\invbreve{F}^{\prime}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}),\ \forall\chi,|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|<1\implies\sup_{\chi\in[\chi_{-},\chi_{+}]}|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|<1

(801)

which for
\varepsilon<1
choice guarantees that:

\invbreve{q}=\sup_{\chi\in\mathbb{R}}|\invbreve{F}^{\prime}(\chi)|<1

(802)

This attempts to complete the proof.

\heartsuit

Appendix H
Questions leading to the present work

Personal but genuine motivation:

The way I followed and the puzzles which interested me might not be everybody’s path, but sketching my own story might resonate with some because it has grown from genuine enthusiasm.

Random mass events:

For a relatively long time -- more or less until the end of my undergraduate studies -- my default framework for probabilistic reasoning would be a mixture of Classical probability theory (providing the principle of indifference
68
68

68

or principle of insufficient reason
) and the Frequentist interpretation (by which I was able to ground derived probabilistic statements to the results of many times repeated experiments).
I think this is no wonder, given that all formal educational material backing my theoretical physicist degree were in accordance (or at least mostly compatible) with this blend of frameworks.
Error analysis in experimental physics
[
185
,
10
]
, the convenient thermodynamic limit in statistical physics, the counters of ionizing radiation from radioactive decay gave a convincing grounding for the objective reality of the long-term relative frequencies. Axiomatic probability theory
[
101
]
provided a rich and robust mathematical language, in which initial assumptions could be transformed to probabilistic statements about complicated events.

However, personally, I always felt a little uneasy when I wanted to sincerely interpret the result of error analysis for a concrete laboratory experiment or – to give a different example – the law of large numbers, which was sometimes introduced as the link between probability theory and statistics.
With tools from “frequentist statistics,” I was equipped with techniques that were asymptotically correct and consistent. However, we only ever had finite samples (and for some more delicate experiments, only a few).
Furthermore, theoretically, the connection between probability theory and real-world events was not entirely convincing because the arguments always felt suspiciously circular
[
75
]
.
69
69

69

Probably the “§2. The Relation to Experimental Data” in Kolmogorov’s axiomatization
[
101
]
made a symbolic attempt to make the connection possible by postulating: “4)
Under certain conditions, which we shall not discuss here, we may assume that to an event
A
which may or may not occur under conditions
\mathfrak{S}
, is assigned a real number
P(A)
which has
the following characteristics:
(a) One can be practically certain that if the complex of conditions
\mathfrak{S}
is repeated a large number of times,
n
, then if to be the number of occurrences of event
A
, the ratio
m/n
will differ very
slightly from
P(A)
.”, Kolmogorov points out in a straightforward footnote that his axiomatization aims to establish a purely mathematical framework, and writes: “In establishing the premises
necessary for the applicability of the theory of probability to the world of
actual events, the author has used, in large measure, the work of R. v. Mises
[
121
]
”

Against all my philosophical doubts, I adopted a pragmatic attitude, neatly summarized by Mark Kac in the following quote:
“If a probability of a certain event was calculated in accordance
with certain assumptions and rules, then the probability (again
calculated according to the same assumptions and rules) that the
frequency with which the event will occur in a large assembly of
trials will differ significantly from the calculated probability is
small.
Modest as the statement is, it is essentially all one can expect
from a purely mathematical theory.
The applicability of such a theory to natural sciences must
ultimately be tested by an experiment. But this is true of all
mathematical theories when applied outside the realm of mathematics, and the vague feeling of discomfort one encounters (mostly
among philosophers!) when first subjected to statistical reasoning
must be attributed to the relative novelty of the ideas.
To me there is no methodological distinction between the
applicability of differential equations to astronomy and of probability theory to thermodynamics or quantum mechanics.
It works! And brutally pragmatic as this point of view is, no
better substitute has been found.”
[
92
]

A Bayesian turn:

My hesitation to accept the previously described views as the only valid framework started to grow when I began to successfully use statistical techniques – routinely applied in experimental physics – to deterministic (but truncated) numerical results related to purely theoretical models
[
11
]
.
In these cases, seemingly none of the usual requirements were present, which might justify a probabilistic or statistical treatment, but these concepts and interpretations somehow worked remarkably well.

This was the point when I started to entertain the thought that maybe it is more beneficial if we liberate the phenomena from the requirement of being objectively stochastic and we reserve the probabilistic language to describe only our partial and subjective knowledge about physical (or even theoretical) “objects” or state of affairs.

My entry point to Bayesian statistics was the famous textbook of E. T. Jaynes (published posthumously in 2003)
[
41
]
. The possibility of a new, broader and more coherent framework captivated my imagination, and I soon started to explore the applicability of its methods to various problems.

However, one issue remained: the unbearable subjectivity of the prior probabilities.

Quest for Objective prior(s):

I think in most situations, the appropriate place where probabilities could be identified is not the outside world but our internal partial description of the world.
However, in my view, this does not automatically mean that one has to give up all principles or criteria to filter out priors, which can potentially ruin any inference based on a finite amount of data.
70
70

70

A simple example for such principle might be Cromwell’s rule
[
110
,
83
]
, which simply suggests not to use a prior, which associates
0
or
1
to specific events (the statement can be made without further complication if the set of possibilities is discrete).

There is wisdom in the bold view of Bruno de Finetti
[
48
]
, emphasizing that an agent’s model about the world is already a subjective mental construction; therefore, constructing a prior might not be a seriously demanding step.
However, putting the subjective Bayesian view aside, I started searching for “Objective” (also known as uninformative) priors, which might serve at least as robust default choices.

Once one starts to search for objective priors, many different suggestions appear, such as the classical principle of indifference, maximum entropy priors, transformation group invariant priors
[
41
]
, Jeffreys prior (which is invariant under diffeomorphisms on the parameter space)
[
41
,
130
,
85
]
, Reference prior
[
13
,
88
]
, etc.

A seemingly plentiful menu of “objective” prior choices gave the motivation to search for arguments and principles that might prefer one to another or construct more “natural” ones.

Little less thinking, little more action:

During the search for convincing principles for priors in Bayesian inference, it appeared that to rate different prior choices (with finite sample sizes), one has to link the result of inference to actions and then compare utilities (or loss functions or rewards) associated with possible consequences.
However, the Bayesian framework is traditionally introduced mainly as an epistemological discipline concerned about knowledge, belief and “rationality”, but makes no meaningful statements about different prior choices. I felt it has to be embedded into a more general decision-making framework, where the main concepts are action, consequences, utilities and strategy.

For instance, consider a scenario where an agent receives a reward in a parametric model. This reward is based on the relative entropy – also known as Kullback-Leibler divergence – between her inferred distribution, which is based on her data, and the real distribution. Furthermore, assume that the parameters of this model have a known
\pi_{0}
prior distribution.
In such a case, the agent can maximize her expected reward (or minimize her expected loss). It can be proven that to achieve this, she must strictly follow the Bayesian updating rule to determine her inferred distribution.

This observation gave motivation to search for decision-making problems, where Bayesian update is not “only” a result of a consistency argument but an optimal solution to an optimization or equilibrium finding problem.

Game theory as foundation:

A natural framework in which randomization and mixed strategies appear is Game Theory
[
97
,
117
,
176
,
32
,
128
]
.
Knowing that they will play a rock-paper-scissors, players can deduce in advance that their best strategy is to choose from all possible moves as unpredictably as possible.
In this case, the concepts of randomization and mixed strategy are results of a careful consideration, solving a paradoxical dilemma where no deterministic decision function could work well.

Traditionally, randomization in mixed strategies was defined and understood with the help of probability theory, but in a way, this might be reversed.
One can ask: if an agent can plan and execute randomized mixed strategies in simple games, then could we use such games to ground concepts in probability theory?
Is it possible, or even desirable, to use other analogies to statistical problems, which do not model real-world events based on degrees of belief or fair coins and dice? Instead, could we map such problems to games and interpret the statistical decision functions as equilibrium strategies?
What could or should we assume about our “opponent”? Are there good default assumptions?

In particular, what is a game in which Bayesian update is the optimal strategy?
Can this framework provide an “objective” prior as an ingredient of our optimal strategy in some kind of inference game?

These questions motivated me to explore the topic, resulting in the present work.
This quest brought me to an exciting journey, leading to various scientific disciplines and providing a range of mathematical puzzles and the possibility of a rich abstract structure.
To illustrate the general concept, I tried to find the simplest statistical inference problem, which can serve as a toy model, and where exact answers can be given.
I hope that I will be able to partly share with the reader the fruits of these mathematical explorations and open (or reopen) the door to structures that emerge from a game theoretic interpretation of probability.
